Result [793]

[1] David Henderson invested in and developed properties through Property Ventures Ltd (PVL) and various subsidiary companies. Between September 2009 and November 2012, PVL and many of its subsidiaries went into liquidation. The companies’ assets were insufficient to pay their creditors. The deficiency was in the hundreds of millions of dollars.

[2] PVL’s liquidator, Robert Walker, considered there were good claims against third parties which might generate recoveries. In 2012 and 2013, he commenced two proceedings on behalf of PVL and some of its subsidiaries against PVL’s former directors (including Mr Henderson), its auditors and its valuers (the PVL proceedings). To fund the PVL proceedings, Mr Walker entered into a litigation funding agreement with SPF No.10 Ltd (SPF), a wholly owned subsidiary of LPF Group Ltd (LPF). Soon afterwards, John Scutter was appointed joint liquidator of PVL.

[3] Between 2015 and 2017, PVL and its subsidiaries reached settlements with the defendants in the PVL proceedings, including Mr Henderson. The defendants paid sums totalling $38,980,286. Of those settlement proceeds, about $33 million was paid to SPF with the balance to Mr Walker and Mr Scutter on account of their fees and expenses. Nothing was paid to any other creditor of PVL or the PVL subsidiaries.

[4] The current proceedings challenge the payment of the settlement proceeds to SPF and Mr Walker. One proceeding is brought by four companies associated with Mr Henderson. Those companies claim to have acquired first ranking securities over some of the PVL subsidiaries’ assets and thereby to have been entitled to some of the settlement proceeds obtained in the PVL proceedings. The other proceeding is brought by Kevin Whitley. Mr Whitley replaced Mr Walker as liquidator of PVL and its subsidiaries in 2021 and 2022 (Mr Scutter having resigned as a liquidator of PVL in 2018).

[5] The plaintiffs in the two proceedings allege that SPF, LPF and Mr Walker are liable to repay some of the settlement proceeds. A similar claim against Mr Scutter was settled shortly before trial. The plaintiffs’ claims include conversion, money had and received, breach of fiduciary duty, knowing receipt, breach of the litigation

funding agreement, undue influence and breach of statutory duty. The defendants dispute liability. They raise several affirmative defences and bring counterclaims.

Factual outline

PVL and its collapse

[6] PVL was established in 1997. Mr Henderson became a director in 2003.

[7] PVL’s business model was that each property investment or development was conducted through a standalone and subsidiary company. Each subsidiary had its own funding arrangements, usually with a second-tier lender. As security for the lending, each subsidiary granted its lender a mortgage over the property and often a general security agreement (GSA). Each subsidiary’s debt was almost always guaranteed by PVL and Mr Henderson. There were also often cross-guarantees by subsidiaries in respect of each other’s debts.

[8] PVL did not borrow directly from lenders. The guarantees of its subsidiaries’ debts that PVL provided were generally unsecured. The exceptions were guarantees that PVL provided to Hanover Finance Ltd (Hanover) and Dominion Finance Group Ltd (DFG). These were secured by GSAs over PVL’s assets. Hanover had the first ranking GSA, DFG the second. Subsequently, Hanover assigned its GSA to Allied Farmers Investments Ltd (Allied).

[9] The PVL group encountered financial difficulties from about 2008. PVL and its subsidiaries were placed into receivership and then liquidation at various dates from September 2009 to November 2012. The securities over the subsidiaries’ properties were enforced in a depressed market. There were very significant shortfalls. Meanwhile, in November 2010, Mr Henderson was adjudicated bankrupt.

The liquidators of PVL and its subsidiaries

[10] PVL was placed into liquidation on 27 July 2010. Mr Walker was appointed liquidator. However, PVL advised the court that it intended to appeal the liquidation decision. The court granted a stay of execution of the liquidation order. The stay was not lifted until PVL abandoned its appeal in February 2012.

[11] Mr Scutter was appointed joint liquidator of PVL with Mr Walker on 4 June 2013. As will be explained, this was largely at the behest of LPF and SPF. Mr Scutter resigned as liquidator of PVL in March 2018.

[12] Mr Walker was also appointed liquidator of various PVL subsidiaries, including all the subsidiaries involved in these proceedings. Mr Scutter was not appointed joint liquidator of the subsidiaries.

Voidable preference recoveries

[13] As liquidator, Mr Walker challenged various payments made to creditors of PVL or its subsidiaries in the lead up to their liquidations. He said they were preferences that were voidable under s 292 of the Companies Act 1993. In 2012 and 2013, Mr Walker recovered voidable preferences totalling about $500,000–700,000 (the precise amount being in dispute).

[14] These voidable preference recoveries were a minor focus of the current proceedings, as I will explain later.

Negotiations towards funding the PVL proceedings

[15] Following his appointment as liquidator, Mr Walker investigated the potential for claims that might increase the pool of assets in the liquidations. His investigations were delayed to some extent by the stay on execution of the liquidation order in respect of PVL, which as I have noted remained in place until February 2012.

[16] Mr Walker identified three groups of potential defendants: the directors of the PVL companies (including Mr Henderson); the auditor of PVL, PricewaterhouseCoopers (PwC); and the valuer of properties within the PVL group, Fright Aubrey Ltd (Fright Aubrey). It was initially contemplated that the plaintiffs in any proceeding against those defendants would be PVL and one of its subsidiaries, Five Mile Holdings Ltd (Five Mile).

[17] There were insufficient assets within the PVL companies to fund the claims against the directors, PwC and Fright Aubrey. Mr Walker tried to interest the main secured creditors, Allied and DFG, to fund the claims. He was unsuccessful.

[18] Mr Walker then turned to potential third-party funders of the litigation. An obstacle to any litigation funding was that Allied held first ranking GSAs over all of PVL’s and Five Mile’s assets (the Allied security). Any proceeds from the litigation would likely be subject to that security. That meant a funder would only be interested in funding the litigation if satisfactory arrangements were first reached with Allied, such as a purchase of the Allied security.

[19] Kevin Sullivan, a barrister advising Mr Walker, identified a Wellington family as a potential litigation funder. In April and May 2012, Mr Sullivan had discussions with Allied about the possible purchase of the Allied security. Allied told Mr Sullivan that Mr Henderson was, notwithstanding his bankruptcy, attempting to purchase (through entities associated with him) the Allied security. Such a purchase would have frustrated the litigation that Mr Walker was contemplating. To try to dissuade Allied from assigning its security to an entity associated with Mr Henderson, on 10 May 2012 the Wellington family wrote to Allied confirming their interest in entering into a conditional agreement to purchase the security. On 25 May 2012, however, the Wellington family’s solicitor advised Mr Sullivan that “[the family] have looked into Henderson and don’t like what they see. Scared off by what he might do I think.”

[20] By then, Mr Sullivan had also identified LPF as a potential source of litigation funding. LPF was founded in 2009. It specialises in managing litigation funding. LPF does not provide litigation funding itself. It identifies suitable cases to fund, incorporates subsidiaries to fund each proceeding and sources funding for them through limited partnerships. In 2012, LPF was one of the few firms operating in the New Zealand litigation funding market, which was still in its relative infancy (compared, for example, to the market in Australia).

[21] The Wellington family having gotten cold feet, Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan pursued discussions with LPF. On 21 August 2012, Jonathan Woodhams, the executive director of LPF, sent a letter to Mr Sullivan and Mr Walker offering funding

from LPF’s subsidiary SPF for a proceeding by PVL. SPF was not incorporated until later, but nothing turns on this. The offer was subject to satisfactory completion of due diligence by SPF, signing formal funding documentation and entry into satisfactory arrangements with Allied.

[22] SPF’s offer proposed, in summary, that it would fund the costs of the litigation (referred to as “Project Costs” in the offer). In exchange, from any proceeds SPF would be repaid the Project Costs and, in addition, be paid a return (calculated as a percentage of the proceeds, subject to a minimum return of twice the Project Costs).

[23] It was contemplated by Mr Walker, Mr Sullivan and Mr Woodhams that any arrangements between SPF and Allied were likely to involve SPF purchasing the Allied security. This created a tension. The proceeds of any litigation by PVL were likely to be subject to the Allied security. If SPF held the Allied security, it would be able to retain all the litigation proceeds up to the amount of the secured debt, not just be repaid the Project Costs and paid its funding return. The debt was understood to be about $60 million, more than any likely litigation proceeds.

[24] Mr Sullivan referred to this tension when responding to SPF’s offer on 29 August 2012. In relation to the contemplated purchase by SPF of the Allied security, Mr Sullivan said that Mr Walker understood that SPF would agree to cede its rights under the security over the litigation proceeds after repayment of the Project Costs and payment of the funding return.

[25] Mr Woodhams presented a revised offer to Mr Sullivan in a letter emailed on 17 September 2012. Mr Woodhams’ covering email said LPF was in agreement that to the extent it had received payment of the Project Costs and its funding return, “the balance of any funds received (or to be received) under the Allied Security shall be held to [Mr Walker’s] order”. In the revised offer, the paragraph which said that the offer was conditional on entry into satisfactory arrangements with respect to the Allied security had had a sentence added to it. The new sentence said it was acknowledged that the arrangements “will include arrangements between the liquidator and SPF in respect of any assignment of the benefit of the security over the assets of [PVL]”.

[26] Mr Walker accepted the revised offer on 18 September 2012.

[27] The revised offer remained subject to the signing of formal funding documentation. Mr Woodhams emailed a draft of the formal funding documentation, in the form of a draft “Deed for Provision of Services in Respect of Litigation” between SPF and PVL (the Services Deed), to Mr Sullivan and Mr Walker on 27 September 2012. Mr Walker responded the same day, saying he had read the draft Services Deed himself.

[28] The draft Services Deed required PVL, on signing the Deed, to deliver to SPF a certification from Mr Walker that he had received independent legal advice on the Deed. Mr Walker instructed MinterEllisonRuddWatts (MinterEllison) to provide him with that advice.

[29] On 17 October 2012, while Mr Walker was awaiting advice from MinterEllison, he was told that Allied was again in negotiations to sell the Allied security to interests associated with Mr Henderson. This put some pressure on Mr Walker to move quickly to finalise the Services Deed. Adding to that pressure was the understanding of Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan that the limitation period for the claim against PwC (which was the strongest of the claims in the proposed litigation) would expire on 13 November 2012.

[30] MinterEllison provided its advice to Mr Walker on 29 October 2012. It said that the Services Deed was “drafted heavily in favour of SPF”.

[31] Following that advice, some minor amendments were made to the draft Services Deed. Mr Walker signed a final version of the Deed, on behalf of PVL, on 31 October 2012. Clause 3.1 said SPF’s obligation to provide funding was conditional on:

(a) the results of a due diligence investigation of the claims being acceptable to SPF; and

(b) SPF entering into satisfactory arrangements with Allied under which SPF would obtain a first ranking security interest over all of PVL’s assets.

However, the Services Deed did not say anything about SPF holding any surplus received under the Allied security to Mr Walker’s order.

[32] Clause 3.8 of the Services Deed obliged PVL to ensure that any money received from successful voidable preference claims be applied to pay the costs of PVL’s liquidation. This reflected a term that was in the funding offer.

[33] SPF did not sign the Services Deed until many months later, in March 2013. In the meantime, the first of the two PVL proceedings was filed.

The first PVL proceeding is filed

[34] In late September 2012, as part of its due diligence, LPF engaged a Wellington barrister, Justin Smith, to provide his opinion on the merits of the proposed claims. He provided his opinion to LPF on 19 October 2012.

[35] Mr Smith was senior counsel for the PVL plaintiffs throughout the PVL proceedings. He was appointed Queen’s Counsel in 2013. I will refer to him as Mr Justin Smith (or Mr Smith KC) to distinguish him from Mr Tim Smith, who was for a time PVL’s solicitor in the PVL proceedings.

[36] The first PVL proceeding was filed in the High Court on 12 November 2012, one day before Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan understood the limitation period in respect of PwC would expire. There were three plaintiffs: Mr Walker (as liquidator of PVL and four of PVL’s subsidiaries); PVL; and Five Mile. No other PVL subsidiary was a plaintiff. The defendants were PVL’s former directors, PwC and Fright Aubrey. One of Fright Aubrey’s directors was also a defendant, but it is unnecessary for the purposes of this judgment to distinguish him from Fright Aubrey.

[37] The claims in the first PVL proceeding were as follows:

(a) Mr Walker, as liquidator, sued the directors for their alleged failure to keep proper accounting records.

(b) All plaintiffs sued the directors for their alleged breaches of directors’ duties.

(c) PVL and Five Mile sued PwC in contract and tort for its alleged negligence in carrying out certain audits.

(d) PVL and Five Mile sued Fright Aubrey for its allegedly negligent valuations.

[38] The first PVL proceeding was filed while Mr Walker was still waiting for SPF to sign the Services Deed. SPF would not sign the Deed until it completed negotiations with Allied for the purchase of the Allied security. Filing the proceeding without a signed Deed posed some risk for Mr Walker, but he felt he had to file it given the limitation issues in respect of PwC.

Finalising the funding of the PVL proceedings

[39] On 18 February 2013, at which time SPF had still not signed the Services Deed, Mr Sullivan telephoned Mr Woodhams. They discussed the proposal that if SPF acquired the Allied security, any surplus received under it (beyond SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed) would be held by SPF for Mr Walker and PVL’s unsecured creditors. The next day, 19 February 2013, Mr Sullivan emailed a draft letter to Mr Woodhams. The draft letter proposed that SPF would, to the extent of any surplus, cede or surrender its security for the benefit of Mr Walker and the unsecured creditors.

[40] On 22 February 2013, Mr Woodhams sought approval from LPF’s board for the funding of the PVL litigation. He did so on the basis that if SPF acquired the Allied security, it would hold any surplus received under it for the benefit of PVL’s creditors. The board gave that approval.

[41] In early March 2013, SPF and Allied reached an agreement. This was recorded in a deed under which Allied assigned its GSAs (and other securities) to SPF. In

exchange, SPF paid $100,000 plus GST to Allied and promised to pay five per cent of any net proceeds received by SPF from the litigation.

[42] Shortly after reaching that agreement with Allied, SPF signed the Services Deed. The Deed was in the same terms as the Deed that Mr Walker had signed on 31 October 2012. It was not amended to incorporate the proposal made by Mr Sullivan in his 19 February 2013 draft letter that SPF would, to the extent of any surplus, cede or surrender its security for the benefit of Mr Walker and the unsecured creditors. At no point did SPF respond directly to that draft letter.

[43] One of the issues in the current proceedings is whether there was in the above circumstances a concluded and binding agreement between PVL and SPF that SPF would hold any surplus of funds received under the Allied security for the benefit of Mr Walker and PVL’s unsecured creditors.

The terms of the Services Deed

[44] Under the Services Deed, SPF agreed to confer two main benefits on PVL in respect of the proceeding brought by PVL against its former directors, PwC and Fright Aubrey. First, SPF agreed to provide PVL with investigative, management and other expertise. Secondly, SPF agreed to pay the Project Costs, with any such payment constituting a loan by SPF to PVL.

[45] The Services Deed defined Project Costs broadly. Generally, they were the costs incurred or assumed by PVL or SPF in relation to the proceeding, including court costs, lawyers’ fees, expert witness fees and the provision of security for costs.

[46] If there was a resolution of the proceeding in PVL’s favour, SPF was entitled to be repaid the Project Costs and to be paid the “Services Fee”. The “Services Fee” was defined as the greater of:

(a) a percentage of the “Net Resolution Sum”, which was the amount received by PVL in respect of the proceeding (whether by judgment, settlement or otherwise) less the Project Costs. The percentage varied

from 25 per cent to 42.5 per cent, depending on how quickly the resolution occurred and the amount of the Project Costs incurred; or

(b) two times Project Costs.

[47] If there were no resolution in PVL’s favour, PVL had no liability to repay any amount of the Project Costs. In short, under the Services Deed SPF was assisting PVL and advancing Project Costs on a “no win, no fee” basis.

The second PVL proceeding is filed

[48] In April 2013, Mr Tim Smith advised Mr Walker and Mr Woodhams of a potential problem with the claim made by PVL and Five Mile against PwC in the first PVL proceeding. That claim alleged breaches of a duty of care by PwC in auditing PVL’s 2006 and 2007 financial statements. PwC’s engagement letter for the 2006 audit contained a clause imposing a four-year limitation period on bringing any claim. It thus appeared that the claim against PwC in respect of the 2006 audit was brought out of time.

[49] Mr Tim Smith proposed a workaround to this problem — having other PVL subsidiaries bring claims in tort against PwC. None of those subsidiaries were party to the PwC engagement letter, and so it was thought that the contractual limitation period in that letter would not bar their claims in tort.

[50] In light of that advice, a second PVL proceeding was drafted under urgency (there being a concern that even a six-year limitation period was in peril) and filed on 23 April 2013. There were ten plaintiffs, all subsidiaries of PVL. The defendants were PwC and Fright Aubrey.

[51] Mr Walker and SPF treated the claims by the PVL subsidiaries in the second PVL proceeding as being covered by the Services Deed. SPF paid the costs of that proceeding as if they were Project Costs. However, none of the PVL subsidiaries ever executed the Deed. An issue in the current proceedings is whether the PVL subsidiaries were bound by the terms of the Deed.

[52] The two PVL proceedings were consolidated in May 2014.

Appointment of Mr Scutter as joint liquidator of PVL

[53] SPF was concerned about the risk involved in PVL having only one liquidator. It proposed that a second liquidator be appointed. On 4 June 2013, Mr Scutter was appointed joint and several liquidator of PVL. Mr Walker remained the sole liquidator of the PVL subsidiaries.

Settlement with Fright Aubrey

[54] The claims against Fright Aubrey were settled in March 2015. PVL and its subsidiaries received settlement payments in three tranches from July to December 2015, totalling $2,180,286. By that time, SPF had advanced Project Costs that exceeded the amount of the settlement. In accordance with the terms of the Services Deed, the settlement payments were paid to SPF in partial repayment of Project Costs that SPF had advanced.

The DFG security

[55] DFG held the next ranking security, after the Allied security, over PVL’s and Five Mile’s assets (the DFG security).

[56] On 20 April 2016, owing to an oversight by SPF, the financing statement registered on the personal property securities register (the PPSR) for the Allied security expired without being renewed. SPF registered a new financing statement the next day.

[57] On 26 September 2016, the receivers of DFG wrote to LPF asserting that on expiry of the financing statement for the Allied security, DFG had assumed first ranking security over PVL’s and Five Mile’s assets. The letter was written by Michael Whale, a consultant at Lowndes, the solicitors for DFG’s receivers.

[58] There was never any determination as to whether the receivers’ assertion was correct. SPF wished to put the matter beyond doubt by purchasing the DFG security. In December 2016, it entered into a deed with DFG under which DFG transferred to

SPF its security over the assets of PVL and Five Mile. In exchange, SPF agreed to pay a purchase price, the calculation of which depended in part on the outcome of the PVL proceedings. The purchase price was a minimum of $500,000 but could be up to

$1,650,000.

PwC’s application for a stay of the PVL proceedings

[59] In the meantime, in March 2015, PwC applied to the High Court for an order staying the PVL proceedings. PwC claimed the proceedings were funded and maintained by SPF pursuant to an arrangement that was champertous and consequently an abuse of process. PwC did not challenge the Services Deed in isolation. Its focus was on the combination of the Deed and the assignment of the Allied security to SPF.

[60] In response to PwC’s application, PVL filed affidavits by Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker addressing SPF’s purchase of the Allied security. Mr Woodhams deposed that SPF purchased the Allied security for defensive reasons to prevent Mr Henderson’s interests acquiring the security. He said “[w]hilst not a legal commitment”, SPF had indicated to Mr Walker that it would work with him to allocate a portion of any proceeds recovered under the Allied security to unsecured creditors. Mr Walker deposed that he shared that understanding.

[61] The High Court declined PwC’s application in July 2015.1 PwC appealed. The Court of Appeal dismissed the appeal in July 2016.2 In December 2016, the Supreme Court granted PwC leave to appeal.3 In so doing, it asked the parties “to address the propriety of the position of the liquidator, given the unsecured creditors are unlikely to be recipients of any proceeds of the proceedings, should they be successful”.4 The Supreme Court granted leave to SPF to intervene in the appeal.

[62] The Supreme Court hearing was scheduled for 16 March 2017. In the lead up to that hearing, there were further discussions between, on the one hand, Mr Walker

1 Walker v Forbes [2015] NZHC 1730, [2015] 3 NZLR 831.

2 PricewaterhouseCoopers v Walker [2016] NZCA 338.

3 PricewaterhouseCoopers v Walker [2016] NZSC 165.

4 At [2].

and Mr Scutter, and, on the other, SPF, about the extent to which SPF would hold any of the proceeds recoverable under the Allied security for the benefit of unsecured creditors and about how to present that position to the Supreme Court. These discussions became tense. At times, Mr Walker and Mr Scutter expressed the view that SPF was under a binding commitment to hold any surplus (beyond its entitlements under the Services Deed) received under the Allied security for the benefit of the unsecured creditors. SPF disagreed.

[63] A resolution (of sorts) was reached in mid-February 2017:

(a) SPF filed an affidavit by Bill Wilson KC, the chair of LPC. Mr Wilson said it was always the intention of SPF to make funds available to the liquidators for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of PVL. He said that SPF undertook that if the proceedings were successful, it would make available to the liquidators, after payment to SPF of its entitlements under the Services Deed and payment of the amounts due to Allied and DFG, a minimum of 10 per cent of the first $75 million received and 50 per cent of any recoveries above $75 million.

(b) PVL filed an affidavit by Mr Walker. He referred to the undertaking in Mr Wilson’s affidavit. He emphasised that the percentages in the undertaking were minimum percentages and that depending on the result of the litigation, he intended to seek more for unsecured creditors and was at liberty to do so. Mr Walker also said this was consistent with discussions that he had with LPF leading up to the entry into the Services Deed.

[64] Discussions between the liquidators and SPF on this point continued. There were disagreements over the content of submissions to the Supreme Court and over whether Mr Walker should file a further affidavit with the Court. I will detail these disagreements later in this judgment. For now, it suffices to note that the plaintiffs in the current proceedings say that at this time SPF was resiling from its earlier commitment to hold the surplus under the Allied security for the benefit of PVL’s unsecured creditors.

[65] After the hearing, but before the Supreme Court delivered judgment, PVL and the PVL subsidiaries settled with PwC. The Supreme Court nonetheless decided to deliver a judgment, doing so in October 2017.5 The Court, by majority, said it would have largely dismissed PwC’s appeal.6 The majority found that because of SPF’s undertaking, there was no abuse of process.7

The Distribution Agreement and the initial Payment Direction

[66] From about May 2017 there were settlement discussions between PVL and PwC. On PVL’s side the settlement discussions were largely fronted by Philip Newland, the sole director of SPF. Mr Newland did so with Mr Walker’s authority. By mid-August 2017, it appeared likely that a settlement would be reached under which PwC would pay $33,800,000.

[67] Alongside the negotiations with PwC, there were negotiations between SPF and the liquidators/PVL as to how the proceeds of any settlements with PwC and the directors should be split between SPF and PVL. These negotiations were over two issues. The main issue was the extent to which, if at all, SPF was obliged to hold the surplus of any settlement proceeds (beyond its entitlements under the Services Deed) for the benefit of PVL’s unsecured creditors. The other issue was whether the costs incurred by SPF in purchasing the Allied security and the DFG security constituted Project Costs under the Deed. That issue also affected the calculation of SPF’s entitlement under the Deed to be paid the Services Fee.

[68] Those negotiations concluded with SPF, Mr Walker, PVL and the PVL subsidiaries entering into an “Agreement on Distribution of Resolution Sum” (the Distribution Agreement) on 19 August 2017. Clause 1.1 of the Distribution Agreement provided that, subject to receipt of the anticipated settlement proceeds of

$33,800,000 from PwC, the following payments should be made from that sum in the following order:

5 PricewaterhouseCoopers v Walker [2017] NZSC 151, [2018] 1 NZLR 735.

  1. The majority said it would have allowed the appeal only to the extent as was necessary to give effect to its procedural directions (namely, that SPF and Mr Walker enter into a contractually enforceable document recording various matters, including SPF’s agreement to the distribution of proceeds as outlined in Mr Wilson’s affidavit): at [94]–[96]. Elias CJ, dissenting, considered that the Court should decline to issue judgment in the case because the matter had settled: at [135].

7 At [91].

(a) Payment of all outstanding Project Costs to SPF, those Project Costs to include the purchase prices of the Allied security and the DFG security.

(b) Payment of the Services Fee to SPF.

(c) Payment of all other amounts payable to SPF in accordance with the terms of the Services Deed.

(d) From the remaining funds, payment of $5,380,000 to PVL and the balance to SPF.

[69] Clause 1.2 provided a mechanism for splitting any settlement recoveries from the defendant directors. The first $2 million was to be allocated 10 per cent to PVL and 90 per cent to SPF. Any recoveries over $2 million were to be shared equally.

[70] Clause 1.4 provided that upon distribution of the settlement recoveries in accordance with cls 1.1 and 1.2, SPF would assign the Allied security and the DFG security to the liquidators.

[71] Clause 4.1 said that the Distribution Agreement was in full and final settlement of all claims the parties might have against each other “in connection with the matters dealt with in this agreement”. The Agreement also said, in cl 5.3, that nothing in the Agreement “shall affect the Services Deed”.

[72] The Distribution Agreement was the result of protracted negotiations. It was signed by Mr Newland for SPF, Mr Walker as liquidator and Mr Walker on behalf of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. Mr Scutter refused to sign it.

[73] On 19 August 2017, at the same time as entering into the Distribution Agreement, Mr Woodhams, for SPF, and Mr Walker, for PVL, signed a payment direction to Meredith Connell (then the solicitors for the plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings) (the Payment Direction). This was signed in anticipation of Meredith Connell receiving settlement proceeds of $33,800,000 from PwC. In the Payment Direction, SPF and PVL provided an irrevocable instruction to Meredith Connell to disburse the proceeds as follows:

(a) $28,420,000 to SPF; and

(b) $5,380,000 to PVL.

[74] The plaintiffs in the current proceedings make several claims in relation to the Distribution Agreement and the negotiations that led to it. They say that it gave SPF greater benefits than it enjoyed under the Services Deed. Among their claims, they say that Mr Walker entered into it as a result of undue influence from SPF, that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary duties as liquidator in entering into it, and that the Agreement is unlawful.

Settlement with PwC and amended Payment Direction

[75] On 25 August 2017, a settlement agreement was entered into with PwC (the PwC settlement agreement). The parties were the liquidators, PVL, the PVL subsidiaries, LPF, SPF and PwC. Both Mr Walker and Mr Scutter signed the agreement. PwC agreed to pay $33,800,000 to settle the claims against it.

[76] Under cl 5.1 of the PwC settlement agreement, PVL, SPF and LPF provided an indemnity to PwC for costs it sustained arising from (a) any of the defendant directors seeking contribution from PwC or (b) Mr Henderson or companies associated with him making a claim to the settlement sum paid by PwC. Associated with that indemnity, cl 2.4 of the PwC settlement agreement obliged LPF to hold a retention of $2 million in net assets pending resolution of the claims against the defendant directors.

[77] In anticipation of those terms being in the PwC settlement agreement, on 25 August 2017 SPF and PVL agreed that PVL would contribute $400,000 to the

$2 million retention that LPF was to hold. This was on the basis that if the claims against the directors were resolved and the retention released to LPF, LPF would return the $400,000 to PVL. To reflect this, SPF and PVL agreed to amend the Payment Direction earlier given to Meredith Connell so that an extra $400,000 would be paid to SPF and commensurately less would be paid to PVL. They also agreed that the bulk of the funds payable to PVL would be paid to a bank account in Mr Walker’s

name. An amended Payment Direction was signed by Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker instructing Meredith Connell to disburse the PwC settlement proceeds as follows:

(a) $28,820,000 to SPF;

(b) $900,000 to PVL; and

(c) $4,080,000 to Mr Walker.

[78] When Meredith Connell received the $33,800,000 settlement payment from PwC, it distributed the payment in accordance with the amended Payment Direction.

Mr Henderson raises his hand

[79] Hovering over the settlement discussions between PVL and PwC were concerns arising from companies associated with Mr Henderson having claimed, from 2016, that they had purchased security interests in some of the PVL subsidiaries. Solicitors acting for those associated companies had written to PVL’s lawyers seeking information about the PVL proceedings. Mr Henderson had also contacted Mr Woodhams directly.

[80] The proposed settlement with PwC required that there be a discontinuance of all the claims against it by all plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings. Such a discontinuance had to be signed by all remaining defendants (as well as the plaintiffs). Mr Henderson was a defendant. PVL and SPF were primarily concerned that Mr Henderson might decline to sign the discontinuance unless his associated companies, who claimed to have security interests in some PVL subsidiaries, received a monetary advantage. They were also concerned that even if the settlement proceeded smoothly, the associated companies would at a later point claim an entitlement to a share of the settlement proceeds under their claimed security interests.

[81] As it turned out, Mr Henderson did not prevent the settlement with PwC from being completed. His associated companies did, however, make claims to a share of the settlement proceeds by bringing one of the current proceedings.

Settlement with the directors

[82] A settlement was reached with the defendant directors on 21 December 2017. They paid $3 million to settle the claims against them. In accordance with the Distribution Agreement, SPF received $2,300,000 from the settlement sum and PVL the balance of $700,000.

[83] Settlement of the claim against the directors meant that the $2 million retention held by LPF under the PwC settlement agreement could be released. Accordingly, LPF returned to PVL the $400,000 that PVL had contributed to that retention sum.

SPF accounts to Allied and DFG

[84] In March 2018, Mr Woodhams calculated the amounts that were payable to Allied and DFG under the agreements to purchase their respective securities. For DFG, he calculated that the maximum amount ($1,650,000) was payable. SPF paid that amount to DFG.

[85] The amount payable to Allied was five per cent of the net proceeds of the PVL proceedings. The net proceeds were to be calculated by reference to, among other things, SPF’s Project Costs. Mr Woodhams calculated those Project Costs. In accordance with the terms of the deed with Allied, he arranged for Meredith Connell to certify to Allied that the Project Costs were “not less than $10,609,189”.

Mr Scutter resigns as liquidator of PVL

[86] After the settlements with PwC and the directors, it took some time for payments to be made to Mr Scutter and Mr Sullivan for their outstanding fees. They eventually agreed to a compromise on their fees. They were paid in early 2018. I note that Mr Walker says he also was never paid the full amount of his fees.

[87] With the PVL proceedings having concluded, the original purpose for having Mr Scutter as a second liquidator of PVL had concluded. He resigned as liquidator on 22 March 2018, with the approval of the High Court.

SPF transfers securities to PVG

[88] Clause 1.4 of the Distribution Agreement provided that after distribution of the settlement proceeds, SPF would assign the Allied security and the DFG security to Mr Walker or his nominee. In August 2018, SPF assigned the securities to Mr Walker’s nominee. The nominee was a trustee of a trust that Mr Walker had settled for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of PVL.

The first of the current proceedings is commenced

[89] The first of the two current consolidated proceedings was commenced in 2019. It is CIV 2019-404-1160 (the 1160 proceeding). The 1160 proceeding has four plaintiffs, led by 100 Investments Ltd (together, the 100 Parties). The 100 Parties allege they hold first ranking securities over the assets of some of the PVL subsidiaries. They say that the settlement proceeds from the PVL proceedings were jointly owned by the plaintiffs in those proceedings, namely PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. They say that their securities entitled them to the subsidiaries’ share of the settlement proceeds and that the defendants deprived them of their interests in the subsidiaries’ share. Initially, there were only four defendants to the 1160 proceeding: Mr Walker, Mr Scutter, LPF and SPF.

Mr Walker and Mr Henderson enter into a settlement deed

[90] On 22 October 2020, Mr Walker and Mr Henderson (and others) entered into a settlement deed (the Settlement Deed). The Deed settled various claims made by Mr Walker (and entities associated with him) against Mr Henderson (and entities associated with him) and vice versa, other than the claims outlined in the 1160 proceeding and any reasonable amendments as might be made to those claims.

[91] Mr Walker contends that the Settlement Deed prevents certain of the claims in the current proceedings from being pursued. The plaintiffs dispute this.

Mr Walker ceases being a liquidator and a litigation guardian is appointed for him

[92] When the 100 Parties commenced the 1160 proceeding, Mr Walker was still the liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. In 2021, Mr Walker ceased being the

liquidator of those companies. At various dates in 2021 and 2022, Mr Whitley was appointed liquidator of those companies.

[93] In July 2021, Mr Walker filed a memorandum in the 1160 proceeding. Among other things, Mr Walker described some serious mental health issues. In due course, Wylie J found that Mr Walker was incapacitated in terms of r 4.29 of the High Court Rules 2016. The Judge appointed David Cooper as Mr Walker’s litigation guardian. Mr Cooper KC (as he subsequently became) was Mr Walker’s litigation guardian and counsel at trial.

The current consolidated proceedings

[94] Upon Mr Walker ceasing to be, and Mr Whitley becoming, the liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries, some of the defendants in the 1160 proceeding wished to join Mr Whitley, PVL and the PVL subsidiaries to that proceeding to ensure they were bound by any judgment. As a result, in February 2022, Mr Whitley, PVL and the PVL subsidiaries (together, the Whitley Parties) were joined as the fifth to seventeenth defendants to the 1160 proceeding.

[95] At about the same time as they were joined to the 1160 proceeding, the Whitley Parties brought their own proceeding, CIV 2022-404-274 (the 274 proceeding). The Whitley Parties are the first to thirteenth plaintiffs in the 274 proceeding. There are eight defendants to that proceeding: the four 100 Parties, Mr Walker, Mr Scutter, LPF and SPF.

[96] The two proceedings were consolidated, by consent, in March 2022. A few months prior to trial, both sets of plaintiffs settled with Mr Scutter and discontinued their proceedings against him.

[97] The 100 Parties and the Whitley Parties both allege that Mr Walker, LPF and SPF are required to repay some of the settlement proceeds received from the PVL proceedings. The two sets of plaintiffs differ as to who should receive any money repaid by those defendants. The 100 Parties say any money (or most of it) should be paid to them as holders of first ranking securities over the assets of the PVL

subsidiaries. The Whitley Parties say any money should be paid to Mr Whitley as liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries.

A separate application by Mr Whitley against Mr Walker

[98] Mr Whitley has brought a separate application against Mr Walker for a review of the reasonableness of the fees charged by Mr Walker as liquidator of PVL (the remuneration application). Mr Whitley brought the remuneration application in November 2021, shortly after he was appointed liquidator of PVL. The application is brought in the PVL liquidation proceeding, which was commenced in the High Court at Christchurch in 2010.

[99] In 2022, LPF applied to consolidate the PVL liquidation proceeding with the current proceedings. The Whitley Parties opposed that application. They told the Court that they did not intend for the court in the current proceedings to rule on the reasonableness or otherwise of Mr Walker’s fees and had no intention to raise that issue in these proceedings. The 100 Parties also opposed the application. They told the Court that issues as to the fees charged by Mr Walker were not part of the 1160 proceeding and would be dealt with separately by Mr Whitley in the remuneration application.

[100] Wylie J declined LPF’s consolidation application.8 The Judge recorded that it was not the plaintiffs’ intention in the current proceedings to challenge the reasonableness of the fees charged by Mr Walker and that they intended to amend their pleadings to reflect that.9 The Judge nevertheless determined the consolidation application on the basis of the pleadings then before the court.10

8 100 Investments Ltd v Walker [2022] NZHC 1379.

9 At [32].

10 At [38].

The claims, affirmative defences and counterclaims

Claims by the 100 Parties

[101] The 100 Parties bring six causes of action. The first four are against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF and are based on the 100 Parties’ alleged security interests in the PVL subsidiaries’ share of the settlement proceeds:

(a) The first cause of action is in conversion. The 100 Parties say that Mr Walker, LPF and SPF acted inconsistently with their security interests in the settlement proceeds. This cause of action assumes that the PVL subsidiaries’ alleged interest in the settlement proceeds — a chose in action — is capable of being converted.

(b) The second cause of action is in money had and received. This is essentially put forward as an alternative to the first cause of action, in case it is found that a chose in action is not capable of being converted.

(c) The third cause of action was described in submissions as “tracing”. The 100 Parties claim that they can trace their security interests into the settlement proceeds received by SPF and LPF.

(d) The fourth cause of action is for breach of fiduciary duty (by Mr Walker) and knowing receipt (by SPF and LPF). The 100 Parties allege that Mr Walker, in conducting the PVL proceedings (including by entering into the Services Deed and Distribution Agreement), breached his fiduciary duty as liquidator in various respects, and that SPF and LPF knowingly received over 80 per cent of the settlement proceeds as a consequence of his breaches.

[102] The defendants deny that the 100 Parties hold security interests in any PVL subsidiary and deny that the subsidiaries were entitled to any share of the settlement proceeds. Further, the defendants say that if the 100 Parties had any rights as secured creditors to the settlement proceeds, those rights are subject to Mr Walker’s right to recover, in priority to any secured creditor, his fees and costs from the proceeds.

[103] The fifth and sixth causes of action are against Mr Walker alone. They are for breach of his duties as liquidator of the PVL subsidiaries (fifth cause of action) and of PVL (sixth cause of action). The 100 Parties bring these causes of action under s 301 of the Companies Act in their capacity as unsecured creditors of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. Mr Walker denies that they are unsecured creditors of those companies and denies breaching his duties.

Claims by the Whitley Parties

[104] The Whitley Parties brought eight causes of action at the start of trial but pursued only six by the end of trial:

(a) The first cause of action is against Mr Walker. It concerns the voidable preference recoveries obtained by him. The Whitley Parties claim that Mr Walker had a duty to hold those recoveries on trust for the unsecured creditors of the PVL entity from which the voidable preference was paid, and that in breach of that duty he used the recoveries to pay for the costs of the PVL proceedings. They bring their claim under ss 284 and 301 of the Companies Act.

(b) The second cause of action is against Mr Walker, SPF and LPF. The Whitley Parties seek declarations that the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement are not binding on PVL or the PVL subsidiaries on various grounds (including that those agreements required court approval or that, in entering into the Distribution Agreement, Mr Walker breached his duty as liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries to act in their interests and without any conflict) as well as declarations that payments to SPF and LPF under those agreements were unreasonable and orders that those payments be repaid. In part, the Whitley Parties bring this claim under s 284 of the Companies Act.

(c) The third cause of action is against SPF for breach of contract. The Whitley Parties say that SPF breached an agreement to hold any surplus received under the Allied security for the benefit of unsecured creditors of PVL, that SPF breached the Services Deed by wrongly claiming

various costs as Project Costs (primarily the payments to Allied and DFG and the provision of security for costs) and that SPF breached the Distribution Agreement also by wrongly claiming various costs as Project Costs.

(d) The fourth cause of action is against Mr Walker for breach of his fiduciary and statutory duties as liquidator of PVL and its subsidiaries. Essentially, the Whitley Parties claim that Mr Walker breached his duties by favouring himself, SPF and LPF to the exclusion of other creditors. They seek orders under ss 284 and 301 of the Companies Act.

(e) The fifth cause of action is against SPF and LPF for knowing receipt. This springs off the breaches of fiduciary duty by Mr Walker alleged in the fourth cause of action. The Whitley Parties claim that SPF and LPF received a portion of the settlement proceeds knowing that those proceeds were being paid to them by Mr Walker in breach of his fiduciary duties.

(f) The sixth cause of action was against SPF and LPF for knowing assistance of Mr Walker’s alleged breach of fiduciary duty in arranging payment of $4,080,000 to himself from the settlement proceeds. The Whitley Parties abandoned this cause of action during the trial.

(g) The seventh cause of action was against SPF and LPF for misrepresentation in relation to the Allied security. The Whitley Parties abandoned this cause of action during the trial.

(h) The eighth cause of action is against SPF and LPF, alleging that they unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. The Whitley Parties ask that the Agreement be set aside and that SPF and LPF repay the additional amounts received under the Agreement that were not otherwise owed under the Services Deed.

[105] The Whitley Parties formally abandoned the sixth and seventh causes of action by filing a fourth amended statement of claim shortly before the end of the trial. That amended claim also deleted a claim (previously part of the fourth cause of action) that Mr Walker had paid himself fees of $4,080,000 “despite not being owed anything”. That deletion reflected an acceptance by the Whitley Parties that Mr Walker had rendered an invoice for those fees and that the reasonableness of the fees was a matter for determination in the remuneration application that Mr Whitley has brought in the PVL liquidation proceeding, rather than being an issue in these proceedings.

Mr Walker’s affirmative defences and counterclaims

[106] Mr Walker pleads the Settlement Deed as an affirmative defence to the claims brought by the Whitley Parties, on the basis that Mr Whitley is associated with Mr Henderson, that the claims brought by the Whitley Parties were therefore settled by the Deed and so it is an abuse of process for those claims to be pursued. Mr Walker brings a counterclaim against the Whitley Parties for damages for breach of the Deed and raises an affirmative set-off defence in respect of the alleged damages liability.

[107] In respect of the claims brought by the 100 Parties, Mr Walker pleads the Settlement Deed as an affirmative defence to the fourth cause of action (alleging breach of fiduciary duty). He says no such claim was made in the 1160 proceeding at the time of the Deed and the claim was therefore settled by the Deed. Mr Walker brings a counterclaim alleging that the 100 Parties have induced the Whitley Parties to breach the Deed by bringing the claims in the 274 proceeding. He raises an affirmative set-off defence in respect of the alleged damages liability for that breach.

[108] Mr Walker pleads two limitation defences. The first is in respect of the claims brought in each proceeding under s 301 of the Companies Act. Those claims are subject to a special start date in s 16(1)(i) of the Limitation Act 2010, namely the date on which the liquidator of the relevant company was appointed. Mr Walker says all the s 301 claims were brought more than six years after that date.

[109] Mr Walker’s second limitation defence applies only to some of the claims brought in the 274 proceeding and does not depend on the special start date that applies to claims under s 301 of the Companies Act. Mr Walker says some of the claims in

the 274 proceeding were brought more than six years after the date of the act or omission on which the claim is based.

LPF’s affirmative defences and counterclaim

[110] LPF pleads three affirmative defences to the Whitley Parties’ claims. It says that cl 4.1 of the Distribution Agreement is a bar to proceedings against SPF by the Whitley Parties, that any claims against LPF are parasitic on claims against SPF and so the claims against LPF are an abuse of process. It raises limitation defences to the second, fifth and eighth causes of action. It says it is entitled to an indemnity from the Whitley Parties under cl 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement and this gives it a set-off against any other liability it may have to the Whitley Parties.

[111] LPF also brings a counterclaim against the Whitley Parties on the basis of the alleged indemnity in cl 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement.

SPF’s affirmative defences and counterclaim

[112] SPF raises three affirmative defences to the Whitley Parties’ claims and brings one counterclaim. These mirror those raised or brought by LPF.

The issues

[113] The pleadings raise a multitude of issues for determination. There is no point in listing them all here. I will identify them when I consider each cause of action (including counterclaims).

[114] That said, there are several issues that it will be convenient to address separately, because of their significance or because they are common to more than one cause of action. These issues are:

(a) Did SPF reach a binding agreement with Mr Walker that it would hold any surplus payable under the Allied security for the benefit of unsecured creditors?

(b) Did Mr Walker breach any fiduciary or other duty by entering into the Services Deed?

(c) Were the PVL subsidiaries bound by the terms of the Services Deed?

(d) Did Mr Walker breach any fiduciary or other duties, or was he unduly influenced, in entering into the Distribution Agreement?

(e) Was the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement, unlawful as an abuse of process?

(f) Are the 100 Parties secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries?

(g) If the 100 Parties are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, what portion of the settlement proceeds are the PVL subsidiaries entitled to?

(h) If the 100 Parties are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, to what extent does Mr Walker enjoy a prior right to recoup from the PVL settlement proceeds his costs and remuneration in obtaining those proceeds?

(i) Does s 284 of the Companies Act confer jurisdiction to make the orders sought by Mr Whitley affecting the rights of Mr Walker, LPF and SPF?

(j) In light of the Settlement Deed, are any of the claims against Mr Walker an abuse of process?

(k) Are the claims under s 301 of the Companies Act out of time under the Limitation Act 2010?

Procedural matters

[115] I heard and determined many interlocutory applications in the months prior to trial. Applications continued during and (unusually) after trial. Some bear noting.

Mr Walker’s application for further discovery relating to the alleged association between Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley

[116] A few months before trial, Mr Walker applied for further discovery from the plaintiffs. The application arose out of affirmative defences and counterclaims that Mr Walker first pleaded in his statement of defence and counterclaim dated 9 October 2023. That pleading was based on the Settlement Deed between Mr Walker and Mr Henderson (among others). The Deed includes undertakings that “[Mr Henderson’s partner] and Mr Henderson or any entity associated with them” will not bring claims (other than the claims then outlined in the 1160 proceeding and any reasonable amendments to them) against Mr Walker. Mr Walker pleaded that Mr Henderson was associated with Mr Whitley (as liquidator of the PVL companies) on the basis of allegations that Mr Henderson:

(a) initiated and orchestrated Mr Whitley’s appointment as liquidator;

(b) funded Mr Whitley to bring the 274 proceeding; and

(c) acted jointly and in concert with Mr Whitley in bringing and pursuing the two consolidated proceedings.

[117] The plaintiffs in both proceedings denied those allegations. Mr Walker sought orders that the plaintiffs provide further discovery of documents relating to that dispute. In a December 2023 judgment, I ordered the plaintiffs to provide further discovery of the documents sought by Mr Walker.11 These included documents recording communications between the plaintiffs or Mr Henderson (or anyone acting on their behalf) and Mr Whitley (or anyone acting on his behalf) in relation to funding provided to Mr Whitley for the 274 proceeding, and documents relating to Mr Whitley’s funding.

[118] In late January 2024, Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley each made a supplementary affidavit of documents in supposed compliance with those discovery orders. It became apparent at trial that their affidavits were deficient. Mr Henderson

11 100 Investments Ltd v Walker [2023] NZHC 3732 at [111].

and Mr Whitley had each failed to discover a deed in which Mr Henderson and the co-trustee of his family trust indemnified Mr Whitley against the costs incurred as liquidator of the PVL companies and agreed to pay Mr Whitley the cost of his time as liquidator. Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley had also failed to discover written communications relating to that deed.

[119] I return to this when I make observations on Mr Henderson’s and Mr Whitley’s evidence.

LPF’s challenge to Mr Whitley’s claim to litigation privilege over his examination of Mr Eathorne

[120] In 2023, Mr Whitley required Ryan Eathorne to be examined under s 261(3)(c) of the Companies Act. Mr Eathorne had been an employee of Mr Walker. He worked closely with Mr Walker throughout the liquidation of PVL and the PVL proceedings.

[121] Mr Whitley examined Mr Eathorne in May 2023, by Zoom. Mr Moss, who is counsel for the Whitley Parties in these proceedings, conducted the examination, with Mr Whitley present. The Zoom meeting was recorded. Mr Whitley claimed litigation privilege over the recording of the examination. He said that the information discussed in the examination was prepared, and the examination was made, for the dominant purpose of preparing for the 274 proceeding.

[122] LPF challenged that claim to privilege. I heard and determined the challenge on the second day of trial. I found that the Whitley Parties did not have a privilege in Mr Whitley’s examination of Mr Eathorne.12

[123] I return to this when I make observations on Mr Whitley’s evidence and when I deal with the allegation that Mr Walker was unduly influenced by SPF to enter into the Distribution Agreement.

12 100 Investments Ltd v Walker [2024] NZHC 468 at [17].

Amendments to pleadings

[124] The pleadings were amended through trial. Most of the amendments were made by consent. Some of the amended pleadings were filed only after trial. The parties co-operated in filing bundles of the final pleadings.

LPF’s discovery after trial

[125] Shortly before the end of trial, it became apparent that LPF’s previous discovery affidavits were incomplete. LPF’s IT provider had conducted searches of LPF’s emails and other electronic documents. This had led to the identification of just over 19,000 potentially relevant documents (the electronic data set). Because of the high number of documents, the parties had essentially agreed on tailored discovery orders. LPF applied those orders to the electronic data set and provided discovery accordingly.

[126] During trial, the Whitley Parties obtained about 17,000 further documents from one of PVL’s former solicitors. They then provided those documents to LPF as part of ongoing discovery. In the course of considering how to deal with such a large number of additional documents during trial, Mr Woodhams conducted his own searches of his email system. This led to the identification of several emails that were not in the electronic data set. LPF’s solicitors advised the parties of this on 10 April 2024.

[127] In response, the 100 Parties applied for an order that LPF file and serve an updated list of documents. They recognised that any further discovery would not be provided until the evidence was concluded and closing submissions had been delivered. They proposed that upon receiving the further discovery they would consider whether to apply to recall any witnesses.

[128] On 16 April 2024, I made an order that LPF provide further discovery by 24 May 2024 (which was more than a month after the parties were expected to make closing submissions). I also ordered that any application by the plaintiffs to recall witnesses be made within ten working days of LPF serving its further affidavit of documents.

[129] It took longer than anticipated for LPF to complete and serve its further affidavit of documents. Issues then arose between the parties about matters such as LPF’s claims to privilege over some of the documents and about how any documents would be admitted into evidence. These matters were not finally resolved until late November 2024.

[130] In the end, the parties filed an additional volume of the common bundle containing some of the documents from LPF’s further discovery. The parties agreed, in a memorandum dated 27 November 2024, that the documents:

... are to be received into evidence by consent as if they were referred to in the plaintiffs’ opening submissions and on the basis that no further submissions are to be filed, and no applications will be made to recall witnesses.

[131] I was hoping that the parties might also agree that I could be deemed to have read the documents. They did not, so I read them.

The evidence

[132] Before addressing any of the issues, I make some observations about the witnesses of fact. They gave evidence about events that, for the most part, occurred from 2012 to 2017, many years prior to trial. Unsurprisingly, those witnesses were often reliant on documents to refresh their memories and sometimes could not recall particular events, even when they were recorded in contemporaneous documents. The case was document intensive. The common bundle was over 15,000 pages.

Mr Henderson

[133] Mr Henderson referred in his brief to a large number of documents, particularly to prove the 100 Parties’ claim that they are secured creditors of some of the PVL subsidiaries. The defendants objected to some of that evidence on the basis it was hearsay. In supplementary questions, Mr Henderson was taken to the documents in issue in order to explain, for example, the circumstances in which they had been executed or had come into his possession. Mr Henderson had no difficulty recalling these circumstances, even when they had occurred many years ago. He described the

circumstances confidently and articulately. To that extent, he was an impressive witness.

[134] Mr Henderson was less impressive under cross-examination. He was often evasive. Worse, on occasions he lied.

[135] I hesitate to find that a witness has lied. I therefore explain my reasons in some detail.

[136] In cross-examination, Mr Henderson was asked about the circumstances in which Mr Whitley, in September 2021, was appointed liquidator of the PVL companies. He was also asked about the funding of Mr Whitley’s costs and fees as liquidator. Mr Henderson was often evasive in answering these questions. For example, Mr Cooper took Mr Henderson to an email he sent to Mr Whitley on 5 September 2021 in which he identified the PVL companies “we are wanting to appoint you to” as liquidator. Mr Cooper suggested that Mr Henderson had engineered the appointment of Mr Whitley, which Mr Henderson denied. There was then the following exchange:

  1. So why did you write “the companies we are wanting to appoint you to are”?

A. Just a turn of phrase so.

Q. Who is the “we” you refer to?

A. It’s, it’s just a general appellation.

[137] Mr Henderson then said that by the time of Mr Whitley’s appointment as liquidator, “the creditors” had agreed in principle to fund Mr Whitley’s fees. When Mr Cooper asked whether there was any written communication about this, Mr Henderson was evasive: “I couldn’t tell you right now whether there was written communication.”

[138] Mr Henderson was then reminded that his affidavit of documents (made shortly before trial, in response to my December 2023 discovery order) did not disclose any written communication agreeing to pay Mr Whitley’s fees. When Mr Cooper asked if

that helped him remember whether the agreement was reached orally, Mr Henderson was again evasive: “I’m having to say I imagine it was reached orally.”

[139] Mr Cooper then asked Mr Henderson which creditors had agreed to fund Mr Whitley’s fees. He answered, “the plaintiff creditors in these proceedings” (that is, the 100 Parties). Mr Cooper returned to the topic a little later in cross-examination:

Q. Do you have a written agreement with Mr Whitley to cover the terms of this financing?

A. No I don’t.

Q. Who are the parties to the agreement or the arrangement to finance?

A. The creditors, the plaintiff creditors.

Q. Are you personally a party to it?

A. No.

...

Q. Do you consider that you have a legal obligation or you or the plaintiff companies to fund Mr Whitley?

A. By that you mean a contractual obligation?

Q. An enforceable obligation.

A. Absolutely, I made a commitment to Mr Whitley and I'll stick to it.

Q. So it’s a binding unwritten contract, is that how you would describe it?

A. Well I’ve never thought of it in lawful terms. I’ve thought of it in moral terms is that I made a commitment to Mr Whitley that these creditors would pay for it and I’m going to make sure that happens.

...

Q. From your recent discovery there are no documents that I have seen which record these payment arrangements you have with Mr Whitley, is that because there are no such documents?

A. I would assume so, yes.

Q. And it must follow from that that the arrangements you have with Mr Whitley have been agreed entirely in discussions?

A. Yes.

[140] Mr Salmon KC traversed the same topic when he cross-examined Mr Henderson. Mr Henderson confirmed that the funding arrangement with Mr Whitley was a “verbal commitment”. Mr Salmon then asked who had agreed to fund Mr Whitley. Mr Henderson answered, “just the plaintiffs here”. Mr Salmon sought clarification:

  1. And you did that with no other parties funding it, just your associated parties who are parties to this proceeding?

A. Yes.

Q. And you’re certain of that?

A. I can’t think who else might be up for contributing Mr Salmon.

[141] In the passages I have quoted in the preceding five paragraphs, Mr Henderson was repeatedly evasive. However, in the end he was clear that the funding arrangement with Mr Whitley was undocumented, that only the 100 Parties were parties to it and that he himself was not.

[142] The reason for Mr Henderson’s evasion became clear when Mr Whitley was cross-examined. Mr Whitley disclosed that he held a written indemnity “over [Mr Henderson’s] apartment in Christchurch”.

[143] Mr Whitley’s disclosure was a surprise. As noted earlier, in December 2023 I had ordered the plaintiffs to discover documents recording communications between the plaintiffs or Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley in relation to funding for the 274 proceeding and documents recording or relating to the funding of the 274 proceeding. Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley had, in response, made discovery affidavits in late January 2024. Neither discovered any written indemnity. Mr Henderson then, in evidence, said that the funding arrangement was not in writing and that he was not a party to it.

[144] Once Mr Whitley disclosed the existence of an indemnity, the plaintiffs discovered the indemnity and related written communications during an overnight break in Mr Whitley’s cross-examination. The indemnity was in the form of a deed from Mr Henderson and Dorset Trustee Services No 2 Ltd (Dorset Trustee). In the deed, Mr Henderson and Dorset Trustee not only indemnified Mr Whitley against the

costs and liabilities he incurred as liquidator of the PVL companies but agreed to pay Mr Whitley for his time as liquidator. That is, the deed contained both an indemnity and a promise to pay Mr Whitley for his time. Further, the deed provided Mr Whitley with a form of security over Mr Henderson’s home (an apartment owned by Mr Henderson and Dorset Trustee).

[145] The related written communications consisted of emails between the parties’ respective lawyers negotiating the deed, including draft forms of the deed. These communications ran from March to September 2022. Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley had failed to discover any of these communications in their affidavits of documents.

[146] The deed’s recitals stated that Mr Whitley had accepted appointment as liquidator of various PVL companies from 22 September 2021 conditional upon Mr Henderson and Dorset Trustee executing the deed. Mr Whitley and Mr Henderson (who was recalled for further cross-examination after Mr Whitley’s disclosure of the deed) confirmed that those recitals were accurate. The deed was not fully executed until 7 September 2022. It had, therefore, taken almost a year after Mr Whitley’s appointment for the deed to be executed. In cross-examination, Mr Whitley said he had got “more than a little tetchy” with how long it took.

[147] When Mr Henderson was recalled for cross-examination about the deed he accepted that his earlier evidence had been incorrect but said he “had completely forgotten about this agreement”. He was insistent:

I can, I can confirm with the Court I forgot completely about this. I’m embarrassed about that. I’ve apologised for that. I have no problem whatsoever with this agreement being before the Court in these proceedings.

[148] I do not accept that Mr Henderson forgot about the deed. It is not credible that he could have forgotten about it, given that:

(a) It was a one-off transaction. Mr Henderson confirmed that he had never before entered into an arrangement in which he provided an indemnity to a liquidator supported by a security over his home.

(b) Mr Whitley’s appointment as liquidator of the PVL companies had been conditional on the deed being executed. Mr Henderson accepted that this condition was a central feature of his arrangement with Mr Whitley.

(c) It had then taken almost a year to execute the deed, during which time Mr Whitley was becoming tetchy about the delay.

(d) During the negotiations towards the execution of the deed, Mr Henderson had received, by email, eight versions of the deed in draft or executed form.

(e) On 20 October 2023, Mr Cooper wrote to Mr Hucker, solicitor for the 100 Parties, requesting discovery of documents recording or relating to funding arrangements between Mr Henderson and Mr Whitley. When the 100 Parties did not volunteer that discovery, Mr Cooper sought and obtained a discovery order from the court in December 2023. Mr Henderson said he “[m]ost certainly” took his discovery obligations seriously. It is not believable that when making his discovery affidavit on 29 January 2024, Mr Henderson could have forgotten that in September 2022 he had provided an indemnity and funding deed to Mr Whitley supported by a security over his home. Mr Henderson gave evidence about five weeks after making that affidavit.

(f) Mr Henderson was otherwise able to recall events from many years before 2022.

[149] For these reasons, I find that Mr Henderson deliberately failed to disclose the deed and the related communications when making his affidavit dated 29 January 2024, lied when he gave evidence that the agreement to fund Mr Whitley’s costs and fees for the PVL liquidations was not in writing or documented, lied when he gave evidence that he was not a party to that agreement, and lied when he gave evidence that he had forgotten about the agreement.

[150] My findings have obvious implications for Mr Henderson’s credibility. Where his evidence is not supported by, or consistent with, other, reliable evidence, I have treated it carefully. However, very little in this case has depended on his credibility. Mr Henderson was not involved in, and gave no evidence on, most of the key events in dispute.

[151] Mr Henderson did give evidence on the alleged status of the 100 Parties as secured creditors. He was the source of many of the documents produced by the 100 Parties to support their case on that issue. Mr Cooper submitted in closing that Mr Henderson had shown himself to be dishonest and, accordingly, the authenticity and accuracy of those documents cannot be assumed. I do not accept that. Those documents were included in the common bundle without the defendants raising any objection to the application of the default rule (in r 9.5(1)(c) of the High Court Rules) that the documents are what they appear to be. Mr Henderson was not challenged on their authenticity and accuracy.

Mr Whitley

[152] Mr Whitley failed to discover the deed of indemnity and the associated correspondence. In cross-examination, he said he did not know why the indemnity had not been discovered but that he did not omit it deliberately. I accept his evidence, for two reasons. First, in cross-examination Mr Whitley was not at all evasive about his funding arrangements with Mr Henderson and his entities. He readily disclosed the existence of the indemnity. He did not claim, as Mr Henderson did, to have forgotten such an important part of his funding arrangements.

[153] Secondly, it became clear that Mr Whitley was in some significant ways a passive participant in these proceedings. There were several instances in which Mr Whitley appeared to be unaware of positions that had been taken on his behalf. For instance, he said he had not instructed his lawyers to resist Mr Walker’s December 2023 discovery application (they did). The most striking instance concerned discovery of the recording of his interview with Mr Eathorne. He said his position “all the way” had been to make full discovery and that if he had been asked about the defendants getting access to the recording he would “[a]bsolutely” have said

yes. But he said he had never been asked. This was puzzling, given that I had had to determine LPF’s challenge to Mr Whitley’s claim to privilege over the recording on the second day of trial. Re-examination did not clarify these matters.13

[154] It is troubling that Mr Whitley was such a passive participant in this litigation. But his passivity is consistent with him being unaware that his January 2024 discovery affidavit was incomplete. I consider it was likely a result of inattention rather than deliberate concealment on his part.

[155] Generally, I found Mr Whitley to be a credible witness. He was frank in answering questions. He made appropriate concessions. That said, Mr Whitley had little involvement in the key events in dispute.

Mr Sullivan

[156] Mr Sullivan was subpoenaed by the Whitley Parties to give evidence. He gave evidence of events from 2012 to early 2018. He said he had reviewed emails and documents from that period to refresh his memory. A will-say statement had been prepared for him, though he had edited it. That statement was measured, albeit it tended to focus on events or documents to which the plaintiffs wished to draw attention. In cross-examination, Mr Sullivan readily answered questions about those events and documents, and about others, in a balanced way.

Mr Scutter

[157] Mr Scutter was also subpoenaed by the Whitley Parties to give evidence. A will-say statement was prepared for him, which he reviewed. He said at the outset that his intention was to assist the Court by being open and clear about what he recalled about the events in issue. He did exactly that, both in his evidence-in-chief and in cross-examination.

13 Mr Whitley clarified his evidence on these matters in an affidavit that he made a few days after his evidence had concluded. The affidavit was made in opposition to an application by the defendants for orders preventing Mr Hucker and Mr Moss from continuing as counsel. I took Mr Whitley’s affidavit into account in determining that application. However, in determining the substantive issues in this judgment I have put his clarifying evidence to one side. That evidence was not given under the constraints that apply to re-examination during trial and I was not asked to consider the evidence for the purposes of determining the substantive issues.

Mr Walker

[158] Mr Walker gave evidence for two and a half days, most of it under cross-examination. He was open and often fulsome in his answers. This was reflected in the plaintiffs’ closing submissions, which drew on many of his answers. He was sometimes argumentative, but that was not surprising given the subject-matter of some of the questions. He was not evasive. Overall, I found him to be a credible witness.

[159] Mr Walker acknowledged that there were some events (such as telephone calls) recorded in contemporaneous documents of which he had no memory. This was not out of the ordinary, given the age of some of the events.

Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams

[160] Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams were both credible witnesses. They openly answered questions in cross-examination. Their answers were measured, albeit sometimes firm, particularly when characterisations of events or documents were put to them. Both readily acknowledged that there were some events that they could not recall from many years before.

Mr Justin Smith

[161] Mr Justin Smith gave evidence about his involvement in the PVL proceedings. In cross-examination, Mr Barker KC and Mr Moss explored aspects of his involvement. Mr Smith was open and measured in his answers.

Issue 1: Did SPF reach a binding agreement with Mr Walker that it would hold any surplus payable under the Allied security for the benefit of unsecured creditors?

[162] The Whitley Parties plead that LPF agreed with Mr Walker that SPF would purchase the Allied security for the sole purpose of protecting its fees under the funding agreement and that “LPF/SPF” would hold the balance of any net proceeds payable under the Allied security (after payment of fees to SPF and any balance of the payment to Allied) for the benefit of unsecured creditors. I refer to this alleged agreement as the “Allied security agreement”. The Whitley Parties allege that the Allied security agreement was reached through correspondence and conversations

between representatives of LPF and Mr Walker from 29 August 2012 to 19 February 2013. Finally, they allege that the Allied security agreement was a term of the funding agreement between SPF and PVL, albeit they acknowledge that the Allied security agreement’s terms were not referred to in the Services Deed.

[163] The 100 Parties do not plead the Allied security agreement. However, they opened and closed their case on the basis there was such an agreement.

[164] The defendants deny there was an Allied security agreement. Whether there was such an agreement is directly relevant to the Whitley Parties’ third cause of action (which alleges SPF breached the agreement). It is also relevant to other causes of action relating to the entry into the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement.

Legal principles governing the formation of contracts

[165] Both the Whitley Parties and the 100 Parties are claiming that there was a binding Allied security agreement — that is, a contract. The parties did not address me on the principles governing the formation of contracts, though their submissions were generally reflective of the principles that I am about to state.

[166] In Fletcher Challenge Energy Ltd v Electricity Corporation of New Zealand Ltd, the Court of Appeal said there were two prerequisites to the formation of a contract: (a) an intention to be immediately bound (at the point when the bargain is said to have been agreed); and (b) agreement, or the means of achieving agreement, on every term which (i) was legally essential to the formation of such a bargain or (ii) was regarded by the parties themselves as essential to their particular bargain.14 These two matters are determined objectively. In doing so, it is permissible to look to the background matrix of facts, including statements made orally or in writing in the course of negotiations.15 It is also permissible to consider subsequent conduct, including what the parties have said to each other after the date of the alleged contract.16 The Court of Appeal expressed reservations about the help that could be

  1. Fletcher Challenge Energy Ltd v Electricity Corporation of New Zealand Ltd [2002] 2 NZLR 433 (CA) at [53].

15 At [54].

16 At [56].

obtained from internal memoranda or statements of subjective intention made by individuals when giving evidence.17

Was there a binding Allied security agreement?

[167] The agreement was alleged to be one between LPF and Mr Walker, whereas the Services Deed is between SPF and PVL. No issue was taken in this regard by the defendants. That was appropriate. The relevant communications by LPF’s representatives were made on behalf of SPF (including in advance of SPF being incorporated). Mr Walker’s communications were as liquidator of PVL.

[168] On behalf of SPF, Mr Woodhams sent an initial offer of funding to Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan in a letter dated 21 August 2012. The letter said the offer was subject to two matters: (a) satisfactory completion of due diligence by SPF; and (b) signing formal funding documentation. The letter included a paragraph addressing the Allied security:

The offer is further conditional on the entry into satisfactory arrangements with Allied Finance Limited and Grant [Thornton] with respect to the first ranking security over the assets of Property Ventures Limited currently held by Allied Finance Limited in respect of which Grant [Thornton] has been appointed receiver.

[169] Mr Sullivan responded to the offer in a letter dated 29 August 2012. In relation to the Allied security, he said:
