Robert, you are the principal in this, you need to start behaving like one, stop hiding behind your henchman it is belittling of your intelligence and competence. Never forget that lawyers are the servants of intelligent men, and the advisors to foolish men with no balls.

[300] In cross-examination it was put to Mr Walker that this email made him feel like he was being “bullied”. Mr Walker denied that, saying he did not care what Mr Sheppard said. I accept Mr Walker’s evidence. It is consistent with what he said at the time: shortly after receiving Mr Sheppard’s email, Mr Walker emailed his team telling them that he had “promptly deleted” the email.

[301] In that same email, Mr Walker told his team that he knew that SPF was worried about getting an extension of its authority to negotiate with PwC, but also that “I think we must withdraw the extra $1.5 m quickly” and “I am not prepared to argue anymore. It has got to stop immediately.”

[302] Later in the morning of 20 July 2017, Mr Walker emailed Mr Woodhams, copying Mr Fowler:

I can no longer do this.

Our deal with the defendants is in jeopardy.

The arrangement is that I will get $5.25 m and I will take on the risks (and opportunities — see below) OTHER than Henderson.

...

The matter is ended.

[303] Still on 20 July 2017, Mr Walker then emailed his team and said he had been put under “intolerable pressure”, that he had “given in” and that he could not fight SPF “and they know that”. Mr Sullivan responded saying “SPF/LPF now need you much more than you need them, given the settlement interest of PwC.”

[304] On 21 July 2017, Mr Walker emailed Mr Woodhams confirming that he had authorised SPF to continue to try to negotiate a settlement with PwC for $30 million or more, and that the authority would not be withdrawn before 31 August 2017.

[305] There were then discussions between the liquidators and their legal team as to a proposal to put to SPF. A draft letter was prepared for Mr Fowler to send to Mr Bigio. It proposed that from any settlement with PwC and the directors of up to $30 million, the liquidators would be paid $5.25 million and $500,000 would be set aside as a fighting fund (unless the litigation risk with respect to Mr Henderson was otherwise removed). It proposed that at any settlement above $30 million, the liquidators would be entitled to 40 per cent of the excess. On 25 July 2017, Mr Scutter said he was (reluctantly) in agreement with the draft letter and Mr Walker said he needed more time to think about it. The draft letter was never sent.

[306] On 27 July 2017, Mr Bigio sent an email to Mr Fowler. He referred to Mr Walker’s email to Mr Woodhams of 20 July 2017 and enclosed a draft distribution agreement. The draft agreement proposed that if the proceeds of settlement with PwC and the directors exceeded $30 million (which in due course they did), the proceeds would be distributed as follows:

(a) Payment to SPF of outstanding Project Costs (defined to include the payments to Allied and DFG) and the Services Fee.

(b) The balance, if any, to be paid as follows:

(i) If the settlement proceeds did not exceed $32.5 million, $5.25 million to PVL and the balance to SPF.

(ii) If the settlement proceeds did exceed $32.5 million, $5.25 million to PVL and the balance to PVL (10 per cent) and SPF (90 per cent).

[307] Mr Fowler was in a trial and so there was a delay in him forwarding the draft agreement to Mr Walker and Mr Scutter. Once Mr Walker received the draft agreement, he sent an email to Mr Woodhams on 3 August 2017, copying Mr Fowler and Mr Eathorne. He said Mr Bigio’s draft was “wholly unacceptable” and that, failing agreement, he was considering a directions application. He added:

You people treat me like a fool. I am reasonable and accommodating despite my legal advice being that I am giving away rights that could leave me vulnerable to a claim by creditors of various sorts. A tipping point has very nearly been reached.

The constant harassment to which I am subject is ... having an adverse impact on my health. You have been warned.

[308] The plaintiffs made much of Mr Walker’s concluding comment. They suggested that it made clear to Mr Woodhams that Mr Walker felt harassed and was experiencing health issues. Mr Woodhams’ evidence was that he did not believe Mr Walker had been constantly harassed and that he regarded Mr Walker’s comment as part of him negotiating. I accept Mr Woodhams’ evidence. Having read many of Mr Walker’s emails, my impression is that not infrequently he expressed himself strongly as part of a negotiating position. In the email in question, it is notable that he also threatened a directions application. Further, Mr Walker immediately forwarded his email to Mr Sullivan and Mr Scutter, saying merely: “Keep you off to try and defuse what has become tense again.”

[309] In any event, SPF regarded Mr Walker’s email as a rejection of SPF’s proposed distribution agreement. On 7 August 2017, Mr Bigio emailed Mr Fowler to that effect. Mr Bigio said that in the absence of any agreement the position reverted to what it had

been before, namely that the distribution of any proceeds (beyond SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed) was at SPF’s discretion, subject to the undertaking given by SPF in the Supreme Court.

[310] The same day, Mr Fowler forwarded Mr Bigio’s email to Mr Walker and Mr Scutter, commenting: “In my view this makes it easy — the liquidators have no choice but to seek directions.” Mr Walker responded by asking when he should revoke SPF’s authority to negotiate with PwC and the directors.

[311] Mr Woodhams’ evidence was that Mr Walker called him after receiving Mr Bigio’s email. Mr Woodhams said Mr Walker was annoyed by SPF’s position, was aggressive in his tone and told Mr Woodhams he and Mr Scutter were in control of the PVL proceedings. Mr Woodhams’ evidence was not challenged, and I accept it.

[312] On 8 August 2017, Mr Bigio emailed Mr Fowler asking whether the liquidators still required resolution of matters between them and Mr Henderson to be a condition of any settlement that they would support. Mr Fowler forwarded the email to the liquidators and asked whether they even wanted to answer this right now. Mr Walker responded:

I think the silent treatment is the best option right now.

I see panic in their eyes — I got a long text from [Mr Newland] this morning

...

I don’t care if Henderson is in or not. ...

But we don’t tell them that and we demand more money.

I haven’t revoked the privilege to negotiate yet. [Mr Sullivan] thought we should keep that in reserve.

...

Mr Scutter sent an email saying he agreed with Mr Walker’s approach.

[313] The “silent treatment” appears to have been employed, as there was a pause in the flurry of communications between the parties. Mr Sullivan drafted a letter and settlement deed for Mr Fowler to send to SPF, proposing that Project Costs exclude the payments to Allied and DFG and that any excess above a $30 million settlement

be shared 50/50, but these drafts were never sent. Then, on 17 August 2017, Mr Sullivan reported to Mr Scutter, Mr Eathorne and Mr Fowler that he had spoken with Mr Walker and that an offer from PwC was imminent and was going to be accepted, and that the directors would also pay $2–3 million. Mr Sullivan said the total settlement was likely to be $37–38 million. He then turned to the distribution dispute:

As matters stand the letter and draft settlement deed has not been sent. [Mr Walker] has however told [Mr Newland] that his position remains that he wants $5.25M up to $30M. And Henderson dealt with.

As Henderson has not been dealt with ... you will need the additional money (which is the $750k to fend off Hendo attacks).

The other key issue is the share of funds over $30M — we have 50% in the letter. [Mr Walker] literally [is] terrified of [Mr Newland] — he will not talk to him and will not authorise any proposal in writing for fear of being rung and bullied.

He wants [Mr Eathorne] and [Mr Fowler] to urgently try to meet with Jonathan Woodhams ... to discuss the proposal verbally to get some high level response.

... John are you ok with this approach?

Mr Scutter replied that he was happy to proceed with that approach.

Mr Eathorne meets with Mr Woodhams on 17 August 2017

[314] In the evening of 17 August 2017, Mr Eathorne sent an email to Mr Walker, Mr Scutter, Mr Sullivan and Mr Fowler saying that he had met with Mr Woodhams that day. By this time, it was expected that PwC would settle for $33.8 million (an expectation that proved correct). Mr Eathorne’s email reported on his meeting with Mr Woodhams as follows:

After robust discussion he would not agree to PVL being distributed anything more than:

$5.38m from the initial $33.8m from PwC; plus 10% of the first $2m from the directors &

50% of ... any subsequent monies from directors. The Henderson factor is unaddressed.

[315] Mr Walker responded promptly that evening. He said:

We need at least $250 to $500k more to deal with [Mr Henderson]. But then do we really? If they get the money, any assertion he makes is against them.

I think we agree to that and then assert our rights when they claim the DFG purchase price as a project cost. And then we will release them for ... $250 to $500k perhaps.

[316] Mr Walker there questioned the need for a fighting fund. He proposed agreeing to the terms Mr Eathorne had outlined, anticipating that the liquidators could subsequently obtain the same amount of funds from SPF (“them”) in exchange for a release. The means by which Mr Walker anticipated this might happen is not clear. There was no oral evidence explaining it.

[317] The same evening, Mr Sullivan forwarded Mr Eathorne’s and Mr Walker’s emails to Mr Fowler, asking if he and Mr Eathorne could come and brief him. Mr Sullivan copied in Mr Eathorne, Mr Walker and Mr Scutter. Mr Sullivan said:

It seems that [Mr Walker] and [Mr Scutter] are being left to not only deal with [Mr Henderson], but also the various secured parties he controls which assert claims against 3 of the named plaintiff companies. You can see that LPF refuse to pay any significant additional monies to the liquidators despite the substantially increased settlement sum.

Clearly it is [Mr Walker] and [Mr Scutter’s] decision on the settlement terms with LPF. I proffer no advice either way.

[318] Mr Sullivan’s only expressed concern was with the absence of additional money to deal with the Mr Henderson litigation factor.

[319] Mr Scutter responded to the email chain (Mr Eathorne, Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan) on 18 August 2017. He said his strong view was that they had no choice but to seek directions, because “there is a large storm coming with Henderson leading the charge”. Mr Scutter did not otherwise express concern with the proposed distribution terms that Mr Eathorne had reported from his meeting with Mr Woodhams.

[320] Later the same day, Mr Sullivan responded to the email chain, reporting that Mr Fowler’s “initial thoughts” included that there was a real need to deal with Mr Henderson after the settlement, that the additional funds to deal with this were

“only a small amount for SPF to concede”, and that if there were no funds available to deal with Mr Henderson, he recommended a directions application.

[321] Also on 18 August 2017, Kim Francis of Meredith Connell (who by then were the solicitors for the plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings) sent an email to Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker. Mr Francis referred to issues arising from potential challenges by entities related to Mr Henderson to the proposed distribution of settlement proceeds. He noted that one option for dealing with those issues was to seek the approval of the court through an application for directions, but that he understood that Mr Smith KC had spoken to Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker about this “and it is not the preferred option”. Mr Walker responded, copying Mr Scutter, that he was “not averse to getting directions”. Mr Scutter replied saying “I think we should proceed on that basis then.” Mr Woodhams responded that the concern with seeking directions was that Mr Henderson would have the right to attend court, and SPF’s preference was to have the funds distributed before any possible challenge.

[322] I pause to note that the parties at different times contemplated two distinct applications for directions. The liquidators’ team discussed (and sometimes threatened SPF with) the possibility of an application for directions to resolve the distribution dispute (in particular, as to whether there was an Allied security agreement). By contrast, Mr Francis was proposing a narrower application for directions: an application seeking approval for whatever distribution was agreed between SPF and the liquidators, with the object of being able to rely on the immunity in s 284(3) of the Companies Act to defend any claim by Mr Henderson. Plainly, it is this narrower form of directions application that Mr Walker said he was “not averse to” and which was the basis on which Mr Scutter said he thought they should proceed.

[323] I also find that Mr Fowler’s recommendation, as conveyed by Mr Sullivan on 18 August 2017, was to make a directions application of this narrower form. Mr Fowler’s recommendation was tied to the lack of funds to deal with the litigation risk presented by Mr Henderson. In cross-examination, Mr Sullivan confirmed that it was in the context of the absence of a “fighting fund” to deal with the threat of litigation from Mr Henderson that Mr Fowler had recommended a directions application.

The signing of the Distribution Agreement and Payment Direction on 19 August 2017

[324] Mr Woodhams then, either on the afternoon of 18 August 2017 or the next morning (a Saturday), had one or more conversations with Mr Walker, during which Mr Walker agreed to the essential terms that Mr Eathorne had set out in his email. On 19 August 2017 at 10.34 am, Mr Woodhams emailed Mr Walker. He said:

As discussed, attached is the agreement between SPF and the Liquidators as to the disbursement of the funds and the sharing of the proceeds.

As agreed, the $33.8 [million] will be disbursed immediately $5.38m to PVL and the balance to SPF.

Can you please print out and return signed.

[325] Attached to the email was an unsigned Distribution Agreement and an unsigned Payment Direction to Meredith Connell. The Distribution Agreement had a signing block for Mr Newland to sign for SPF, a signing block in which each of Mr Walker and Mr Scutter would sign on their own behalf as liquidator, and a series of signing blocks for Mr Walker to sign as liquidator on behalf of each PVL company. Mr Woodhams asked that the documents be signed and returned. A few minutes later, Mr Walker forwarded the email to Mr Scutter.

[326] Mr Walker then phoned Mr Scutter and asked him to sign the Distribution Agreement urgently. Mr Scutter refused to sign as his preference was to apply for directions. This annoyed Mr Walker. He reported it to Mr Woodhams, who then called Mr Scutter and asked him to sign the Distribution Agreement that day. Mr Scutter refused.

[327] Mr Woodhams then changed the Distribution Agreement by changing the signing block in which Mr Walker and Mr Scutter were each to sign on their own behalf as liquidator. He simply removed the signature line for Mr Scutter. He sent that version of the Distribution Agreement to Mr Walker, who signed it (and the Payment Direction). Mr Walker understood that he was entitled to do so.

[328] Mr Walker must have signed the Distribution Agreement by about midday on 19 August 2017, because at 12.06 pm Nick Williams (of Meredith Connell) sent an email to Mr Scutter. The email attached a Word version of the proposed settlement

deed with PwC. Mr Williams also said: “[Mr Woodhams] is having [Mr Newland] sign the doc that [Mr Walker] has signed and once through to me I will send to you (or [Mr Woodhams] will).”

[329] There is no evidence that Mr Scutter asked Mr Williams what document Mr Walker had signed. I infer that Mr Scutter knew from this email, if not before, that Mr Walker had signed the Distribution Agreement.

[330] At 1.14 pm, Mr Walker emailed a copy of the signature page of the Distribution Agreement to Mr Scutter (and Mr Woodhams), saying: “John — I have signed, on behalf of us both, the distribution agreement as attached (or below?) as discussed.” The signature page showed that it had yet to be signed on behalf of SPF.

[331] At 1.15 pm, Mr Scutter forwarded Mr Walker’s email to Mr Sullivan, simply saying “FYI”. At 1.29 pm, Mr Scutter forwarded Mr Williams’ email to Mr Sullivan. A few minutes later, he asked Mr Sullivan for any high-level comments on the proposed settlement deed with PwC. Mr Sullivan responded at 1.59 pm, noting that the deed had an indemnity in favour of PwC and saying:

... (but those monies will need to be paid by LPF which I would hope is covered by the distribution agreement). I think you should ask Robert for a copy of that asap.

[332] At 3.53 pm, Mr Woodhams emailed the Distribution Agreement, signed by Mr Newland for SPF, and the Payment Direction, signed by himself for SPF, to Mr Williams, copying Mr Walker and Mr Scutter. Mr Scutter almost immediately forwarded the email to Mr Sullivan, merely saying “FYI”. Mr Sullivan responded that that was “the document I thought he may have signed” and noted the need to render his and Mr Scutter’s bills before settlement with PwC.

[333] At no time on 19 August 2017 did Mr Scutter or Mr Sullivan, after becoming aware that Mr Walker had signed the Distribution Agreement without Mr Scutter, express any concern about that.

The amended Payment Direction

[334] Clause 2.4 of the PwC settlement agreement obliged LPF to hold a retention of $2 million pending resolution of the claims against the defendant directors. In anticipation of that provision, on 25 August 2017 Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker agreed that PVL would contribute $400,000 to the $2 million retention. To reflect this, they agreed to amend the Payment Direction so that an extra $400,000 would be paid to SPF and commensurately less would be paid to PVL. They also agreed that the bulk of the funds payable to PVL would be paid to a bank account in Mr Walker’s name. That bank account was Mr Walker’s practice account. Meredith Connell distributed PwC’s $33,800,000 settlement payment in accordance with the amended Payment Direction.

The terms of the Distribution Agreement

[335] The Distribution Agreement began by describing three sets of parties:

(a) SPF.

(b) Mr Walker and Mr Scutter as liquidators of PVL and various PVL subsidiaries. The Agreement mostly used the shorthand “Liquidators” when referring to these parties, though sometimes “Liquidator” was used.

(c) PVL and various PVL subsidiaries. The Agreement used the shorthand “Plaintiffs” when referring to these parties.

[336] While the Distribution Agreement described Mr Scutter as a party, he was not a party, because he never agreed to it. He told Mr Walker and Mr Woodhams that he refused to sign the Distribution Agreement. He did not give Mr Walker authority to sign on his behalf. The Agreement, as presented to Mr Scutter, had a signing block in which each of Mr Walker and Mr Scutter would (as the “Liquidators”) sign on their own behalf. That signing block was altered merely by removing Mr Scutter’s signature line. It was not altered to show Mr Walker as signing on behalf of Mr Scutter.

[337] That Mr Scutter was not a party was of little moment to the operation of the Distribution Agreement, as the key operative provisions mostly involved only SPF and PVL. But it provides context to the plaintiffs’ claim that Mr Scutter’s refusal to sign the Agreement somehow impugns Mr Walker’s decision to enter into it.

[338] Returning to the terms of the Distribution Agreement, the background section said the parties wished to record an agreement reached in respect of the distribution of the “Resolution Sum”, defined to include “all proceeds received by the Plaintiffs in respect of any claim to which they are parties”. There was then a reference to the PVL proceedings.

[339] The key operative clauses were cls 1.1 to 1.4. Clause 1.1 addressed the distribution of the expected settlement proceeds of $33.8 million from PwC (the PwC Proceeds). It said the parties agreed the following payments should be made in the following order from the PwC Proceeds:

(a) Payment of all outstanding Project Costs to SPF in accordance with the Services Deed, such Costs to include the purchase price of the Allied security and the DFG security (cl 1.1(a)).

(b) Payment of the Services Fee to SPF in accordance with the Services Deed (cl 1.1(b)).

(c) Payment of all other amounts (if any) payable to SPF in accordance with the Services Deed (cl 1.1(c)).

(d) From the remaining funds, the sum of $5,380,000 to PVL and the balance to SPF (cl 1.1(d)).

[340] The effect of this agreed cascade of payments was that the most PVL could receive from the PwC Proceeds was $5,380,000. At the same time as the Distribution Agreement was signed, SPF and PVL signed the Payment Direction, under which they irrevocably directed Meredith Connell to disburse $5,380,000 of the PwC Proceeds to PVL and the balance to SPF. And, in due course, $5,380,000 was the amount that was

paid to PVL (or at PVL’s direction) from the PwC Proceeds. In those circumstances it was unnecessary, in order to calculate PVL’s entitlement under cl 1.1, for there to be any calculation of the Project Costs, the Services Fee or other amounts payable to SPF under the Services Deed. I will return to this point.

[341] Clause 1.2 addressed the distribution of any settlement proceeds from the defendant directors. The parties agreed that the following payments should be made:

(a) The first $2 million was to be allocated and paid to PVL as to 10 per cent and SPF as to 90 per cent.

(b) From any monies in excess of $2 million, 50 per cent was to be paid to PVL and 50 per cent to SPF.

[342] Clause 1.3 said:

For the avoidance of any doubt, all payments made to or on behalf of SPF in accordance with:

(a) paragraphs 1.1(a)–(c) (as the case may be) are made in accordance with the Services Deed and in reliance by SPF on the security created under the Services Deed;

(b) paragraphs 1.1(d) or 1.2 (as the case may be) are made pursuant to and in enforcement by SPF of the Allied Debts and the Dominion Debts.

[343] I return to the effect of cl 1.3 much later in this judgment.

[344] Clause 1.4 provided that following the distribution of the Resolution Sum in accordance with cls 1.1 and 1.2, and subject to some provisos that I need not mention, “SPF shall assign to the Liquidators or their nominees all of its right and interest in the Allied Debts and the Dominion Debts.”

[345] “Allied Debts” and “Dominion Debts” were defined in the Distribution Agreement to include the Allied security and the DFG security. Thus, as part of the resolution reached in the Agreement, SPF was agreeing to assign those debts and securities to the liquidators. In due course, on 17 August 2018, Mr Walker settled the PVG Securities Trust for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of PVL. The trustee

was PVG Securities Trustee Ltd (PVG Securities). The same day, SPF assigned the Allied and DFG debts and securities to PVG Securities as Mr Walker’s nominee. By that time, Mr Walker was the sole liquidator of PVL (Mr Scutter having been removed in March 2018).

[346] The Distribution Agreement also contained several machinery and other provisions of note. Clause 2.2 provided:

All calculations of the sums payable in accordance with paragraph 1.1 shall be made by SPF and provided to the Liquidator for review prior to being finalised by SPF. The Liquidator shall advise SPF of any errors in the calculations within 3 days of being provided the draft calculations. Notwithstanding the Liquidator’s right of review, SPF's calculations shall be final and binding on the parties.

[347] As explained above, the parties contemporaneously instructed Meredith Connell that PVL would be paid $5,380,000, its maximum entitlement under cl 1.1, with the balance to SPF. That was the only calculation required by cl 2.2. In those circumstances, cl 2.2 did not require SPF to provide the liquidators with calculations of matters such as Project Costs or the Services Fee.

[348] Clause 4.1 was a full and final settlement clause. Relevantly, it provided:

SPF, the Liquidators and the Plaintiffs agree that ... this agreement is in full and final settlement of all claims that SPF (on the one hand) and the Liquidators and the Plaintiffs (on the other hand) may have against each other in connection with the matters dealt with in this agreement and each of them irrevocably and unconditionally waives all such claims.

[349] The “matters dealt with” in the Distribution Agreement included the parties’ respective entitlements to payments from any settlement proceeds from PwC or the defendant directors. One effect of cl 4.1 was that no party could make further claims to payments from those proceeds.

[350] On the other hand, cl 5.3 provided that nothing in the Distribution Agreement should affect the Services Deed, and to the extent there was any inconsistency between the two, the terms of the Services Deed should prevail. One consequence of cl 5.3 was that SPF remained obliged to fund the PVL proceedings by advancing Project Costs (both in respect of the claim against PwC, which was not quite settled, and in

respect of the claims against the directors, which were some way from being settled). Yet, because of the full and final settlement in cl 4.1, SPF had no entitlement to be repaid those Project Costs or to be paid any further Services Fee in respect of them. This was to PVL’s benefit and exposed SPF to risk, as is evident from SPF seeking to renegotiate, in mid-December 2017, the terms on which it would continue to fund the claims against the directors (when it looked as if those claims might go to trial). It did, however, remain the case that under cl 11.1 of the Services Deed SPF could, on short notice and without cause, terminate its obligations under the Deed and thereby escape liability to advance further Project Costs.

The compromise reached by the Distribution Agreement

[351] Underlying many of the plaintiffs’ allegations about the Distribution Agreement is a contention that it produced an outcome heavily in favour of SPF. It will therefore be useful to compare the outcome under the Agreement with the outcome that would have obtained under the positions the parties were adopting at the time. Those positions reflected two disputes. The main one was whether there was an Allied security agreement. The other was whether the payments to Allied and DFG should be classified as Project Costs. That dispute had a significant effect on the amount of the Services Fee, as that was likely to be calculated as two times Project Costs.

[352] In making this comparison it is necessary to use some information that was not known at the time of the Distribution Agreement: the final Project Costs (some of which were incurred after the Agreement, particularly on the claims against the directors) and the amount of the settlement with the directors. Using that information does not detract from the usefulness of the comparison, as when settling the parties had a shared expectation as to the likely sum of any settlement with the directors (which proved largely accurate) and would have been able to make a rough assessment of likely future Project Costs.

[353] In making the comparison, however, I have not taken account of the disputes raised by the plaintiffs in these proceedings as to SPF’s entitlements but which were not part of the positions taken by the liquidators at the time. This is because the aim

of the comparison is to see how the outcome under the Distribution Agreement compares with the parties’ positions at the time. I have in this analysis therefore put to one side, for example, the plaintiffs’ argument that Project Costs do not include the security for costs posted by SPF merely by way of a bond.

[354] SPF’s position was that the payments to Allied and DFG were Project Costs and there was no Allied security agreement but that it was bound by the undertaking given in the Supreme Court. That undertaking was to pay to PVL 10 per cent of the balance of the settlement proceeds after payment to SPF of its entitlements under the Services Deed. On that position:

(a) Settlement proceeds were $38,980,286.

(b) Project Costs were $12,125,145 and the Services Fee was thus

$24,250,290, a total of $36,375,435. From this, SPF accepted that there had to be a $2,670,000 deduction for a refund of security for costs. SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed would therefore be

$33,705,435.

(c) The balance of the settlement proceeds after SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed was $5,274,851. This was to be paid 90 per cent to SPF ($4,747,366) and 10 per cent to PVL ($527,485).

(d) Total distributions would therefore be $38,452,801 to SPF and

$527,485 to PVL.

[355] On the liquidators’ position, by contrast:

(a) Settlement proceeds were $38,980,286.

(b) Project Costs were $10,034,008 and the Services Fee was thus

$20,068,016, a total of $30,102,024. Deducting $2,670,000 for the refund of security for costs, SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed would therefore be $27,432,024.

(c) The balance of the settlement proceeds after SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed was $11,548,262. This was to be paid to PVL.

(d) Total distributions would therefore be $27,432,024 to SPF and

$11,548,262 to PVL.

[356] Under the Distribution Agreement, the actual payment to PVL was $6,080,000. This was in the middle of the two parties’ positions.

Was Mr Walker under a duty to obtain court approval for the Distribution Agreement or for payments under it?

[357] Mr Moss addressed this issue on behalf of the plaintiffs. He submitted that the combination of the following two factors created a duty on Mr Walker to obtain court approval before entering into the Distribution Agreement:

(a) To create a contractual right for SPF to take preference under cl 1(1) of sch 7 to the Companies Act, Mr Walker and Mr Scutter were required to ensure that the payments made to SPF were properly and reasonably incurred.

(b) A dispute had arisen between the liquidators and SPF, and then between the liquidators themselves, as to SPF’s entitlements under the Services Deed, including the Allied security agreement.

[358] In relation to the first factor, Mr Moss referred me to the Court of Appeal’s statements in Commercial Factors Ltd v Meltzer:28

[52] ... a liquidator may agree that monies owing under a contract entered into by the liquidator as agent of the company in liquidation will be a liquidator’s liability. In those circumstances the liquidator is, in effect, agreeing to treat any amount that becomes owing to the contracting party as an expense of the liquidation, and therefore claimable by the liquidator as a first priority payment pursuant to cl 1(1)(a) of sch 7 of the Companies Act 1993. That is as part of “the Fees and Expenses properly incurred by the liquidator in carrying out the duties and exercising the powers of the liquidator”. If issues arise, the High Court will determine whether the particular fees and expenses have been “properly incurred”. The liquidator is

28 Commercial Factors Ltd v Meltzer [2018] NZCA 505, [2019] 2 NZLR 484 (footnotes omitted).

an officer of the court and explicitly subject to the court’s jurisdiction pursuant to s 284 of the Companies Act.

[53] Were the court, in its supervisory jurisdiction, to conclude that particular expenses had not been properly incurred, and therefore — contrary to the contract made by the liquidator — not an expense of the liquidation, then the court may order the liquidator to repay the expenses pursuant to s 284 of the Companies Act.

[359] I do not accept that the first factor supports the imposition of a duty on Mr Walker to obtain court approval for the Distribution Agreement. Schedule 7 lists, in order of priority, the expenses, costs and other claims that, by virtue of s 312, enjoy preferential status over the claims of other unsecured creditors. The first priority group, described in cl 1(1)(a) of sch 7, includes “fees and expenses properly incurred by the liquidator in carrying out the duties and exercising the powers of the liquidator”. Mr Moss is correct that if such fees and expenses are not properly incurred, they will fall outside the priority conferred by cl 1(1)(a). It is also true, as the Court of Appeal said in Commercial Factors, that if an issue arises as to whether a fee or expense was “properly incurred”, the High Court can determine the dispute. But it does not follow from either point that a liquidator is under a duty to obtain approval from the Court before entering into a contract in which fees and expenses will be incurred.

[360] As to the second factor, Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker was “essentially being forced”, whether by undue influence or otherwise, to agree to a significant compromise to “the position agreed at the time of time of entry into the [Services Deed]”, namely under the Allied security agreement. As I explain below, I do not accept that Mr Walker entered into the Distribution Agreement by undue influence — far from it. Mr Walker was well advised on the distribution dispute, was in a good negotiating position, and applied negotiating pressure on SPF.

[361] I accept that the Distribution Agreement involved compromise. That was its essential purpose. But, contrary to how the plaintiffs characterise it, the compromise did not involve Mr Walker giving up known contractual rights under the Allied security agreement. Whether there was a binding Allied security agreement was in dispute. Neither side could be sure that there was or was not a binding agreement.

[362] It is more accurate to say that the compromise reached in the Distribution Agreement was one between the parties’ respective positions. It involved give and take on both sides. Determining whether to agree to that compromise was a commercial decision for Mr Walker as liquidator (I deal with Mr Scutter’s refusal to sign the Agreement in a moment). Mr Walker could have applied to the court for a direction approving the compromise. It is far from clear that a court would have been prepared to give approval (given that, as I said earlier, the courts will rarely give a direction if a purely commercial decision is in issue). But Mr Walker was certainly not under a duty to obtain approval.

[363] Mr Moss also placed weight on Mr Walker having been advised to seek directions, placing particular reliance on advice given to Mr Walker by Mr Fowler on 7 and 18 August 2017, near the end of the negotiations. I first note (to put this advice in context) that Mr Walker was well aware of the possibility of seeking directions and that this would not be looked on favourably by SPF (because of the likelihood it would prejudice the prospects of settlement with PwC). Mr Walker raised this possibility with SPF to put negotiating pressure on them.

[364] Mr Fowler’s advice on 7 August 2017 was that the liquidators had no choice but to seek directions. However, this advice was in response to SPF having reverted to its original negotiating position (namely, that expressed in its undertaking in the Supreme Court). The final Distribution Agreement was quite different.

[365] On 18 August 2017, after Mr Eathorne had reported that Mr Woodhams would not agree to terms any better than those ultimately found in the Distribution Agreement, Mr Sullivan conveyed to Mr Walker and Mr Scutter that if a fund was not made available to fight Mr Henderson, Mr Fowler “recommends a directions application”. As noted earlier, this was advice that the liquidators apply for directions seeking approval for whatever distribution was agreed, with the object of being able to rely on an immunity to defend any claim by Mr Henderson. It was not advice that they seek directions on the distribution dispute. Whether to require a fighting fund was a matter of commercial judgment. Mr Fowler’s recommendation was conveyed in the same email chain in which Mr Sullivan had (quite correctly) stated: “Clearly it

is [Mr Walker] and [Mr Scutter’s] decision on the settlement terms with LPF. I proffer no advice either way.”

[366] Finally, Mr Moss said Mr Scutter’s refusal to sign the Distribution Agreement without obtaining directions was a reason that Mr Walker had a duty to obtain court approval. I do not accept this. It is important to keep in mind the two capacities in which Mr Walker signed the Agreement. First, he was signing on his own behalf. He was of course entitled to do so. Secondly, he was signing on behalf of PVL and the various PVL subsidiaries. As to the subsidiaries, Mr Walker was their sole liquidator, so he was entitled to sign for them. As to PVL, Mr Scutter was also a liquidator. Section 242 of the Companies Act provides that two or more liquidators must act jointly, unless the order of the court appointing the liquidators states that the liquidators may exercise their powers individually. The order appointing Mr Scutter as liquidator of PVL did state that. Mr Walker was therefore able to enter into and sign the Agreement on PVL’s behalf.

[367] Not only was Mr Walker entitled to sign the Distribution Agreement notwithstanding Mr Scutter’s refusal to do so, I consider it was reasonable for him to do so in the circumstances. Mr Scutter accepted he had been appointed as liquidator of PVL primarily in case Mr Walker became incapacitated. He played very much a secondary role to Mr Walker. I accept he played a more active role in the distribution dispute. But on the key issue of that dispute — whether there was an Allied security agreement — he did not have the knowledge that Mr Walker had acquired from having dealt with SPF from the outset. In that respect, it is telling that Mr Walker’s assessment of the strengths of the liquidators’ position on the dispute (illustrated, for example, in him softening the draft email from Mr Scutter in mid-February 2017) was more realistic than that of others on his team. It is also telling that Mr Scutter did not, once Mr Walker had signed the Agreement, raise any concerns about the substance of the Agreement.

[368] Mr Moss placed weight on the expert evidence of Michael Whale. Mr Whale said he understood that Mr Fowler and Mr Sullivan had given firm advice that the liquidators should apply for directions on how the settlement proceeds should be allocated and that Mr Scutter refused to sign the Distribution Agreement. He opined

that in those circumstances, a reasonable liquidator would have applied for directions as to the distribution of the settlement proceeds. In cross-examination, however, he quite appropriately accepted that if the advice from Mr Fowler and Mr Sullivan was not as he understood it to be, the basis of his opinion would not hold. The advice from Mr Fowler and Mr Sullivan was, as I have explained, more nuanced than Mr Whale understood it to be. His opinion does not therefore affect my assessment.

Was Mr Walker under a duty to ensure the terms of the Services Deed were complied with when entering into the Distribution Agreement?

[369] To recap, the 100 Parties plead, in their fifth cause of action, that Mr Walker failed to ensure the terms of the Services Deed were complied with when entering into the Distribution Agreement. The Whitley Parties plead that the Agreement is not binding for several reasons, including that it was contrary to the Services Deed. The Whitley Parties also plead that a number of payments under the Agreement were unreasonable expenses in the liquidations because they were not valid Project Costs under the Services Deed. Implicit in these pleadings is the allegation that Mr Walker was under a duty to ensure the terms of the Services Deed were complied with when entering into the Agreement.

[370] I do not accept that Mr Walker had such a duty. A dispute had arisen, both as to whether certain costs were Project Costs under the Services Deed and as to whether there was an associated Allied security agreement. Mr Walker was entitled to compromise that dispute. A compromise of a dispute over an antecedent agreement, by its nature, will usually not “comply” with the agreement, particularly where the dispute also involves an alleged associated agreement. Mr Walker was therefore not required to ensure the compromise complied with the terms of the Services Deed.

Did Mr Walker breach any fiduciary duty by entering into the Distribution Agreement?

[371] Mr Walker was, as liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries, a fiduciary as regards those companies.29 This did not, however, mean that every duty he owed to

29 Dunphy v Sleepyhead Manufacturing Co Ltd [2007] NZCA 241, [2007] 3 NZLR 602 at [22]. See also Andrew R Keay McPherson & Keay: The Law of Company Liquidation (5th ed, Sweet & Maxwell, London, 2021) at [8-049].

those companies as liquidator was a fiduciary duty. As Ipp J observed in Permanent Building Society (in liq) v Wheeler:30

It is essential to bear in mind that the existence of a fiduciary relationship does not mean that every duty owed by a fiduciary to the beneficiary is a fiduciary duty.

[372] Mr Moss delivered the primary submissions for both sets of plaintiffs on this topic. He submitted that Mr Walker breached three duties that were fiduciary in nature. He said those duties were:

(a) the duty to act in good faith;

(b) the duty to act in the best interests of the PVL companies and their creditors; and

(c) the duty to act impartially between the various groups whose interests he was required to advance or consider.

[373] The first two asserted duties are not separate duties. There is but one relevant duty, and it is (as Mr Moss did acknowledge) a subjective duty. The duty is to act in good faith in what the liquidator believes to be the best interests of the company (whose interests will be represented by the interests of the unsecured creditors and, in the event of a surplus, the shareholders).31 It is analogous to the equivalent duty imposed on directors of a company.32 Although there is some debate as to whether this duty is truly a fiduciary duty, I proceed on the basis that it is.33

30 Permanent Building Society (in liq) v Wheeler (1994) 11 WAR 187 (SC) at 237. Ipp J’s observation was endorsed by Millett LJ in Bristol and West Building Society v Mothew [1998] Ch 1 (CA) at 17. See also Chirnside v Fay [2006] NZSC 68, [2007] 1 NZLR 433 at [15] per Elias CJ and [72] per Tipping J.

31 Heath and Whale, above n 22, at [22.3]. See also Mason v Lewis CIV-2010-404-8, 21 December 2010 at [27]–[29].

32 Keay, above n 29, at [8-049].

33 See Peter Watts Directors’ Powers and Duties (3rd ed, LexisNexis, Wellington, 2022) at [6.2] (addressing the equivalent duty imposed on directors).

[374] I accept that a liquidator has a duty to act impartially as between those who are interested in the property of the company in liquidation.34 This is also generally regarded as a fiduciary duty.35 Again, I proceed on that basis.

[375] As to whether these duties were breached, it will be convenient to start with the alleged breach of the duty to act impartially. A recurrent theme in the plaintiffs’ case was that Mr Walker preferred his own interest as an unsecured but preferred creditor (for his fees) over the interests of other unsecured creditors. I have already touched upon this theme when rejecting the 100 Parties’ contention that Mr Walker breached a fiduciary or other duty when entering into the Services Deed. In relation to the Distribution Agreement, the theme manifested itself in Mr Moss’s submission that Mr Walker “[put] himself ahead of the creditors” by entering into that agreement.

[376] The short answer to Mr Moss’s submission is that Mr Walker was put ahead of the other unsecured creditors by reason of the preference for a liquidator’s fees and expenses in s 312(1) and cl 1(1)(a) of sch 7 of the Companies Act, not by reason of his entry into the Distribution Agreement. The partiality towards Mr Walker was a legislative one, not one that he created by his own act.

[377] Mr Moss also submitted that Mr Walker favoured SPF’s interests over the interests of unsecured creditors because the Distribution Agreement meant that SPF would receive payment of the Services Fee earlier than it otherwise would have. It is correct that the Agreement accelerated the distribution to SPF, as the Services Deed provided that if there was a partial settlement (such as the settlement with PwC), payment of the Services Fee would await final resolution of the PVL proceedings. But this does not mean that Mr Walker was preferring SPF over unsecured creditors. Mr Walker was compromising a dispute, and the compromise involved give and take between the two parties.

[378] I turn now to the allegations that when entering into the Distribution Agreement Mr Walker breached his duty to act in good faith in what he believed to be

34 Keay, above n 29, at [8-049].

35 Heath and Whale, above n 22, at [22.3]; and Keay, above n 29, at [8-049] and [8-056].

the best interests of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker breached this duty in five ways.

[379] First, Mr Moss said Mr Walker failed to properly assess what SPF was claiming as Project Costs (and therefore the Services Fee) and to seek legal advice to ensure all those costs were claimable pursuant to the Services Deed. I do not accept this. Mr Walker knew that there were two key aspects to the dispute: whether the payments to Allied and DFG were Project Costs and whether there was an Allied security agreement. He received advice on both aspects. A compromise that included the second aspect meant there was no point in “ensur[ing]” that every claimed cost was payable under the Services Deed.

[380] Secondly, Mr Moss said Mr Walker should have recognised that it was not in the companies’ (or their creditors’) best interests to enter into an agreement regarding the distribution of the settlement proceeds until the entire PVL litigation had been settled. Mr Moss put this point on an objective basis. The test is subjective. I am satisfied that Mr Walker, in good faith, considered it to be in the companies’ interests to enter into the Distribution Agreement before settlements had been reached with PwC and the directors. Mr Walker knew that he had some negotiating leverage over SPF that he was going to lose once there were settlements (particularly a settlement with PwC). Prior to any settlement, Mr Walker could threaten to withdraw instructions to PVL’s solicitors, to withdraw SPF’s authority to negotiate or to seek directions. Mr Walker employed these threats in his negotiations with SPF.

[381] Thirdly, Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker failed to advise the Supreme Court of the liquidators’ position on the Allied security agreement and of the change in circumstances following the settlement with PwC and the entry into the Distribution Agreement. This submission reflected the Whitley Parties’ pleading that Mr Walker misled or failed to advise the Supreme Court of the correct position in relation to the Allied security agreement and as to the distributions made following the Agreement.

[382] The short answer to this submission and pleading is that Mr Walker acted on the advice of solicitors and Mr Smith KC as to how to present these matters to the Supreme Court. In those circumstances, there is no basis for finding that Mr Walker

failed to act in good faith in what he (subjectively) believed to be the best interests of the companies. It is also difficult to understand how this pleading could impugn Mr Walker’s entry into the Distribution Agreement.

[383] Given the seriousness of the allegation that the Supreme Court was misled, I consider a slightly longer answer is appropriate. The first aspect of Mr Moss’s submission was that the Supreme Court was led to believe that what Mr Moss called “true” unsecured creditors (namely, unsecured creditors other than the liquidators) would receive something if there were proceeds after the payments to SPF in accordance with its undertaking. I do not accept that. In Mr Walker’s affidavit to the Supreme Court, he referred to SPF’s undertaking and said:

The undertaking is to provide a minimum percentage of funds to me as liquidator of the plaintiff companies. These funds are for the unsecured creditors (including the claim of Mr Scutter and I as liquidators).

[384] The second aspect of Mr Moss’s submission was that when Mr Walker advised the Supreme Court in September 2017 of the settlement with PwC, he failed to advise it that “true” unsecured creditors would not receive the minimum entitlement to which they were entitled under SPF’s undertaking. Again, that submission is based on the incorrect premise that Mr Walker had represented to the Court that the unsecured creditors did not include the liquidators. He had represented the opposite.

[385] Fourthly, Mr Moss said that although the good faith duty is generally a subjective one, the Supreme Court had, in Madsen-Ries (as liquidators of Debut Homes Ltd (in liq)) v Cooper, identified a number of exceptions and qualifications to that position (when dealing with the equivalent directors’ duty), including “where there is no evidence of actual consideration of the best interests of the company”.36 In fact, the Supreme Court said that was not an exception or qualification at all, explaining:

[114] ... The point is that directors cannot subjectively believe they are acting in the best interests of the company if they have failed to consider the interests of the company or, where required, the interests of all of the creditors, including prospective creditors.

  1. Madsen-Ries (as liquidators of Debut Homes Ltd (in liq)) v Cooper [2020] NZSC 100, [2021] 1 NZLR 43 at [113(a)].

[386] In this respect, Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker made no effort whatsoever to consider what the best interests of the PVL companies and their creditors were in relation to entry into the Distribution Agreement. He said that Mr Walker did not suggest in his evidence that he believed that entering into the Agreement was in any way done for the general benefit of the companies and their creditors “other than himself, whom he was undoubtedly preferring”.

[387] I reject this submission. In relation to the distribution dispute with SPF, the interest of the PVL companies (represented by the interests of their creditors) was to maximise their return from the PVL proceedings. That involved, first, maximising the recoveries from PwC and the directors and, secondly, maximising the distributions of the proceeds to the PVL companies. These are not difficult objectives to understand, particularly for a liquidator. The first aspect (maximising recoveries) involved years of work by Mr Walker and his team. Unsurprisingly, the plaintiffs make no complaint about that work or the result of it. They would have it that Mr Walker, having spent years trying to maximise the recoveries and knowing that this was in the interests of the PVL companies, then failed to consider that it was also in the interests of the PVL companies to maximise the distributions of the proceeds to those companies. I am satisfied that Mr Walker did consider exactly that. After the distribution dispute arose in late 2016, Mr Walker engaged the services of Mr Sullivan and then Mr Fowler. Mr Scutter’s evidence was that Mr Walker then thought actively and tactically about how to communicate with SPF on the dispute. This is reflected in the documentary evidence, which shows that Mr Walker employed negotiating tactics against SPF. The result was a compromise far more beneficial to PVL than SPF’s opening position.

[388] Finally, Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker breached his duty of good faith by entering into the Distribution Agreement without the consent of Mr Scutter and in “complete contradiction to the legal advice he had received from Mr Fowler that both parties were required to sign” the Agreement. I have largely addressed the absence of Mr Scutter’s consent already. Mr Walker subjectively believed he was entitled to sign the Agreement. Although it is not strictly relevant (since the good faith duty is subjective), I have found that Mr Walker’s belief was both correct and reasonable. As to Mr Fowler’s advice, this was conveyed by Mr Sullivan in an email sent on the

morning of 18 August 2017. Mr Sullivan said Mr Fowler had asked him to convey his “initial thoughts”, which included:

He is very conscious that any settlement needs to be signed by both [Mr Scutter] and [Mr Walker]. Equally any resolution with SPF should be agreed by both and in writing.

[389] This advice was not definitive. It was conveyed as “initial thoughts”. It said that any settlement (with PwC) “needs to be” signed by both liquidators, whereas the resolution with SPF “should be” agreed by both. Further, there is no evidence that Mr Fowler had been made aware of the terms on which the liquidators had been appointed, which entitled the liquidators to exercise their powers individually. That Mr Walker acted inconsistently with Mr Fowler’s initial thoughts does not show a breach of his duty of good faith.

[390] That deals with the fiduciary duties that Mr Moss submitted had been breached. In addition, Mr Barker submitted that Mr Walker breached a fiduciary duty that he owed to the 100 Parties as secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries. Later in this judgment I find that the 100 Parties have not proven that they are secured creditors. It is therefore not necessary for me to address Mr Barker’s submission.

[391] I conclude that Mr Walker did not breach any fiduciary duty by entering into the Distribution Agreement.

Did Mr Walker breach any statutory duty by entering into the Distribution Agreement?

[392] In closing, Mr Moss referred to a liquidator’s principal duty, in s 253 of the Companies Act, to realise the assets of the company and distribute the proceeds to its creditors and, if there is any surplus, to its shareholders. He said a liquidator also has a duty to act with skill and care. He submitted that Mr Walker had breached that duty.

[393] The Whitley Plaintiffs alleged many things (indeed, too many) in their fourth amended statement of claim, but they did not allege that Mr Walker breached a duty to act with skill and care. They are not entitled to advance the claim for the first time in closing. Moreover, the breaches of statutory duty advanced by Mr Moss were

essentially the same as those advanced in relation to Mr Walker’s alleged breach of his duty of good faith, all of which I have rejected.

[394] Mr Moss also submitted that a liquidator has a duty to have regard to the views of creditors and that Mr Walker failed to do so. This also was not pleaded and so cannot be advanced. Moreover, the duty arises only in the circumstances set out in s 258 of the Companies Act. There was no suggestion those circumstances had arisen here.

Did SPF and LPF unduly influence Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement?

[395] The Whitley Parties allege that LPF and SPF unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement and thus it is unconscionable that LPF and SPF retain the benefit of that Agreement. This pleading does not distinguish between LPF and SPF. It will suffice to refer to SPF.

[396] In order to succeed on a claim of undue influence, the claimant must prove that the alleged influence led to the making of the impugned transaction, and that the influence was undue in the sense that the transaction was not the result of the free exercise of an independent will by the counterparty. A claimant can establish undue influence either by proving actual undue influence or by recourse to an evidential presumption of undue influence.37 In closing, Mr Moss confirmed that the Whitley Parties were seeking to establish actual rather than presumed undue influence.

[397] Mr Moss submitted that SPF influenced Mr Walker “by applying substantial pressure on him to enter the Distribution Agreement”. He emphasised two aspects of the way SPF applied the pressure. First, he said SPF would try to get Mr Walker to disregard the advice of his lawyers or Mr Scutter. He referred to Mr Woodhams’ emails to Mr Walker of 14 February 2017 (which complained of Mr Sullivan’s “interference”) and 20 February 2017 (which asserted that Mr Sullivan and Mr Scutter had made “baseless” allegations regarding SPF’s conduct), and to Mr Sheppard’s

37 Green v Green [2016] NZCA 486, [2017] 2 NZLR 321 at [35] and [48], approving the principles summarised by Winkelmann J in Green v Green [2015] NZHC 1218, (2015) 4 NZTR 25-017 at [100(c)–(d)].

email to Mr Walker of 20 July 2017 (which said Mr Walker was “hiding” behind his lawyer). Secondly, Mr Sullivan said SPF refused to abide the request not to deal directly with Mr Walker on the dispute and instead “continued to pepper him with phone calls”.

[398] In respect of the first aspect emphasised by Mr Moss:

(a) I accept that Mr Woodhams’ emails of 14 and 20 February 2017 were an attempt to get Mr Walker to disregard Mr Sullivan’s and Mr Scutter’s advice and views. This is clear from the face of the emails. But I do not accept that those attempts influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. He did not enter into the Agreement for a further six months. In the meantime, Mr Walker retained Mr Sullivan’s services and both Mr Sullivan and Mr Scutter regularly conveyed their views to Mr Walker. The compromise reached in the Agreement reflected those views (in that it was a considerable way from SPF’s undertaking in the Supreme Court), albeit not fully.

(b) I do not accept that Mr Sheppard’s email influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. As noted earlier, I accept Mr Walker’s evidence that he was not “bullied” by this email and did not care what Mr Sheppard said. This is consistent with the evidence of those who dealt with Mr Walker at the time (to which I will shortly refer), which is to the effect that Mr Walker was not one to be “bullied”. Also, Mr Sheppard’s email was sent a month before Mr Walker entered into the Distribution Agreement.

[399] As to the second aspect, Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams each contacted Mr Walker directly on the distribution dispute after there had been a request that communications on that matter be between Mr Bigio and Mr Fowler. Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams contacted Mr Walker directly several times in July 2017. However, that contact did not lead to Mr Walker entering into any agreement. To the contrary, on 3 August 2017 Mr Walker emailed Mr Woodhams telling him that Mr Bigio’s latest proposal was “wholly unacceptable”. A few days later, on 7 August 2017, he called

Mr Woodhams and expressed annoyance with SPF’s position and reminded Mr Woodhams that he (Mr Walker) and Mr Scutter were in control of the PVL proceedings. So, despite the direct contact by Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams, Mr Walker was still expressing himself forcefully and reminding SPF that he and Mr Scutter held some leverage. Further, Mr Walker was doing so in direct contact that he instigated with Mr Woodhams.

[400] Mr Woodhams also had one or more conversations directly with Mr Walker either on the afternoon of 18 August 2017 or the next morning. In those conversations, Mr Walker agreed to the essential terms of the Distribution Agreement that Mr Eathorne had set out in his email of 17 August 2017. Shortly thereafter, Mr Walker signed the Agreement. Obviously, there is an immediate connection in time between the conversations and Mr Walker entering into the Agreement. I am nonetheless not satisfied that Mr Woodhams’ direct contact with Mr Walker led to Mr Walker entering into the Agreement. The sequence of events is as follows:

(a) On 8 August 2017, Mr Walker recommended “the silent treatment”. This appears to have been employed until 17 August 2017. That day, Mr Sullivan sent an email recording Mr Walker’s proposal that Mr Eathorne and Mr Fowler meet with Mr Woodhams.

(b) Mr Eathorne met with Mr Woodhams later that day (I infer that Mr Fowler was unavailable) and sent a reporting email to Mr Walker, Mr Scutter, Mr Sullivan and Mr Fowler that evening. His email set out the most that Mr Woodhams proposed for distribution to PVL.

(c) Mr Walker responded promptly, saying “I think we agree to that” and that a fighting fund could be obtained in another way.

(d) Mr Sullivan then responded. The only concern he raised was the lack of a fighting fund.

(e) The next morning, 18 August 2017, Mr Scutter said he thought they had to seek directions to deal with the “large storm” led by Mr Henderson.

He did not otherwise express concern with the proposed distribution terms that Mr Eathorne had reported and to which Mr Walker thought they should agree.

(f) Later that morning, Mr Sullivan conveyed Mr Fowler’s recommendation that directions be sought because of the lack of a fighting fund (but not for any other reason).

(g) About an hour later, Mr Francis sent an email to Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker, noting the option of seeking directions from the court in order to be able to rely on an immunity and noting his understanding that this was “not the preferred option”. He asked Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker to send through “the anticipated potential distributions”. Mr Walker said merely that he was “not averse” to getting directions. Mr Scutter said he thought directions should be sought. At 12.28 pm, Mr Woodhams said his view was that directions should not be sought. He was the only one of the three to explain his view.

[401] Mr Walker’s response to Mr Eathorne’s email shows that Mr Walker was already agreeable to what Mr Woodhams had proposed to Mr Eathorne and that he did not think a fighting fund was necessary. Subsequent communications from Mr Sullivan, Mr Scutter and Mr Fowler addressed the lack of a fighting fund and whether there should therefore be a directions application but raised no other concern about the proposed distribution. So, from Mr Walker’s perspective the only outstanding issue was whether directions should be sought. In response to Mr Francis’s email, Mr Walker expressed his view on that issue in lukewarm terms and Mr Woodhams explained his view. In these circumstances, I am not satisfied that Mr Woodhams’ direct contact with Mr Walker later that afternoon and the next day led to Mr Walker entering into the Distribution Agreement.

[402] In conclusion, I reject the submission that the two aspects on which Mr Moss focussed influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement.

[403] As well as focussing on those two aspects, Mr Moss submitted more broadly that SPF applied “substantial pressure” on Mr Walker and that this led to him entering into the Distribution Agreement. It is difficult to disagree with a submission pitched in such broad terms. The negotiations leading to the Agreement went on for months. Some of SPF’s written communications on the dispute were worded forcefully. Mr Walker said that some of his discussions with Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams were “heated”. All this can be characterised as SPF applying “substantial pressure” on Mr Walker. I am satisfied that this pressure (among other things) led to Mr Walker entering into the Agreement. But this is a rather uninteresting conclusion. Substantial pressure is commonly found in commercial negotiations. And here, substantial pressure was exerted by both sides. The written communications from Mr Walker and his lawyers were forcefully expressed. Mr Walker said he was the “tempestuous party” in the discussions. Mr Walker threatened to withdraw negotiating authority or to seek directions. All this led to SPF entering into the Agreement.

[404] I now turn to consider whether any influence from SPF was undue, in the sense that the Distribution Agreement was not the result of the free exercise of an independent will on the part of Mr Walker. In addressing this issue I will assume that SPF’s influence that led to the Agreement was not only the “substantial pressure” it exerted but also the two specific aspects that I have rejected (in case I am wrong in that rejection).

[405] Mr Moss submitted that the influence was undue because SPF was a well-resourced funder that was able to exploit Mr Walker’s vulnerable state of mind arising from his mental and financial distress, in impairment of his free will. He said that SPF knew Mr Walker was mentally and financially unstable.

[406] I first clear one matter out of the way. Mr Walker’s evidence was that his depression started some time after the Distribution Agreement was entered into. That evidence was not challenged, and the plaintiffs did not adduce any expert evidence or any medical records from the time. In closing, the plaintiffs did not suggest that Mr Walker was suffering from any mental illness when entering into the Agreement. To be clear, I find he was not.

[407] I accept that Mr Walker was experiencing stress during the negotiations for the Distribution Agreement. That is hardly surprising. The negotiations occurred against the backdrop of Mr Walker (and others) being involved in briefing witnesses and otherwise preparing for a 12-week trial that was to commence in early 2018. By that time, Mr Walker had committed time and resources to the PVL proceedings and the PVL liquidations for five years.

[408] The plaintiffs emphasise some of the statements that Mr Walker made to his team or to Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams about the stress he was experiencing and the pressure he was being placed under by SPF. Having read numerous of Mr Walker’s emails (even more than the many I have quoted or referred to in this judgment), and having heard Mr Walker’s evidence over two days, I consider those communications need to be read in context and treated with care. Mr Walker often uses florid language. He resorts to parable. He is prone to exaggeration. For example:

(a) Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker had “buck[led]” following two phone calls from Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams on 3 July 2017, referring to Mr Walker’s statement in his email to his team on 5 July 2017: “I am drained of psychic resources”. It is hard to know what “psychic resources” would be needed in this context, but in any event it is clear Mr Walker was not writing literally — he immediately went on to say it was as if he had “won WWII only [to] have to fight the Russians”.

(b) Mr Moss relied on Mr Walker’s email to his team on 20 July 2017 that “I have been put under intolerable pressure and I have given in” and “I cannot fight them and they know that”. But Mr Walker had not actually given in and did continue to fight, as can be seen in his firm email to Mr Woodhams on 3 August 2017 that Mr Bigio’s draft agreement was “wholly unacceptable”.

(c) Mr Walker concluded that same email by saying “[t]he constant harassment to which I am subject is ... having an adverse impact on my health. You have been warned.” In evidence, Mr Walker said this was

“perhaps an exaggeration”. I am confident it was. Immediately after sending that email to Mr Woodhams, Mr Walker forwarded the email to Mr Scutter and Mr Sullivan. In so doing, he did not refer to harassment or his health, merely saying “[k]eep you off to try and defuse what has become tense again”. Mr Scutter responded a few minutes later. He did not express any concern.

[409] Mr Walker insisted in his evidence that his free will was not impaired. He said he understood the Distribution Agreement, received advice throughout and believed at the time (and still does) that the Agreement was a “reasonable compromise”. Under cross-examination, he said he did not think he was being bullied by SPF and that he was “not that easily bullied”.

[410] Mr Walker’s evidence is not determinative. But it is supported by the evidence of those who dealt with him at the time. Mr Sullivan said that Mr Walker would have periods were he was “strong and aggressive” and others where he was more passive and became reliant on others to take the lead. Mr Scutter said that he, Mr Walker and their legal team were thinking actively about their legal options and strategy and tactically about how to communicate with SPF on the distribution dispute. He said that Mr Walker was a “great” tactical thinker.

[411] Mr Smith KC dealt with Mr Walker over an extended period, including for several months after the Distribution Agreement was entered into. He described Mr Walker as clever, energetic in mind and insistent when he thought he was not being listened to. He said that Mr Walker looked physically unwell and at times behaved as if he was stressed. Under cross-examination, Mr Smith KC said he did not see the stress as remarkable. He said nobody told Mr Walker what to do or what to think (or, if they tried, they did not get very far) and that Mr Walker challenged and questioned advice regularly. Mr Smith KC concluded:

At no time that I recall did he compromise other than on a principled basis or allow himself to be directed as opposed to being advised. He never shrunk from an argument: quite, I may say, the contrary. I would describe him as one of the less biddable lay clients I have acted for.

[412] Then there is Mr Eathorne. As explained earlier, Mr Whitley examined Mr Eathorne under s 261(3)(c) of the Companies Act. Mr Whitley accepted in cross- examination that the impression one would get from listening to the three-hour examination would be “quite different to the depiction of Mr Walker advanced in [Mr Whitley’s] case”. The cross-examination continued:

Q. [The impression is] much more favourable, isn’t it?

A. Yes.

Q. It presents in a much more professional, [sic] doesn’t it?

A. Yes.

Q. It presents him as not being pushed over by [LPF], doesn’t it?

A. Yes.

...

  1. And the way Mr Eathorne talks about him we’d sort of like our staff to talk about us like that, wouldn’t we?

A. Absolutely.

  1. And the way he talks about LPF’s influence is at odds with Mr Walker being a pushover who was suborned, agree?

A. That’s Mr Eathorne’s view, yes.

[413] Mr Eathorne’s view is quite important. He worked more closely with Mr Walker than any of the witnesses. Mr Whitley accepted that Mr Eathorne was “almost uniquely placed” to inform the Court as to what had really happened.

[414] In summary, the evidence is very clear that Mr Walker entered into the Distribution Agreement through the free exercise of his own independent will. The plaintiffs have fallen well short of proving otherwise.

Summary

[415] I reject all the allegations that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary or other duties in entering into the Distribution Agreement. I reject the claim that SPF and LPF unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Agreement.

Issue 5: Was the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement, unlawful as an abuse of process?

[416] Mr Moss submitted that the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement, was unlawful because it amounted to an impermissible assignment of a bare cause of action and was therefore an abuse of process. He advanced this submission by comparing the position under the amended Deed with the position presented to the Supreme Court (including the undertaking by SPF) when it heard PwC’s appeal. Mr Moss submitted that if the Supreme Court had known of the amended Deed, it would have found it to be an abuse of process.

[417] Mr Moss submitted that on this ground — presented as additional or alternative to the many other challenges to the Distribution Agreement that I have just addressed

  • — the Agreement and Deed should be declared unlawful. Mr Moss sought that declaration either under s 284 of the Companies Act, the Court’s inherent jurisdiction or s 73 of the Contract and Commercial Law Act 2017.

[418] I have concerns as to whether this claim was properly pleaded. The Whitley Parties’ second cause of action makes many disparate allegations about the Distribution Agreement, including that it was unlawful because of the absence of court approval. But they did not clearly plead that the Services Deed as amended by the Agreement was unlawful as an abuse of process. They do plead that the Supreme Court would have found the Deed unlawful had the Court been told of the ultimate distribution of the settlement proceeds. But they do not plead that the Court would have done so on the ground of abuse of process.

[419] In any event, there is no merit to the claim. It proceeds on the assumption that the Distribution Agreement produced a worse outcome for the unsecured creditors than SPF’s undertaking. That assumption would be correct only if SPF’s undertaking was to provide a minimum return to unsecured creditors other than the liquidators. That was not how the undertaking was presented to the Supreme Court. As I have shown, on the (correct) basis that the liquidators were unsecured creditors, the Distribution Agreement produced a better outcome for unsecured creditors than SPF’s undertaking.

Issue 6: Are the 100 Parties secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries?

[420] The 100 Parties plead that each of them is a first ranking secured creditor of one or more PVL subsidiaries. They plead that their securities over the subsidiaries were obtained by assignment from the original creditor or an intermediate assignee. They plead that, as at 30 May 2019, the securities secured loan balances totalling more than $216 million.

[421] Mr Walker, LPF and SPF deny all these allegations. They therefore put the 100 Parties to proof, in particular as to the validity of the alleged assignments and the loan balances owing at the date of the alleged assignments. LPF also contended that if the 100 Parties had ever held securities in the PVL subsidiaries, they had subsequently surrendered those securities.

[422] It is necessary to deal with this issue by reference to each of the 100 Parties. All of the alleged debtor companies to which I refer are PVL subsidiaries (and are parties to each proceeding).

[423] The defendants ultimately did not dispute that any securities that were validly assigned extended (as a matter of interpretation) to the PVL subsidiaries’ causes of action in the PVL proceeding. Nor did they suggest that any securities were invalid by reason of non-registration on the PPSR. I therefore do not refer to those matters in the analysis that follows.

100 Investments

[424] 100 Investments claims to be a secured creditor of Lichfield Ventures Ltd (Lichfield), by way of an assignment from Propertyfinance Securities Ltd (Propertyfinance Securities). It pleads that:

(a) On 30 March 2005, Lichfield and Propertyfinance Securities entered into a GSA under which Lichfield granted to Propertyfinance Securities a security interest in all its property.

(b) On 30 March 2005, PVL (and other PVL companies) executed a deed of guarantee of Lichfield’s indebtedness in favour of Propertyfinance Securities.

(c) On 24 November 2006, Lichfield and Propertyfinance Securities entered into a loan agreement under which Propertyfinance Securities agreed to lend $2,662,500 to Lichfield.

(d) By a deed of assignment dated 27 January 2016, Propertyfinance Securities assigned to 100 Investments all its debt and security interests in Lichfield.

[425] Mr Henderson gave evidence of Lichfield granting the GSA and PVL granting the guarantee and produced the GSA and guarantee ((a) and (b) above). The defendants did not seriously challenge that those transactions had occurred. I find they did.

[426] Mr Henderson gave evidence of the 24 November 2006 loan agreement and produced it. The defendants had objected to admission of the agreement on the ground of hearsay. Mr Henderson gave evidence that he signed the document on behalf of Lichfield (and on behalf of PVL, a covenantor under the agreement). The defendants did not pursue the objection. The document is admissible, and I find the loan agreement was entered into.

[427] Mr Henderson did not give any evidence as to any loan having been advanced under the loan agreement. He instead addressed the loan balance at the date of the alleged assignment. I return to that below.

[428] The alleged deed of assignment from Propertyfinance Securities to 100 Investments was in issue. Mr Henderson did not sign this deed. The 100 Parties did not call the two signatories as witnesses. The defendants objected to the admission of the deed of assignment on the ground it was hearsay.

[429] Under s 17 of the Evidence Act 2006, a hearsay statement is generally not admissible. Section 4 of the Act defines a “hearsay statement” as one made by a person other than a witness which is offered in evidence to prove the truth of its contents. Under s 4, a “statement” means, relevantly, a spoken or written assertion by a person of any matter.

[430] The deed of assignment contains some assertions. For example, recital A says: “The Borrower [(Lichfield)] is indebted to the Lender [(Propertyfinance Securities)] for the Debt.” As I understood their closing submissions, the 100 Parties rely on this recital (among other things) to prove that Lichfield was indebted to Propertyfinance Securities at the date of the assignment. Mr Barker emphasised in closing that Mr Henderson was closely involved in negotiating the deed of assignment. But that does not mean that Mr Henderson was the maker of the statement in recital A. None of the makers of the statement were witnesses. It is therefore a hearsay statement.

[431] The 100 Parties submitted that any hearsay statements in the deed of assignment should be admitted under the business records exception in s 19 of the Evidence Act. They submitted that no useful purpose would be served by requiring the person who supplied the information used to compose the deed to be a witness, as they could not reasonably be expected to recollect the matters dealt with in the information (s 19(1)(b)), or that undue expense or delay would be caused if the person were required to be a witness (s 19(1)(c)). I will assume, without deciding, that the deed is a “business record” in terms of s 16(1) of the Act. I consider that neither s 19(1)(b) nor (c) is satisfied. This was, self-evidently, not a transaction that occurred on a regular basis in the business of Propertyfinance Securities or 100 Investments. There is no reason to think that whoever supplied the information reflected in the recitals could not recollect (particularly by reference to other documents) whether Lichfield was indebted to Propertyfinance Securities at the time. Any expense or delay in calling that person as a witness would not have been undue, given the importance of the issue.

[432] In other respects, however, the deed of assignment does not contain assertions. Its operative provisions, such as the assignment provisions, do not assert anything. The assignment provisions effect an assignment rather than assert that an assignment

has taken place. They are not statements (as defined in the Evidence Act), let alone hearsay statements. The deed of assignment is therefore admissible to prove that the assignment from Propertyfinance Securities to 100 Investments occurred.

[433] The defendants did not raise any issue as to the authenticity of the deed of assignment or as to whether it had been signed: they did not object to the application of r 9.5(1)(b)–(e) of the High Court Rules to the deed of assignment and did not challenge Mr Henderson on these matters.
