By contrast, Mr Barker submitted that those fees did not relate to the PVL proceedings but rather to the more general liquidation, with the consequence that no part of those fees could be recouped from the settlement proceeds.

[573] I reject both submissions. The correct result lies somewhere in between. As to Mr Cooper’s submission, Mr Walker did not say that the entirety of the $4.08 million represented work done on the PVL proceedings. Nor can that be assumed from the material that Mr Walker referred to. The comparative tables he produced do not identify how much of his and his staff’s time was spent on the PVL proceedings as opposed to general liquidation work. That also cannot be discerned from the underlying timesheets that Mr Walker produced. Likewise, no such distinction is made in the invoices that Mr Walker rendered when the settlement was reached with PwC.

[574] As to Mr Barker’s submission, Mr Walker’s unchallenged evidence was that “very significant amounts” of time during the course of the PVL proceedings were not charged to SPF. This was supported by Mr Scutter’s evidence and by contemporaneous documents. I consider it follows that a substantial part of the

$4.08 million was for work done on the PVL proceedings. The question is: how much?

[575] The original budget for the PVL proceedings included $555,000 for liquidator’s costs. By late November 2015 that had been used up and Mr Walker was saying that he had uncharged time of about $520,000. In the absence of contrary evidence, I infer that Mr Walker continued to charge SPF for only some of his and his

staff’s time on the PVL proceedings, in roughly the same proportions as he had up to November 2015. I give no weight to Mr Walker’s 28 January 2015 memorandum in which he suggested his unbilled time was in the millions of dollars. Mr Walker acknowledged that this was a plea for payment. I regard it as an exaggeration for the purposes of negotiating with SPF.

[576] William Apps, an expert called by LPF, calculated that the total amount paid by SPF to Mr Walker as Project Costs (including the $150,000 paid by SPF in February 2016) was $1,679,061 excluding GST.65 I therefore infer that Mr Walker undercharged SPF by about $1,573,000 through the course of the PVL proceedings. Allowing for GST, I conclude that Mr Walker’s remuneration for work that led to the realisation of the settlement proceeds amounts to approximately $1.8 million. This is encompassed within the invoices rendered by Mr Walker following the PwC settlement.

[577] Ordinarily, I would also have to assess whether that remuneration was reasonable. However, there was no issue between the parties as to the reasonableness of Mr Walker’s remuneration. That is to be determined in the separate remuneration application brought by Mr Whitley in the PVL liquidation proceeding.

What reasonable costs could Mr Walker recoup from the settlement proceeds?

[578] The only costs that it is suggested Mr Walker could recoup from the settlement proceeds are the costs he incurred in making payments to SPF. Two issues arise:

(a) What amount of SPF costs did Mr Walker incur in realising the settlement proceeds?

(b) How much of those SPF costs were reasonable?

[579] Before addressing those issues, I clarify one matter. These issues are about the rights to the settlement proceeds as between Mr Walker and the 100 Parties. They are not about the rights of SPF. If I were to conclude that Mr Walker had unreasonably incurred costs to SPF, that would affect Mr Walker’s right of recoupment. But it would

65 I have not included the relatively insignificant amount paid by SPF for Mr Scutter’s fees.

not necessarily follow that SPF was liable to repay, either to Mr Walker, the 100 Parties or anyone else, the “unreasonable” payment. Any such liability could arise only from the application of some principle other than the principle governing Mr Walker’s right of recoupment. This distinction was not always observed in the plaintiffs’ case.

What amount of SPF costs did Mr Walker incur in realising the settlement proceeds?

[580] A distinction needs to be drawn between, on the one hand, the payments made to SPF under the Services Deed (as amended by the Distribution Agreement) for Project Costs and the Services Fee and, on the other, the payments made to SPF under the Allied security and the DFG security. Only the former can be characterised as costs incurred by Mr Walker in realising the settlement proceeds. The latter cannot. They were, rather, a distribution of part of the proceeds in accordance with SPF’s rights under the Allied security and the DFG security. This is not to question the validity of SPF’s claim to those payments under those securities. But the present inquiry is into the extent of Mr Walker’s claim to priority (as against putative secured creditors) for costs incurred in realising the proceeds.

[581] The parties to the Distribution Agreement agreed, in cl 1.3, that some payments to SPF were made under the Services Deed and other payments were made under the Allied security and the DFG security. It will be convenient to repeat cl 1.3 in full:

For the avoidance of any doubt, all payments made to or on behalf of SPF in accordance with:

(a) paragraphs 1.1(a)–(c) (as the case may be) are made in accordance with the Services Deed and in reliance by SPF on the security created under the Services Deed;

(b) paragraphs 1.1(d) or 1.2 (as the case may be) are made pursuant to and in enforcement by SPF of the Allied Debts and the Dominion Debts.

[582] I see no reason to go behind the Agreement. Clause 1.3(b) identifies that two payments to SPF are under the Allied security and the DFG security. One of the payments is easy to quantify: the payment under cl 1.2 was the payment from the directors settlement. SPF received $2.3 million from that settlement. That payment is not a cost that Mr Walker is entitled to recoup from the settlement proceeds in priority to any secured creditors with an interest in the proceeds.

[583] The other payment referred to in cl 1.3(b) — the payment under cl 1.1(d) — is not so easy to quantify. Under cl 1.1(d), SPF was to receive the balance of the PwC settlement proceeds ($33.8 million) after (a) payment of its Project Costs and the Services Fee in accordance with the Services Deed and (b) payment of $5,380,000 to PVL. In order to quantify that balance, it is necessary to quantify the amount of Project Costs, and the Services Fee, that SPF was entitled to be paid. The Distribution Agreement did not quantify that amount and the parties to the Agreement did not quantify it at the time. Quantification therefore has to be done retrospectively.

[584] Before undertaking the quantification exercise, I note that there is a limit on how much the payment of Project Costs and the Services Fee could have been under cl 1.1(d). The PwC settlement proceeds were $33.8 million, of which $5.38 million was paid to PVL. The balance — and the most that the payment of Project Costs and the Services Fee could have been — was $28,420,000.

[585] I now turn to quantification. The focus is on Project Costs, as the Services Fee is a product of that amount. While the parties to the present proceedings agreed on some elements that were Project Costs, there were some significant disputes.

[586] In resolving those disputes, what needs to be established are the Project Costs at the time of the PwC settlement, not Project Costs at the end of the litigation in early 2018. This is because the effect of cl 4.1 of the Distribution Agreement (the full and final settlement provision) was that SPF had no entitlement to be repaid any Project Costs that it might advance after the PwC settlement, or to be paid any further Services Fee in respect of those Project Costs. Further, SPF’s entitlement to Project Costs needs to be determined in accordance with any amendments made to the Services Deed by the Agreement.

[587] Mr Apps analysed SPF’s records of Project Costs. His evidence, based on those records, was that the Project Costs paid or payable at the time of the PwC settlement totalled $9,691,710. There was no contrary analysis in evidence. To that amount Mr Apps added allowances for further payments to DFG ($1.15 million) and

Allied ($97,757).66 He therefore assessed total Project Costs at that time as

$10,939,468. He said SPF’s entitlement to the Services Fee at that time was

$21,878,935 (two times total Project Costs).

[588] I accept Mr Apps’ analysis. However, Mr Apps was merely giving evidence of his analysis of SPF’s records. He was not purporting to give an expert opinion on whether the various disputed items were or were not Project Costs. I now turn to those disputes.

[589] First, the plaintiffs challenged $1.95 million of security for costs that was provided by SPF in the PVL proceedings by way of bank bonds. In closing, Mr Moss acknowledged that the definitions of “Project Costs” and “Security for Costs” in the Services Deed appeared to cover the provision of security for costs in this manner. But Mr Moss said that the evidence of John Walker (who was called by LPF to give expert evidence on litigation funding) was that the face value of a bond would not normally be considered a Project Cost. Mr Moss submitted, based on that evidence, that security for costs could be claimed as a Project Cost only to the extent that SPF incurred an out-of-pocket cost (for example, by paying cash into court or backing a bond with cash).

[590] I do not accept that submission. The Services Deed defines “Project Costs” as meaning:67

... all costs, disbursements and liabilities of any nature that are incurred or assumed (whether absolutely or contingently) by ... SPF in relation to or in connection with ... the Proceedings.

Those words themselves cover a bank bond or bank guarantee, whether backed by cash or not. The matter is put beyond any doubt by the definition then saying that Project Costs “includ[es] ... the provision of Security for Costs in any form acceptable to a Court” and by the definition of “Security for Costs”, which means “any gross cash

66 The amount that Mr Apps allowed for the Allied payment was significantly less than the amount that SPF eventually paid. The reason for this is not clear. However, it reduced the amount of Project Costs that Mr Apps assessed as having been paid or accrued at that time.

67 Emphasis added.

amount or the face value of any security”.68 Mr John Walker’s evidence is not relevant to the plain meaning of these words.

[591] Secondly, the plaintiffs said that the further anticipated payments to Allied and DFG did not fall within the definition of Project Costs. Mr Moss accepted that the initial payment to Allied of $115,000 (including GST) was a Project Cost. But he submitted that the further payment to Allied was to be five percent of any net proceeds received by SPF from the litigation. He said this was a payment made after the costs under the Services Deed had already been paid. He said the same was true of the payment that was to be made by SPF to DFG. In addition, he said that the DFG payment arose from SPF’s own negligence in allowing the Allied security to lapse.

[592] Those submissions ignore the plain words of the Distribution Agreement. Clause 1.1(a) said that the outstanding Project Costs were to be paid to SPF, “such Project Costs to include the purchase price of the Allied Debts ... and the purchase price of the [DFG] Debts”.

[593] It is likely that the definition of Project Costs in the Services Deed was broad enough to capture the purchase prices payable by SPF for the Allied security and the DFG security. That definition covers liabilities, including contingent liabilities, incurred by SPF “in relation to or in connection with” the Deed or the PVL proceedings. But cl 1.1(a) of the Distribution Agreement puts the matter beyond any doubt.

[594] Thirdly, in closing Mr Moss submitted that SPF’s payment of $150,000 to Mr Walker in February 2016 was not for costs relating to the PVL litigation. This was not pleaded. Further, Mr Walker’s and Mr Woodham’s evidence about this payment (which I addressed when looking at Mr Walker’s remuneration) was not challenged. I find this payment did relate to the PVL litigation.

[595] Fourthly, Mr Moss challenged several miscellaneous costs:

68 Emphasis added.

(a) Mr Smith KC’s fee of October 2012 for his initial opinion. Mr Moss said the definition of Project Costs only includes costs incurred “from the date of this Deed (not before)”. But the Services Deed, although not signed by SPF until March 2013, bears the date of 31 October 2012. Further, Mr Smith KC’s fee was not paid until December 2012.

(b) Mr Bigio’s fees during 2017. Mr Bigio was engaged by SPF in relation to the hearing before the Supreme Court and in relation to the distribution dispute. These matters plainly relate to or are in connection with both the Services Deed and the PVL proceedings. They are therefore Project Costs.

(c) Solicitors’ fees in 2016 and 2017 for attendances relating to the priority dispute with DFG, the Supreme Court hearing and the distribution dispute. I find that all these were “in relation to or in connection with” the Deed or the PVL proceedings.

(d) Fees for media consultancy. Up to the PwC settlement, these total about

$21,000. Mr Moss submitted that an explanation for these had not been given and that they cannot have been for the benefit of the PVL proceedings. I disagree. It is not unusual for media consultants to be engaged on significant litigation. The consultants’ invoices reference “Property Ventures Limited / PwC”. Mr Woodhams gave evidence that all the miscellaneous costs were within the definition of Project Costs (and, therefore, incurred in relation to the PVL proceedings). His evidence was not challenged. I find these were Project Costs.

(e) Further expenses of Mr Woodhams of $1,616.15. Mr Woodhams’ evidence, to which I have just referred, was not challenged. I find these were Project Costs.

[596] I therefore find that, as at the date of the PwC settlement, the total Project Costs were $10,939,468. This meant that the Services Fee (two times Project Costs) was

$21,878,935. These amounts totalled $32,818,403. From this, there had to be deductions for the following, both of which Mr Apps noted in his analysis:

(a) SPF had already received partial repayment of Project Costs of

$2,180,500 from the Fright Aubrey settlement.69

(b) SPF was going to enjoy, as a result of the PwC settlement, a reduction in some of the security for costs that it had provided. This reduction was quantified a few weeks after the settlement as $560,000.

[597] Allowing for those deductions, SPF’s entitlement to be paid Project Costs and the Services Fee at the time of the PwC settlement was $30,077,903. In fact, SPF was paid less than that for Project Costs and the Services Fee under the Distribution Agreement. As explained earlier, the most that SPF could have been paid for Project Costs and the Services Fee under the Agreement was $28,420,000. That is what SPF was paid for those items under the Agreement.

[598] Together with the earlier partial repayment from the Fright Aubrey settlement, this means that Mr Walker incurred costs to SPF totalling $30,600,500 in realising the settlement proceeds.

How much of the SPF costs were reasonable?

[599] The defendants noted that Mr Walker incurred the costs to SPF pursuant to obligations undertaken in the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement. They submitted that the reasonableness of those costs should therefore be assessed by considering whether it was reasonable for Mr Walker to enter into the Deed and the Agreement. That, they submitted, had to be assessed as at the respective times the Deed and the Agreement were entered into.

[600] Mr Barker, by contrast, submitted that the reasonableness of the costs had to be assessed as at the time the costs were paid. Mr Barker relied on Australian authorities dealing with the approval of settlements of class actions. He said these

  1. It appears the Fright Aubrey settlement proceeds were slightly smaller, $2,180,286. The difference is immaterial.
authorities showed that the test for approval is whether the settlement was “fair and reasonable” and that the terms of any funding agreement are not determinative of whether a settlement will be approved.

[601] The authorities to which Mr Barker referred are concerned with s 33V of the Federal Court of Australia Act 1976 (Cth). Section 33V(1) provides that a representative proceeding may not be settled or discontinued without the approval of the Court. Section 33V(2) says that if the Court gives such an approval, it may “make such orders as are just with respect to the distribution of any money paid under a settlement or paid into the Court”. Mr Barker referred me to Blairgowrie Trading Ltd v Allco Finance Group Ltd (recs and mgrs apptd) (in liq) (No 3), in which Beach J held that s 33V(2) empowers the Court to modify a funding agreement.70

[602] Mr Barker did not provide a comprehensive review of the Australian authorities. I have not found it necessary to undertake such a review myself.71 This is because the Court’s power to review and modify a funding agreement, recognised in Blairgowrie, derives from s 33V, which of course is inapplicable here.

[603] In my view, the reasonableness of the costs that Mr Walker incurred has to be assessed by reference to the circumstances at the time Mr Walker entered into the obligations that gave rise to those costs. Otherwise, hindsight will be brought to bear on the assessment. It is important to resist using hindsight in assessing the reasonableness of costs under litigation funding agreements. The hindsight will only ever be employed at a time when all the litigation risks are over and proceeds have been realised from a settlement or judgment. It is then all too easy to forget the risks that the funder undertook and to regard the funder’s return as being unreasonable. Here, for example, SPF would likely have lost almost its entire investment had the PwC stay application succeeded.

70 Blairgowrie Trading Ltd v Allco Finance Group Ltd (recs and mgrs apptd) (in liq) (No 3) [2017] FCA 330, (2017) 343 ALR 476 at [101].

71 In a later judgment (to which Mr Barker also referred me), Lee J declined to express a view on whether s 33V of the Federal Court of Australia Act 1976 (Cth) empowers the Court to review a funding agreement: Clarke v Sandhurst Trustees Ltd (No 2) [2018] FCA 511 at [17]–[18].

[604] I find that it was reasonable for Mr Walker to enter into the Services Deed on the terms that it contained. At the time, there appeared to be claims against PwC and the directors that had the potential to produce returns to creditors. There were no assets within PVL to fund the PVL proceedings. Mr Walker had tried, unsuccessfully, to interest Allied and another third party (the Wellington family) to fund the proceedings. No other secured creditor showed any interest in funding the proceedings. In particular, Tomanovich Holdings (which purports to have become a secured creditor of a PVL subsidiary in June 2010) expressed no interest in funding the proceedings. This meant that the proceedings were only going to be brought if Mr Walker entered into an agreement with a litigation funder.

[605] Mr John Walker’s evidence was that in 2012 there were six litigation funders in the Australian market. Only one of them was active in the New Zealand market, and it had a budget limit of $2 million, which was too small for the PVL proceedings. The only other funder active in the New Zealand market was LPF, which had been operating since 2009. LPF was the only funder taking on claims the size of the PVL proceedings. This meant that Mr Walker’s options for litigation funding were very limited.

[606] Mr John Walker identified a multitude of risks in each of the claims against PwC and the directors. He said these risks made the claims a riskier endeavour than most other large-scale funded cases. He said that Mr Walker had to persuade a litigation funder to assume those risks on the basis of non-recourse funding.

[607] In his opinion, the terms of the Services Deed were generally standard for the Australian and New Zealand market in 2012, including the definition of Project Costs (subject to one caveat) and the Services Fee. He noted that in 2012 most funding agreements with companies in liquidation were on the basis that the funder would be reimbursed any project costs and be paid a fee of the greater of three times project costs and 20 to 45 per cent of the gross proceeds of the claim. He would not have expected any other funder in the market to agree to fund the PVL proceedings for lesser rates than in the Deed or at all.

[608] Mr John Walker was cross-examined extensively, but much of the cross-examination explored the way in which the Australian courts review the reasonableness of settlements of class actions. The aspects of his evidence that I have just outlined were not challenged.

[609] There was one caveat to Mr John Walker’s opinion that the definition of Project Costs was market standard. It concerned security for costs. He said that in 2012 it was market standard for any payments of security for costs by a funder in the form of cash into court or by cash-backed bonds to be included within the definition of project costs. In cross-examination and re-examination he clarified that this meant that if a bond was not backed by cash, the market standard would be that only the upfront cost of obtaining the bond, but not the potential liability under it, would be within the definition of project costs. Although he was not taken to it, this contrasted with the definitions of Project Costs and Security for Costs in the Services Deed, under which the potential liability would be a Project Cost.

[610] The plaintiffs submitted that this meant it was not reasonable to include within Project Costs the $1.95 million that SPF had provided as security for costs by way of bank bonds, because SPF had not shown that these bonds were backed by cash.

[611] I reject that submission, for two reasons. First, Mr Newland said in cross-examination that “[w]e had to back with hard cash the security for costs as it became available”. That evidence went unchallenged. Secondly, I consider the definitions of Project Costs and Security for Costs were reasonable in the wider context in which the Services Deed was entered into. SPF was the only viable funder in the market. SPF was taking on a much riskier endeavour than most other large-scale funded cases. SPF was charging a Services Fee lower than the market: the greater of two times (not three times) Project Costs or a percentage of net (not gross) proceeds. And there is nothing commercially unreasonable about counting the face liability of a bank bond as a Project Cost. If the proceedings had failed and the bank bond had been called on, that face value would have crystallised as a very real cost for SPF. So, even if the bank bonds had not been backed by cash, I would have regarded it as reasonable for Mr Walker to pay SPF on the basis (as agreed in the Services Deed) that they were Project Costs.

[612] The final issue is whether it was reasonable for Mr Walker to agree, in the Distribution Agreement, that Project Costs would include the purchase prices of the Allied and DFG debts and securities. In closing, Mr Moss accepted that SPF’s first payment to Allied ($115,000) was a Project Cost because the Services Deed was conditional on obtaining an assignment of the Allied security and so the initial payment was connected with the Deed and with the PVL proceedings. He submitted, however, that the second payment ($441,137.15) could not reasonably be considered a Project Cost because it was to be paid from the net proceeds after payments were made to SPF under the Deed. Among other things, he said it was not a cost to SPF at all. Mr Moss said that the same applied to the purchase price of the DFG security of

$1.65 million. In addition, he submitted that it was not reasonable to count that purchase price as a Project Cost, given that the purchase of the DFG security became necessary only because of SPF’s failure to renew the PPSR registration of the Allied security.

[613] Whether these costs were reasonable needs to be determined at the time the Distribution Agreement was entered into. That includes taking into account the compromise that was reached in the Agreement. The following points are relevant.

[614] First, the amounts that Mr Moss put in issue total approximately $2.09 million of Project Costs. Given that the Services Fee was going to be calculated at two times Project Costs, Mr Moss submitted that the inclusion of the purchase prices of the Allied security and the DFG security as Project Costs meant that SPF received an additional $6.27 million from the overall settlement proceeds. But that would only have been the case had there been an Allied security agreement under which SPF was bound to hold the proceeds received under the Allied security for PVL. Given that there was no such agreement, the inclusion of these items as Project Costs had a much smaller impact on the parties’ respective returns than was submitted by Mr Moss. This can be most easily illustrated by considering the effect that the inclusion of these items as Project Costs would have had on the parties’ respective returns had SPF merely complied with the undertaking it had given to the Supreme Court (to pay to PVL 10 per cent of the balance of the settlement proceeds, after first deducting its entitlements under the Services Deed). In that case:

(a) Inclusion of these two items would have increased SPF’s total entitlement to be paid Project Costs and the Services Fee by

$6.27 million.

(b) That would, however, have reduced the balance of the settlement proceeds and therefore reduced SPF’s entitlement under the Allied security by the same amount of $6.27 million.

(c) The effect of SPF’s undertaking was that the reduction in (b) was only 90 per cent of the $6.27 million.

(d) The inclusion of the two items would therefore have increased SPF’s overall return by 10 per cent of $6.27 million, which is $627,000.

[615] In actuality, of course, SPF did not merely comply with the undertaking. SPF paid more to PVL than it had initially undertaken to pay. Further, the structure of the payments agreed under cls 1.1 and 1.2 of the Distribution Agreement was such that the inclusion of the Allied and DFG payments as Project Costs made no difference to SPF’s overall return. Under the Agreement, SPF was to receive $28,420,000 from the PwC settlement (under either the Services Deed or the Allied security and the DFG security) and an agreed share of any directors settlement (under the securities). The only effect of including the Allied and DFG payments as Project Costs was to increase the proportion of the $28,420,000 that was attributable to SPF’s return under the Deed rather than its return under the securities. This, indeed, is the case for all the contested Project Costs.

[616] Secondly, the effect of cl 1.1 of the Distribution Agreement was that the most SPF could be paid for its return under the Services Deed was $28,420,000. That was significantly less than SPF’s entitlement under the Deed (assuming inclusion of the Allied and DFG payments as Project Costs) at the time of the PwC settlement. Mr Apps’ calculation of SPF’s entitlement at that time (net of the deductions for the payment from the Fright Aubrey settlement and the reduction in security for costs) was $30,077,903. However, in reaching that amount Mr Apps did not include as Project Costs the entire amount that Mr Moss put in issue. Mr Apps allowed for the

full DFG purchase price but only $97,757 for the further Allied payment. Had Mr Apps allowed the full amount of that further payment ($441,137.15), the calculation of SPF’s entitlement would have been $31,108,043. That is approximately

$2.69 million more than the maximum SPF was able to recover under cl 1.1. This means that the consequence of the inclusion of the Allied and DFG purchase prices as Project Costs was that SPF received an additional $3.58 million under the Deed (not the $6.27 million suggested by Mr Moss) than it otherwise would have.

[617] Thirdly, this amount can only be treated as “additional” if SPF was not already, under the Services Deed, entitled to it. I consider that the definition of Project Costs in the Deed captured the purchase prices paid to Allied and DFG. The definition includes costs and liabilities of any nature that are assumed absolutely or contingently by SPF “in relation to or in connection with” the Deed or the PVL proceedings. SPF’s liabilities to Allied and DFG were “in connection with” both the Deed and the PVL proceedings, because SPF would never have entered into the agreements with Allied and DFG were SPF not funding the PVL proceedings under the Deed.

[618] Fourthly, Mr John Walker’s evidence did not assist, one way or another, in understanding whether it was market standard for the post-settlement payments to Allied and DFG to be included within Project Costs. In his brief he expressed the view that it was reasonable for the purchase prices paid to Allied and DFG to be included within Project Costs. However, in his oral evidence it emerged that he was unaware that part of those purchase prices was to be paid after settlement and that the amount of the payment depended on the amount of net proceeds. Once he became aware of those matters, he felt unable to say, without further information, whether including those payments as Project Costs was usual.

[619] Fifthly, the post-settlement payments that SPF made to Allied and DFG were real costs to SPF. They reduced the return that SPF would otherwise obtain (either as funder or as assignee of the debts and securities).

[620] These factors lead me to the view that, even before considering the other compromises made in the Distribution Agreement, it was reasonable for the further Allied payment to be treated as a Project Cost. That accounts for $1.32 million of the

“additional” $3.58 million that SPF received under the Services Deed, leaving a balance of $2.26 million. That balance reflects the effect of including the DFG purchase price as a Project Cost on SPF’s return under the Services Deed (but noting that it made no difference to SPF’s overall return under the Deed and the securities).

[621] There is force in Mr Moss’s submission that it is unreasonable to include as a Project Cost a cost that has arisen from SPF’s failure to renew the PPSR registration for the Allied security. However, the submission loses much of its force when it is realised that the inclusion of this cost had no effect on SPF’s (or PVL’s) overall return. The inclusion merely affected the portion of the $28,420,000 that the parties agreed, in the Distribution Agreement, to attribute to SPF’s return under the Services Deed. I consider it was reasonable for the parties to reach that agreement, given the other benefits that accrued to PVL under the Distribution Agreement: SPF agreed to caps on its returns from the PwC and directors settlements and thereby agreed to pay significantly more to PVL than it had initially undertaken to pay; SPF remained obliged to advance Project Costs yet would enjoy no further return from those advances; and SPF agreed to transfer the Allied security and the DFG security to the liquidator for the benefit of PVL’s unsecured creditors.

[622] As to the second of those benefits, SPF advanced at least $1 million in Project Costs after the PwC settlement. Under the Services Deed, SPF would have been entitled to be repaid those costs and be paid an additional Services Fee of at least $2 million. Under the Distribution Agreement, the parties agreed that SPF’s share of the directors settlement proceeds should be attributed solely to a return under the Allied and DFG securities. This characterisation of SPF’s return outweighed the effect of including the DFG purchase price as a Project Cost.

[623] I therefore conclude that Mr Walker reasonably incurred $30,600,500 in costs to SPF in realising the settlement proceeds.

[624] I add some observations. The plaintiffs at times presented their cases as if it was obvious that it was unreasonable for a litigation funder to receive as much of the settlement proceeds as SPF did here. This overlooked three interrelated matters. First, litigation funding on a non-recourse basis is a high-risk investment in any

circumstances and, as Mr John Walker identified, the PVL litigation was a riskier endeavour than most other large-scale funded cases. No other funder was prepared to touch it. Secondly, one of the risks for the funder is that the claim proves more costly and time-consuming than expected. This risk manifested itself here. The solicitors engaged on the PVL proceedings provided SPF with a budget and projected timeline for the proceedings at the outset. Even leaving to one side the Allied and DFG costs, the actual expenditure on key items such as legal costs was two to three times the budget and the proceedings took about twice as long to resolve as forecast. A funder naturally and reasonably seeks some protection from this risk by negotiating a return that can (depending on outcome) be a product of project costs. The level of SPF’s return therefore reflects the manifestation of this risk in the PVL proceedings. Thirdly, SPF was not merely providing funding. It was also providing the time and expertise of Mr Woodhams and Mr Newland. Among other things, they had a leading role in settlement negotiations with Fright Aubrey, PwC and the director defendants. Their efforts were instrumental in producing substantial settlements.

Summary

[625] Mr Walker was entitled to recoup from the settlement proceeds, in priority to any secured creditor, a total of $32,400,500: $1,800,000 for his remuneration and

$30,600,500 in costs. This is 83 per cent of the total settlement proceeds.

[626] If I had found the 100 Parties to be secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, I would have found $1,352,000 of the settlement proceeds to be subject to their securities. That is only a part of the settlement proceeds. An issue then arises as to how the right of recoupment is exercised in such circumstances. Can Mr Walker recoup the first $1,352,000 of his costs from that portion, leaving nothing for the putative secured creditors, or can he recoup only a proportionate amount (83 per cent) of that portion?

[627] I did not receive any submissions on this point, though Mr Barker’s approach assumed that any recoupment would be proportional only. I agree with that approach.

[628] It follows that had I found the 100 Parties to be secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, the net amount available to them after Mr Walker exercised his right of recoupment would have been 17 per cent of $1,352,000, which is $229,840.

Issue 9: Does s 284 of the Companies Act confer jurisdiction to make the orders sought by Mr Whitley affecting the rights of Mr Walker, LPF and SPF?

[629] In several of his causes of actions Mr Whitley seeks orders under s 284(1)(a) and (b) of the Companies Act, which provides:

284 Court supervision of liquidation

(1) On the application of the liquidator, a liquidation committee, or, with the leave of the court, a creditor, shareholder, other entitled person, or director of a company in liquidation, the court may—

(a) give directions in relation to any matter arising in connection with the liquidation:

(b) confirm, reverse, or modify an act or decision of the liquidator:

...

[630] The orders that Mr Whitley seeks under s 284(1)(a) and (b) constitute substantive remedies or relief against Mr Walker, LPF or SPF. For example, Mr Whitley’s second cause of action makes claims about the validity of the Distribution Agreement and about the reasonableness of payments made under it. Mr Whitley then seeks relief that includes:

(a) a direction under s 284(1)(a) that various Project Costs were unreasonable expenses of the liquidations of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries and that the sum of $16,954,678 has to be repaid by SPF and LPF to the Whitley Parties; and

(b) an order under s 284(1)(b) reversing the decision of Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement.

[631] The defendants submitted that s 284(1)(a) and (b) do not confer jurisdiction to make substantive orders against third parties in the way sought by Mr Whitley.

Can the court make directions under s 284(1)(a) of the Companies Act having the effect of substantive orders against third parties?

[632] In relation to s 284(1)(a), the defendants relied on a judgment of Associate Judge Paulsen in Dalton v Mackley.72 There, the liquidators of a company considered that the company had purchased certain assets from Mr Mackley prior to liquidation. Mr Mackley disputed this. The liquidators brought an originating application for directions under s 284(1)(a) to resolve the dispute. Although framed as an application for directions, the liquidators asked the court “to rule that [the company] owns the assets and to order Mr Mackley to deliver up the assets to them”.73 The Judge was asked to determine whether the orders sought by the liquidators were ones that the court could make under s 284(1)(a).

[633] The Judge held that the orders sought were not ones that could properly be made under s 284(1)(a).2025_3486.html#_bookmark73">74 As to the text of the provision, the Judge said:75

[24] The text of s 284 and other indications provided by the Companies Act are supportive of a view that the proper subject of directions under s 284(1)(a) is as to the manner in which a liquidation should be carried out under the control of the liquidator and not in the making of binding orders in the nature of judgments. The section appears in pt 16 of the Act, the scheme of which is to provide liquidators with as little interference from the court as is practicable while retaining a measure of court supervision of liquidators upon application. The section also appears in a subpart headed “Appointment and supervision of liquidators” and s 284 itself is headed “Court supervision of liquidation”. The word “supervision” connotes management or oversight not the making of binding orders against third parties in the nature of a judgment.

...

[27] Third, central to the operation of the section is s 284(3). That provides a liquidator who has “obtained a direction with respect to a matter connected with the exercise of the powers or functions of liquidator” and who acts in accordance with the direction, with a defence to a claim in relation to anything done or not done in accordance with the direction. The use of the phrase “the exercise of the powers or functions of liquidator”, again supports the view that the section is concerned with directing the liquidator in the proper discharge of its functions. ...

72 Dalton v Mackley [2021] NZHC 2329.

73 At [2].

74 At [42].

75 Footnotes omitted.

[634] The Judge then reviewed case law, including Australian decisions on provisions equivalent to s 284(1)(a). One of the cases the Judge relied on was a decision of Goldberg J in the Federal Court of Australia, Re Ansett Australia Ltd (No 3).76 Re Ansett was concerned with the then Australian equivalent to s 284(1)(a), s 479(3) of the Corporations Act 2001 (Cth). Judge Paulsen quoted the following passage from Goldberg J’s judgment:77

[44] When liquidators and administrators seek directions from the Court in relation to any decision they have made, or propose to make, or in relation to any conduct they have undertaken, or propose to undertake, they are not seeking to determine rights and liabilities arising out of particular transactions, but are rather seeking protection against claims that they have acted unreasonably or inappropriately or in breach of their duty in making the decision or undertaking the conduct. They can obtain that protection if they make full and fair disclosure of all relevant facts and circumstances to the Court. In Re G B Nathan & Co Pty Ltd (In Liq) (1991) 24 NSWLR 674, McLelland J said at 679 – 680:

“The historical antecedents of s 479(3) ..., the terms of that subsection and the provisions of s 479 as a whole combine to lead to the conclusion that the only proper subject of a liquidator’s application for directions is the manner in which the liquidator should act in carrying out his functions as such ...

...

Modern Australian authority confirms the view that s 479(3) ‘does not enable the court to make binding orders in the nature of judgments’ and that the function of a liquidator’s application for directions ‘is to give him advice as to his proper course of action in the liquidation; it is not to determine the rights and liabilities arising from the company’s transactions before the liquidation’.” (Cases cited omitted.)

[635] Judge Paulsen concluded:

[41] I consider it most unlikely, given the legislative history and terms of s 284, as well as the themes advanced by the Companies Act 1993, that Parliament intended to confer on liquidators the right to seek substantive judgments against third parties under the guise of seeking directions. Such claims are not in reality seeking directions and usually will be unsuitable for determination in a summary proceeding under s 284 because of the need for particularised pleadings, discovery, interlocutory applications and the existence of significant factual disputes.

76 Re Ansett Australia Ltd (No 3) [2002] FCA 901, (2002) 115 FCR 409.

77 Dalton v Mackley, above n 72, at [37], citing Re Ansett Australia Ltd (No 3), above n 76.

[636] The Judge then noted the possibility of regularising the proceeding by treating it as if it had been commenced in the correct manner. In that regard, he referred to an observation of McLelland J in Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq):78

The procedures of the court are sufficiently flexible to enable proceedings commenced as an application for directions to be changed into proceedings for the determination of substantive rights, and this is sometimes a convenient course in order to avoid the need to commence further proceedings involving additional cost and delay... However it is important that the distinction between the two kinds of proceedings be not lost sight of or blurred, and such a fundamental change should not be permitted unless the court is satisfied that those affected either consent to that course ... or will not suffer injustice in consequence of the alteration to the status of the proceedings.

[637] Mr Moss accepted that Dalton v Mackley supported the general proposition that an application for directions should not be used as a shortcut to seek judgments against third parties. But he said that the case did not decide that an application for directions could not be used to determine third party rights where the third parties had been given a proper opportunity to be heard. He said that, in that respect, Dalton was distinguishable. There, the liquidators had brought an originating application, which (as Judge Paulsen noted) is a summary proceeding. Here, Mr Whitley was seeking directions under s 284 in the context of an ordinary proceeding.

[638] In support of his submission, Mr Moss relied on a decision of the Federal Court of Australia, Australian Securities Commission v Melbourne Asset Management Nominees Pty Ltd.79 There, the Court said:80

It has been accepted that Courts have power to make final orders in preference claims on an application by a liquidator under sections similar to s 479(3) ... There is no logical reason why final orders binding on other persons cannot be made on applications under s 479(3) with respect to other subject matters. The second passage from [Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq)] cited above refers to consent of parties or to cases where a party “will not suffer injustice in consequence of the alteration to the status of the proceedings”. This is the crucial matter. In proceedings brought by a liquidator under s 479(3), I can see no reason why binding orders cannot be made where the parties affected have been given the opportunity to be heard. ... [In this case the] parties affected by the directions and orders sought on the motion in the winding up proceedings are before the Court and have been heard.

78 Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq) (1991) 24 NSWLR 674 (NSWSC) at 680, as quoted in

Dalton v Mackley, above n 72, at [47].

  1. Australian Securities Commission v Melbourne Asset Management Nominees Pty Ltd [1994] FCA 1031; (1994) 49 FCR 334 (FCA).

80 At 352, referring to Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq), above n 78, at 680.

[639] Mr Moss noted that while Judge Paulsen had cited G B Nathan with approval, the Judge had not referred to Melbourne Asset Management. Mr Moss submitted that the correct position was that directions could be made under s 284(1)(a) affecting the substantive rights of third parties so long as those third parties had been given the opportunity to be heard. Here, he said, the defendants had been given every such opportunity.

[640] I consider, with respect, that the position is more nuanced than that suggested by the parties or by Judge Paulsen in Dalton v Mackley. There are two aspects to the issue regarding the scope of the directions that can be made under s 284(1)(a). One is procedural, the other substantive.

[641] The procedural aspect of the issue was the focus of cases such as G B Nathan, Melbourne Asset Management and Dalton v Mackley. Directions that affect the rights of third parties should, of course, be made only if the parties affected have had a proper opportunity to be heard. In most cases, a summary procedure (such as the originating application procedure used in Dalton v Mackley) will not provide a proper opportunity to be heard. But there may be some rare instances in which a summary procedure is sufficient. This was the position adopted by the Federal Court of Australia in Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq) v Balanced Securities Ltd, in which the Court reconciled the apparently divergent positions taken in Melbourne Asset Management and GB Nathan.81

[642] Here, I accept Mr Moss’s submission that the defendants were given a proper opportunity to be heard. Mr Whitley sought the directions in an ordinary proceeding. There was discovery. There were many interlocutory applications. There was a trial.

[643] But there is a substantive aspect to the issue as well. Mr Whitley advanced his claim on the basis that directions affecting the rights of third parties could be made under s 284(1)(a) even if the Whitley Parties did not succeed on their other causes of action against those third parties. Most obviously, the Whitley Parties sought repayment of various Project Costs from SPF and LPF under causes of action for breach of contract, knowing receipt and undue influence. Independent of those causes

81 Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq) v Balanced Securities Ltd [2007] FCA 1443 at [51].

of action, the Whitley Parties sought a direction under s 284(1)(a) that those various Project Costs were “unreasonable” expenses in the liquidations and that the sum of

$16,954,678 had to be repaid by SPF and LPF to the Whitley Parties.

[644] Mr Moss was unable to point me to any authority that s 284(1)(a) could be used in this way to bypass the requirements of the causes of action that a company in liquidation or a liquidator might have against a third party. I consider it is implicit in the Australian authorities that a provision such as s 284(1)(a) cannot be used in this way. In Meadow Springs Fairway Resort, for example, the Federal Court of Australia contemplated that if determination of an issue summarily under the equivalent to s 284(1)(a) was not appropriate the parties could instead “simply commence their own substantive proceedings”.82 That observation makes sense only if the issue itself is to be resolved by applying the general law, rather than by some consideration of what is “reasonable” under s 284(1)(a).

[645] I therefore consider that s 284(1)(a) does not confer any jurisdiction to make substantive determinations of rights in the manner sought by Mr Whitley. The Whitley Parties’ claims against the defendants must be determined by applying the applicable substantive rules (such as of the law of contract and the law of knowing receipt). Section 284(1)(a) cannot be used to paper over any cracks that the Whitley Parties may have in those claims.

Can the court make an order under s 284(1)(b) affecting rights acquired by a third party as a consequence of the challenged act or decision of the liquidator?

[646] As noted, in his second cause of action Mr Whitley seeks an order under s 284(1)(b) reversing the decision of Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. The Whitley Parties claim equivalent relief in their eighth cause of action in undue influence, seeking an order setting aside the Distribution Agreement.

[647] Mr Moss’s closing submissions made it clear that the order under s 284(1)(b) reversing Mr Walker’s decision was sought independently of the claim in undue influence. He said the ground on which the order was sought was that Mr Walker’s

82 At [51].

decision to enter into the Distribution Agreement without first obtaining directions from the court was one no reasonable liquidator would have made. He acknowledged that an order reversing Mr Walker’s decision would affect LPF’s and SPF’s rights but said LPF and SPF knew that Mr Walker’s decision was unreasonable and proceeded to take the risk of entering into the Agreement anyway.

[648] Mr Salmon submitted that the power to reverse a liquidator’s actions under s 284(1)(b) does not extend to affecting rights acquired by an innocent third party as a consequence of the challenged act or decision of the liquidator. He relied on a decision of the Full Court of the Supreme Court of Victoria, Re Chirnside, in which Irvine CJ said, in relation to an equivalent provision in the bankruptcy context:83

But I do not think that the actual words of the section, properly construed, cover, under the words “act or decision”, a completed sale by the trustee which, apart from this section, would pass the property to a purchaser. Had it been intended to vest in the Bankruptcy Court a general jurisdiction to set aside contracts entered into, for any reason, either for fraud, or innocent misrepresentation, or for imprudence, or for carelessness, or other breach of duty on the part of the trustee, or for any other reason, I think the Legislature would have adopted language much more apt for that purpose, and instead of saying that the “act or decision” may be “confirmed, reversed or modified,” would have used words apt to express such a power, with all its consequences.

...

... the words “act or decision” ... refer to matters between the trustee and some person aggrieved by a decision of his being disposed of, and were not intended to confer a general jurisdiction upon the Court of Bankruptcy in relation to rights acquired as a result of his act or decision by outsiders.

[649] While there is a great deal of sense in Irvine CJ’s decision, and it has been essentially adopted in one decision of the High Court,84 it has not found universal favour. The Full Court of the Federal Court of Australia declined to follow it in Willoughby v Official Trustee in Bankruptcy (WA).85 In England and Wales it has been assumed, without the point having been decided, that the equivalent provision in that jurisdiction empowers a court to reverse a concluded transaction such that the rights of a third party are affected.86

83 Re Chirnside [1929] VicLawRp 22; [1929] VLR 217 (VSC) at 222–224.

84 Commissioner of Inland Revenue v Hulst (2000) 19 NZTC 15,693 at [44]–[45].

  1. Willoughby v Official Trustee in Bankruptcy (WA) [2000] FCA 757, (2000) 102 FCR 261 (FCAFC) at 32.
  2. Re Edennote Ltd [1996] EWCA Civ 1349; [1996] 2 BCLC 389 (CA); and Mahomed v Morris [2000] EWCA Civ 46; [2000] 2 BCLC 536 (Ch) at 540, upheld on appeal in Mahomed v Morris [2000] All ER (D) 193 (CA).

[650] More significantly, and as Mr Moss submitted, the Court of Appeal in Callis v Pardington upheld a decision of Barker J under s 241(5) of the Companies Act 1955 (the predecessor to s 284(1)(b)) setting aside an assignment that the liquidator had entered into.87 This directly affected the rights of the putative assignee. While the Court did not directly engage with the question whether (the now) s 284(1)(b) empowers a court to reverse a transaction so as to affect the rights of a third party, nor refer to Re Chirnside, the decision is authority that s 284(1)(b) can be used in that way. I am bound to follow it.

[651] This merely means that s 284(1)(b) can be used in a way that affects the rights of third parties. The court has a discretion whether to make an order under s 284(1)(b), indicated by the “may” in the opening words. That a reversal would affect the rights of third parties will be a relevant consideration in determining whether to exercise the discretion. Further, the discretion will in any event only be exercised in cases of fraud, a lack of good faith or where the liquidator has acted unreasonably.88

Issue 10: In light of the Settlement Deed, are any of the claims against Mr Walker an abuse of process?

[652] The Settlement Deed settled various claims made by Mr Walker (and entities associated with him) against Mr Henderson (and entities associated with him), and vice versa. Mr Henderson agreed that neither he nor any entity associated with him would bring any further claim against Mr Walker with respect to his conduct of the PVL liquidations, other than with respect to the claims outlined in the 1160 proceeding and any reasonable amendments as might be made to those claims.

[653] Mr Walker says that Mr Henderson has breached the Settlement Deed and that the 100 Parties and the Whitley Parties have either breached the Deed or knowingly induced breaches of it. Mr Walker alleges two breaches. First, he says Mr Henderson is “associated” with the Whitley Parties and so bringing the 274 proceeding in March 2022 and pursuing it was a breach of the Deed. Secondly, he says an amendment to the statement of claim in the 1160 proceeding in February 2022, to add a claim that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary duties, was a breach of the Deed.

87 Callis v Pardington (1996) 1 BCSLR 211 (CA).

88 At 215.

[654] Mr Walker says that the 274 proceeding and the breach of fiduciary duty claim in the 1160 proceeding are therefore abuses of process.

The parties to and terms of the Settlement Deed

[655] The Settlement Deed began by listing the following parties: Mr Walker in his personal capacity and as liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries; Mr Eathorne in his personal capacity and as liquidator of various companies; PVG Securities; Mr Henderson; Kristina Buxton (Mr Henderson’s partner); 100 Investments; RFD Finance; and FTG Securities.

[656] That list might suggest that PVL and the PVL subsidiaries were not themselves parties to the Settlement Deed. However, I find that they were parties to the Deed. Clause 1.11 states that Mr Walker agreed “on behalf of both himself personally as liquidator and on behalf of the [PVL] Companies” that the Deed settled various claims those companies had against Mr Henderson and Ms Buxton. Clause 11.2 is phrased similarly. Those clauses make sense only on the basis that PVL and the PVL subsidiaries were parties to the Deed. Further, the execution pages listed those companies as parties, with Mr Walker signing on their behalf as liquidator.

[657] The Settlement Deed includes the following provisions:

  • 1.16 Ms Buxton and Mr Henderson or any entity associated with them or their interests agree not to bring any further claim against Mr Walker and Mr Eathorne and their advisors with respect to their conduct of the liquidations of the Companies up until the date of settlement, other than with respect to the claims outlined in the 100 Investments proceeding and subject to any reasonable amendments as may be made to that claim.
  • 1.17 Ms Buxton and Mr Henderson or any entity associated with them or their interests agree not to bring any further claim against Mr Walker or his advisors with respect to his conduct of the liquidations of the Companies or touching on the issues in the proceedings, the liquidations or the Companies including any transactions involving property of the Companies, other than with respect to the claims outlined in the 100 Investment proceeding and subject to any reasonable amendments as may be made to that claim.
  • 1.18 Ms Buxton and Mr Henderson agree to seek to procure that any liquidator appointed by them or their entities, or any person in any way associated with them, also will not bring any claims or take any steps [of] any nature against Mr Walker or Mr Eathorne arising out of

companies in which Mr Henderson and/or Ms Buxton have had an interest.

...

Further assurances

1.24 Each party shall do all things, execute and deliver all documents and use all reasonable endeavours to do all things as may be reasonably requested by that other party to obtain the full benefit of this deed according to its true intent.

[658] The following issues arise:

(a) Are the Whitley Parties associated with Mr Henderson?

(b) If so, is pursuit of the 274 proceeding a breach of the Settlement Deed and therefore an abuse of process?

(c) Is pursuit of the fourth cause of action in the 1160 proceeding an abuse of process?

Are the Whitley Parties associated with Mr Henderson?

[659] Mr Moss submitted that, reading the Settlement Deed as a whole, an entity was “associated” with Mr Henderson only if Mr Henderson both controlled the entity and had a beneficial interest in it. He said Mr Whitley and the other Whitley Parties were not under Mr Henderson’s control: Mr Whitley brought the 274 proceeding in discharge of his duty as liquidator; his duties were to the unsecured creditors of the PVL companies; and Mr Whitley was clear in his evidence that he was not beholden to Mr Henderson. Further, Mr Henderson did not have any beneficial interest in any of the Whitley Parties.

[660] I consider Mr Moss’s interpretation of “associated” is unduly narrow. I accept Mr Cooper’s submissions as to the meaning of that word in the Settlement Deed. It is clear, reading the Deed as a whole, that the purpose of the Deed was to settle a broad range of claims that had been made between the parties and to prevent further claims being made (other than the claims in the 1160 proceeding). The purpose was to protect the defendants from further claims rather than, as Mr Moss might have it, to prevent

Mr Henderson (or any other claimant) from enjoying some beneficial return from further claims. That purpose could be undermined if Mr Henderson or Ms Buxton were at liberty to arrange for a third party to bring the further claims that they had undertaken not to bring. Clauses 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18 addressed that potential gap by extending the no-claim undertaking to any entity or person “associated” with Mr Henderson, Ms Buxton or their interests. In this context, I agree with Mr Cooper that “associated” means “[j]oined in companionship; united in action or purpose ... allied”.89

[661] On that meaning, all the Whitley Parties are associated with Mr Henderson. The relevant facts all point one way. Mr Henderson paid Mr Whitley $10,000 (through FTG Securities) shortly before Mr Whitley was appointed liquidator of the PVL companies (the other Whitley Parties). Mr Henderson was instrumental in arranging for Mr Whitley to be appointed as liquidator. Mr Henderson introduced Mr Moss (with whom Mr Henderson has a long-term professional relationship) to Mr Whitley for the purpose of representing the Whitley Parties in these proceedings. Mr Henderson agreed to indemnify and fund Mr Whitley for the costs of the liquidations, including the costs of this litigation (and then actively concealed this arrangement). More recently, Mr Moss’s fees for these proceedings have been paid directly by Mr Henderson rather than Mr Whitley. There was ample evidence that Mr Henderson has been actively involved in the conduct of the 274 proceeding.

Is pursuit of the 274 proceeding a breach of the Settlement Deed and therefore an abuse of process?

[662] Mr Moss submitted that even if the Whitley Parties are associated with Mr Henderson, the 274 proceeding is materially the same as the 1160 proceeding that was excluded from cls 1.16 and 1.17 and that therefore the pursuit of the 274 proceeding does not breach the Settlement Deed.

[663] I do not accept that submission. First, the exclusion in cls 1.16 and 1.17 is of “the claims outlined” in the 1160 proceeding and “any reasonable amendments as may be made to [those] claim[s]”. It is true, as Mr Moss submitted, that the facts underlying

89 Oxford English Dictionary (2nd ed, Clarendon Press, Oxford, 1989) vol 1 at 718.

the claims in the two proceedings are broadly the same and that there is some similarity in the legal basis of the claims advanced by the two sets of plaintiffs. But that is to focus solely on the basis of the claims. In identifying a claim, and therefore in determining whether one claim is the same as another, one must look not only at the basis of the claims but also at the parties to it (and at the remedy or relief sought). Here, the plaintiffs in the two proceedings are different and so the claims are different. Moreover, there are claims in the 274 proceeding that are not advanced in the 1160 proceeding. This means the claims in the 274 proceeding are not ones that are excluded from the operation of cls 1.16 and 1.17.

[664] Secondly, there is no equivalent exclusion in cl 1.18. There, Mr Henderson agreed:

... to seek to procure that any liquidator appointed by them or their entities, or any person in any way associated with them, also will not bring any claims or take any steps [of] any nature against Mr Walker ... arising out of companies in which Mr Henderson and/or Ms Buxton have had an interest.

The Whitley Parties are associated with Mr Henderson. They have brought claims and taken steps against Mr Walker in the 274 proceeding. Those claims and steps arise out of the PVL companies in which Mr Henderson had an interest.

[665] It follows that the pursuit of the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is a breach of cls 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18 of the Settlement Deed by Mr Henderson and the 100 Parties (other than Tomanavich Holdings). This has implications for Mr Walker’s counterclaim against those parties but by itself does not mean that pursuit of the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is an abuse of process. For there to be an abuse, the plaintiffs in the 274 proceeding, the Whitley Parties, must be abusing process.

[666] The Whitley Parties, other than Mr Whitley (that is, the PVL companies), are parties to the Settlement Deed. They are associated with Mr Henderson, and so it is arguable that they are in breach of cls 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18. I did not hear detailed argument on that point. It is unnecessary for me to decide it, because they are clearly in breach of the further assurances provision, cl 1.24. Clause 1.24 is engaged by Mr Walker making a request for another party to execute a document or do some other thing. While he made no formal request, it was implicit from his pleading of an

affirmative defence based on the Deed that he was requesting the Whitley Parties to cease the 274 proceeding against him. The PVL companies breached cl 1.24 by continuing to pursue the proceeding against Mr Walker. This means it was an abuse of process for them to continue to pursue the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker.

[667] Mr Whitley was not a party to the Settlement Deed and so did not breach it. Mr Walker pleaded that it was nonetheless an abuse of process for Mr Whitley to pursue the 274 proceeding against him. Two essential grounds were pleaded. The first was that Mr Whitley’s conduct in pursuing the proceeding was in breach of the PVL companies’ obligations under cl 1.24 of the Deed. The second was that Mr Whitley had knowingly induced Mr Henderson’s breaches of the Deed or his abuse of process.

[668] As to the first ground, every claim that Mr Whitley makes in the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is made on behalf of the PVL companies. I therefore consider that Mr Whitley can be in no better position, so far as the question of abuse is concerned, than the PVL companies. If it is an abuse for party A to bring a claim, the abuse does not disappear by party B bringing the claim on party A’s behalf. It is therefore just as much an abuse for Mr Whitley to continue to pursue the proceeding against Mr Walker as it is for the PVL companies to do so.

[669] This means it is not necessary for me to determine, for the purposes of determining the abuse of process point, whether Mr Whitley knowingly induced Mr Henderson to breach the Settlement Deed. It is, however, a matter to which I return when considering Mr Walker’s counterclaim against the Whitley Parties

Is pursuit of the fourth cause of action in the 1160 proceeding an abuse of process?

[670] The fourth cause of action in the 1160 proceeding alleges breach of fiduciary duty by Mr Walker (and knowing receipt by SPF and LPF). This claim was not pleaded at the time of the Settlement Deed. Mr Walker says the fiduciary duty claim against him is different from the claims that were pleaded at that time and is not a “reasonable amendment” to them. He says it is therefore barred by cls 1.16 and 1.17.

[671] Mr Barker submitted that the fourth cause of action was a reasonable amendment to the existing claims because it was largely a reformulation of those claims. He said the fiduciary duty claim was largely the same as the causes of action under s 301 of the Companies Act (now, the fifth and sixth causes of action).

[672] In cls 1.16 and 1.17 Mr Henderson, Ms Buxton and entities associated with them agreed not to bring any further “claim” against Mr Walker, other than with respect to the “claims” that were then outlined in the 1160 proceeding and subject to any reasonable amendments as may be made to those claims. In the context of a proceeding, a “claim” means a demand for some remedy or relief based on a particular set of alleged facts — in other words, a cause of action. The carve out in cls 1.16 and

1.17 was therefore for reasonable amendments to the existing causes of action. The fourth cause of action was entirely new. It was not an amendment to an existing cause of action. And even if “claim” were to be interpreted more broadly as meaning “allegation”, I do not consider the allegations in the fourth cause of action to be reasonable amendments to the allegations in the two causes of action under s 301 of the Companies Act. There is some overlap between the allegations in those causes of action, but it is not significant. The alleged breaches are for the most part quite different.

[673] It follows that bringing the fourth cause of action against Mr Walker was a breach of the Settlement Deed by Mr Henderson and by the other parties to the Deed. Those parties included all the 100 Parties except Tomanavich Holdings. However, it is clear that Tomanavich Holdings knowingly — through Mr Henderson — participated in the breach. The 100 Parties’ pursuit of this cause of action is therefore an abuse of process.

Issue 11: Are the claims under s 301 of the Companies Act out of time under the Limitation Act 2010?

[674] Both sets of plaintiffs make claims against Mr Walker under s 301 of the Companies Act. Section 301 provides, relevantly:

  1. Power of court to require persons to repay money or return property

(1) If, in the course of the liquidation of a company, it appears to the court that a person who has taken part in the formation or promotion of the company, or a past or present director, manager, administrator, liquidator, or receiver of the company, has misapplied, or retained, or become liable or accountable for, money or property of the company, or been guilty of negligence, default, or breach of duty or trust in relation to the company, the court may, on the application of the liquidator or a creditor or shareholder,—

(a) inquire into the conduct of the promoter, director, manager, administrator, liquidator, or receiver; and

(b) order that person—

(i) to repay or restore the money or property or any part of it with interest at a rate the court thinks just; or

(ii) to contribute such sum to the assets of the company by way of compensation as the court thinks just; or

(c) where the application is made by a creditor, order that person to pay or transfer the money or property or any part of it with interest at a rate the court thinks just to the creditor.

...

[675] The claims by the plaintiffs under s 301 are all claims for monetary relief. They are each therefore a “money claim” in terms of the Limitation Act.90 Under s 11(1) of the Limitation Act, a money claim is generally time-barred if it is filed at least six years after the date of the act or omission on which the claim is based (the claim’s “primary period”), unless the late knowledge provisions in the Act apply.

[676] However, s 16 of the Limitation Act specifies special start dates for various money claims. Under s 16(1)(i), the start date for the primary period of a claim under s 301 is “the date on which the liquidator of the company ... was appointed”. Mr Walker says that means the date on which the first liquidator was appointed to the company. If he is correct, all the s 301 claims were filed after the six-year primary period: the first liquidators were appointed to the PVL companies at various dates up to 9 February 2012, and all claims were filed after 9 February 2018.

[677] The plaintiffs, by contrast, say that s 16(1)(i) refers to the date of appointment of the current liquidator, in this case, Mr Whitley. If they are correct, the claims were

90 Section 12(1).

filed within the primary period, as Mr Whitley was not appointed as liquidator of the companies until various dates in 2021 and 2022. In the alternative, the plaintiffs rely on the late knowledge provisions of the Limitation Act. As a further alternative, the plaintiffs rely on s 50 of the Limitation Act, under which the court has a discretion to grant relief on a time-barred claim if it is ancillary to another claim which was made within time. The plaintiffs did not place any reliance on s 49 of the Act (which deals with the conversion of trust property).

Does “the liquidator” in s 16(1)(i) mean “the first liquidator” or “the current liquidator”?

[678] Mr Walker and the plaintiffs each said that the other’s interpretation would produce absurd outcomes. The plaintiffs said Mr Walker’s interpretation would allow a liquidator to act with impunity and wait out the six-year period from their appointment to escape liability. Mr Walker said the plaintiffs’ interpretation would mean the limitation period could be continually restarted by the appointment of a new liquidator. For example, the 100 Parties first made their s 301 claims in June 2019, when Mr Walker was still liquidator. On the plaintiffs’ interpretation, those claims were time-barred when first made but were revived by the limitation period being restarted on Mr Whitley’s appointment as liquidator.

[679] The meaning of s 16(1)(i) must be ascertained from its text and in the light of its purpose and its context.91

[680] The text of s 16(1)(i) provides no assistance on this interpretative issue. It refers merely to the date of appointment of “the liquidator” of the company. This could mean either the first liquidator or the current liquidator.

[681] The purpose of the Limitation Act provides limited assistance on the issue. Section 3 says the purpose of the Act is “to encourage claimants to make claims for monetary or other relief without undue delay by providing defendants with defences to stale claims”. Mr Walker can say that the plaintiffs’ interpretation, by allowing the limitation period to be continually reset, would mean stale claims could be advanced.

91 Legislation Act 2019, s 10(1).

Mr Whitley can say that he did not act with undue delay once he was appointed liquidator. The position is different for creditors, as the 100 Parties claim to be, who make a s 301 claim. The plaintiffs’ interpretation would mean that the appointment of a new liquidator could rescue a creditor from the consequences of undue delay and strip a liquidator (or director or other defendant to a s 301 claim) of a defence to a stale claim.

[682] More assistance is obtained from the purpose of s 16(1)(i). This purpose can be ascertained by considering the way in which previous limitation statutes applied to claims under s 301 of the Companies Act (or its predecessors).

[683] Under previous limitation statutes, time started running when a cause of action accrued. Previous statutes did not have an equivalent to s 16 of the 2010 Act, which specifies special start dates for particular claims. A claim under s 301 of the Companies Act (or its predecessors) was therefore subject to the general rules found in limitation statutes.

[684] The Court of Appeal was concerned with the application of a limitation period to a claim under s 254 of the Companies Act 1908 (a predecessor to s 301 of the 1993 Act) in Re J E Hurdley and Son Ltd (in liq).92 The issue was whether time began running under s 254 from the commencement of the company’s liquidation or from the date the cause of action arose. The Court said it was the latter. Myers CJ said the reason for this was plain:93

[Section 254] does not create any new cause of action. It is merely procedural, and does no more than provide a summary method of procedure with the object of simplifying litigation and minimizing expense. What it contemplates is merely a new method of procedure for the determination of existing causes of action: it does not create new liabilities or new rights.

[685] In Re Maney and Sons De Luxe Service Station Ltd, the Court of Appeal addressed the application of the Limitation Act 1950 to a claim under s 320 of the Companies Act 1955.94 Section 320 does not have a direct equivalent in the

92 Re J E Hurdley and Son Ltd (in liq) [1941] NZGazLawRp 45; [1941] NZLR 686 (CA).

93 At 723.

94 Re Maney and Sons De Luxe Service Station Ltd [1969] NZLR 116 (CA).

Companies Act 1993.95 However, in its analysis of s 320 the Court also addressed s 321 of the 1955 Act, which is the immediate predecessor to s 301. The Court held that s 320 created a new cause of action of which the liquidation of the company was a material part. This meant that time did not begin to run for limitation purposes until commencement of the liquidation. The Court contrasted that with the position under s 321, which they saw, as the Court had earlier done in Hurdley, as a procedural section that did not create any new cause of action.96

[686] In Arataki Properties Ltd v Craig, the liquidator brought a claim under s 321 against a company’s directors.97 One of the directors pleaded that the claim was time-barred. The liquidator argued that the cause of action accrued at the date the liquidation commenced rather than at the date on which the company could first have issued proceedings in respect of the alleged beach and invited the Court of Appeal not to follow Hurdley.

[687] The Court of Appeal declined the invitation. Cooke P delivered the leading judgment, with which McMullin, Somers and Casey JJ agreed. Cooke P acknowledged that it would be easier for liquidators to carry out their investigations of the affairs of failed companies if a cause of action under s 321 were treated as accruing at the date of liquidation rather than the date of breach.98 But he said there was a “strong current” of authority behind Hurdley, and he was not persuaded to depart from it.99 He added:100

The settled interpretation that the section provides a new way of examining into and enforcing an existing liability to the company should not be disturbed by judicial decision. Perhaps there are arguments of policy for extending the time scope of the section; but to say the least they are not overwhelming. If there is to be any change it is best left to the legislature.

[688] The legislature did make a change by enacting s 16(1)(i) of the Limitation Act 2010. Clearly, the purpose of the provision was to address a perceived

95 Sections 135 and 136 of the Companies Act 1993 cover some of the ground previously covered by s 320 of the Companies Act 1955 but are in different language and are not dependent on a company being in liquidation.

96 At 125 per North P, 130–131 per Turner J and 134 per Haslam J.

97 Arataki Properties Ltd v Craig [1986] 2 NZLR 294 (CA).

98 At 296.

99 At 298.

100 At 298.

problem with the consequences of Hurdley, Maney and Arataki. Beyond that, the legislative history of the Act provides no assistance. There was no discussion of special start dates in the first two Law Commission Reports leading to the 2010 Act.101 The potential for inclusion of special start dates for some claims was raised in the third Law Commission Report, but not in respect of s 301 claims.102 The provision that became s 16(1)(i) made its first appearance as cl 15(1)(h) of the Limitation Bill 2009.103 The explanatory note to the Bill said:104

Clause 15 specifies special start dates for various money claims. The special start date for a claim under section 301 of the Companies Act 1993 (which, in a liquidation, empowers the court to require persons to repay money or return property), for example, is the date on which the liquidator of the company or overseas company was appointed. When time should begin to run for claims under corresponding earlier provisions in the Companies Act 1955 was discussed in Re Maney and Sons De Luxe Service Station Limited, Maney v Cowan [1969] NZLR 116 (CA).

[689] In the absence of any specific guidance in the legislative history, the purpose of s 16(1)(i) is best understood by considering the contest in each of Hurdley, Maney and Arataki. The contest was between time running from the date on which the underlying cause of action arose (which, in the case of a claim against directors or others involved in managing the company, will predate liquidation) and time running from the date of liquidation. The perceived problem that s 16(1)(i) remedies is that, as a consequence of the Court of Appeal opting for the former over the latter, in practice a liquidator would have less than six years (sometimes much less) from the date of liquidation to bring a claim under s 301 against a delinquent director or other manager. The purpose of s 16(1)(i), therefore, is to remedy the problems faced by liquidators in having (in)sufficient time to bring s 301 claims against directors or others whose acts or omissions predated liquidation.

[690] That this is the purpose of s 16(1)(i) is revealed by the drafter’s apparent lack of thought as to how the provision would work where the s 301 claim is against, rather than by, a liquidator. Such a claim will always be in respect of an act or omission that

  1. Law Commission Limitation Defences in Civil Proceedings (NZLC R6, 1988); and Law Commission Tidying the Limitation Act (NZLC R61, 2000).

102 Law Commission Limitation Defences in Civil Cases: Update Report for the Law Commission

(NZLC MP16, 2007) at [64] and following.

103 Limitation Bill 2009 (33-1).

104 Limitation Bill 2009 (33-1) (explanatory note) at 8.
