post-dates liquidation. If an aggrieved creditor or shareholder wishes to bring a s 301 claim alleging that the current liquidator has misapplied company property, the effect of s 16(1)(i) is that time will have started to run on the date on which the liquidator was appointed, even though the liquidator’s alleged misapplication will have occurred after appointment. So, although the evident purpose of s 16(1)(i) is to delay the start date of the limitation period, in most claims against a liquidator the effect of the provision will be to accelerate the start date.

[691] Given that the purpose of the provision is to remedy the problems faced by liquidators in having (in)sufficient time to bring s 301 claims against directors or others whose acts or omissions predated liquidation, the present interpretative issue

  • — which arises only where there have been successive appointments of liquidators — should be resolved by reference to that purpose. Mr Walker’s interpretation better matches that purpose. If time starts to run on the date on which the first liquidator is appointed, there are six years from that date for that liquidator and any successor liquidator to bring a s 301 claim against the delinquent director. On the plaintiffs’ interpretation, the limitation period would be restarted on the appointment of a successor liquidator, and there would be no limit on how many times it could be restarted by further appointments of liquidators. The Limitation Act’s 15-year longstop period then could not apply, as it applies only if a claim is made after its primary period.105 I can see no good reason for the Limitation Act operating in that way.

[692] I therefore consider that in s 16(1)(i) the date on which “the liquidator” was appointed means the date on which “the first liquidator” was appointed. The effect of this interpretation is that time starts to run for a s 301 claim on the date of the company’s liquidation. I appreciate that the legislature could easily have drafted s 16(1)(i) to say this expressly but did not. I also acknowledge Mr Moss’s point that there is a contrast with s 16(1)(j), which says the start date for certain claims in respect of a corporation or bank subject to statutory management is “the date on which the corporation or bank was declared to be subject to statutory management”. But those points are answered by the fact that s 241(5) of the Companies Act says that the

105 Limitation Act 2010, s 11(2)(b).

liquidation of a company “commences on the date on which ... the liquidator is appointed”. The date on which the liquidator is appointed is therefore the same as — and is understood as a proxy for — the date of commencement of the liquidation. The position is different under the respective statutes allowing for the statutory management of corporations or banks. Neither contains an equivalent to s 241(5). Both provide that the statutory management commences on the date and time specified in the Order in Council declaring that a corporation or bank is subject to statutory management.106

[693] Finally, this interpretation does not produce the absurd outcome raised by the plaintiffs. They said that the interpretation would allow a liquidator to act with impunity and wait out the six-year period from their appointment to escape liability. This overlooks that s 301 is merely a procedural provision. It does not create any new cause of action. If a liquidator breaches their duties to the company, the company will have a cause of action against the liquidator. A replacement liquidator can enforce that cause of action by bringing an ordinary proceeding in the name of the company. The replacement liquidator is not limited to bringing a claim under s 301. The limitation period for the ordinary proceeding will, under s 11(1), start on the date of the act or omission on which the claim is based. It will not be subject to the special start date in s 16(1)(i).

[694] I have considered whether the claims that Mr Whitley has brought under s 301 could be characterised as ordinary proceedings by the PVL companies. I see no basis on which that could be done on the pleadings. Indeed, in his closing submissions Mr Moss stated that “it is Mr Whitley bringing these causes of action rather than all the Whitley plaintiffs”.

[695] The consequence of this interpretation is that all the plaintiffs’ s 301 claims are time-barred, unless the plaintiffs can avail themselves of the late knowledge or ancillary claims provisions of the Limitation Act.

  1. Corporations (Investigation and Management) Act 1989, s 38(3); and Banking (Prudential Supervision) Act 1989, s 117(4).

Can the plaintiffs avail themselves of the late knowledge provisions of the Limitation Act?

[696] Under s 11(2) of the Limitation Act, if a claimant has late knowledge of the claim and the claim is made after its primary period, the applicable limitation period is three years after the late knowledge date (the claim’s “late knowledge period”).107 The “late knowledge date” is defined in s 14. It is the date on which the claimant gained knowledge (or, if earlier, ought reasonably to have gained knowledge) of all the facts on which the claim is based.

[697] The 100 Parties plead that the relevant facts on which their s 301 claims are based were not known to them until after September 2017, when they were advised of the disbursement of funds under the Distribution Agreement. Their s 301 claims plead that they suffered loss as a result of the distributions made under that Agreement. Plainly, they did not know of that loss until the distributions were made. Their s 301 claims were brought within three years of September 2017 and so are within the late knowledge period.

[698] As for the Whitley Parties, Mr Moss submitted that Mr Whitley was only appointed replacement liquidator of the PVL companies between 22 September 2021 and 25 February 2022 and that he could not have had knowledge of the relevant acts or omissions before those dates. He said the s 301 claims were brought within three years of those dates.

[699] I accept both points. However, Mr Moss’s submission assumes that it is Mr Whitley’s knowledge that counts for the purposes of the late knowledge provisions. The Limitation Act defines “claimant” as “the person on whose behalf a claim is made (whether by that person or another person)”.108 A liquidator who makes a s 301 claim does so on behalf of the company in liquidation. It is the company that has the cause of action and, as cases such as Arataki make clear, s 301 is merely procedural. That the liquidator is making the claim on the company’s behalf is reinforced by s 301(1)(b), the effect of which is that any orders made on a successful claim by a liquidator will be for the benefit of the company.

107 Section 11(3).

108 Section 4 definition of “claimant”.

[700] What matters, therefore, is when the PVL companies knew of the relevant acts and omissions. Several people who acted in the liquidation of the companies — primarily Mr Walker but also Mr Eathorne and, in the case of PVL, Mr Scutter — knew of the relevant facts by late 2017. If the knowledge of any of them is attributed to the PVL companies, the Whitley Parties cannot avail themselves of the late knowledge provisions. It is only if their knowledge is not attributed to the companies that the late knowledge provisions are available.

[701] Mr Cooper said the companies were fixed with knowledge of all relevant events as they occurred, but he did not elaborate. Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker’s knowledge could not be attributed to Mr Whitley, but he did not address whether Mr Walker’s knowledge (or Mr Scutter’s or Mr Eathorne’s) could be attributed to the PVL companies.

[702] In support of his submission, Mr Moss relied on PMT 20210 Ltd (in liq) v Mark.109 In PMT a liquidator brought a claim under s 348 of the Property Law Act 2007 against the company’s directors to set aside a disposition of property. The claim was brought outside the primary period in the Limitation Act. The liquidator therefore relied on the late knowledge provisions of the Act. The defendants contended that the liquidator ought reasonably to have known the relevant facts early in the liquidation, some time before he gained actual knowledge. If the defendants were right, the claim was out of time. Grice J held that the liquidator could not reasonably have gained the relevant knowledge at the time the defendants contended.110 The claim was therefore brought within the late knowledge period. PMT is not relevant to the attribution issue in this case, as it was not contended that the claimant was the company (rather than the liquidator) and so there was no consideration of whether the directors’ knowledge should be attributed to the company.

[703] Whether the knowledge of an alleged wrongdoer should be attributed to a company for the purposes of the late knowledge provisions of the Limitation Act is a

  1. PMT 20210 Ltd (in liq) v Mark [2022] NZHC 169. I note there is an error in the intituling of the judgment. The first plaintiff’s registered name was PMT 2010 Ltd.

110 At [73].

difficult question. It is not necessary for me to answer it. Even if Mr Walker’s and Mr Scutter’s knowledge were not attributed to the PVL companies, Mr Eathorne’s would be. It follows that the Whitley Parties cannot avail themselves of the late knowledge provisions.

Should relief be granted on the s 301 claims as ancillary claims?

[704] Section 50 of the Limitation Act confers on the court a discretion to grant relief on a time-barred claim if it is ancillary to another claim which was made within time. The discretion may be exercised “if [the court] thinks it just to do so on an application made to it for [that] purpose”.111

[705] The 100 Parties made, in their reply to Mr Walker’s defence, an application for relief under s 50. It is not necessary to consider that application, given that their s 301 claims are within the late knowledge period.

[706] The Whitley Parties did not make any application for relief under s 50. They did not raise it in opening. Their reliance on s 50 appeared for the first time in closing submissions. As a consequence, Mr Walker did not address in his closing the issues that would have been raised had an application been made or had reliance on s 50 at least been signalled in opening. In these circumstances, I decline to grant relief to the Whitley Parties under s 50.

100 Parties’ first cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): conversion

[707] The 100 Parties’ first cause of action is in conversion. It depends on them showing that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries. They have failed to do so, and for that reason this cause of action fails.

[708] Had the 100 Parties shown that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, I would have found the net amount to which they would have been entitled from the settlement proceeds (after Mr Walker exercised his right of recoupment) to be $229,840.

111 Section 50(3).

[709] Given that the 100 Parties failed to show that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, it is not necessary for me to determine whether a chose in action (the 100 Parties’ right to a portion of the settlement proceeds) can be converted.

100 Parties’ second cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): money had and received

[710] This cause of action fails for the same reason as the 100 Parties’ first cause of action. It is not necessary for me to determine whether the various other elements of the claim in money had and received could be established against the defendants.

100 Parties’ third cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): tracing

[711] Although the 100 Parties did not formally abandon the third cause of action, in closing Mr Barker said that “[a]s the proceeds themselves appear no longer to be identifiable, it is not possible to maintain a claim for tracing”. In any event, given that the 100 Parties failed to show that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, their third cause of action fails.

100 Parties’ fourth cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): breach of fiduciary duty and knowing receipt

[712] The fourth cause of action is for breach of fiduciary duty (by Mr Walker) and knowing receipt (by SPF and LPF). This cause of action fails because:

(a) the 100 Parties have failed to show that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries;

(b) Mr Walker did not breach any fiduciary duties; and

(c) in respect of Mr Walker, pursuit of this cause of action is an abuse of process.

[713] It is not necessary for me to determine whether LPF received money paid in alleged breach of fiduciary duty or whether LPF and SPF had the requisite knowledge to incur liability in knowing receipt.

100 Parties’ fifth cause of action (against Mr Walker): breach of duty as liquidator

[714] The 100 Parties’ fifth cause of action is brought under s 301 of the Companies Act in their alleged capacity as unsecured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries. Mr Barker submitted that the 100 Parties have standing to make a s 301 application for the unsecured component of the debts allegedly owed by the PVL subsidiaries.

[715] I have found that none of the 100 Parties have proved that they are secured creditors of any PVL subsidiary. In making that finding, I found that the 100 Parties had not proved that the debts arising from the various loan agreements entered into by the PVL subsidiaries were assigned to the 100 Parties. It follows that they have not proved that any debts are owed by the PVL subsidiaries to them.

[716] This cause of action therefore fails.

100 Parties’ sixth cause of action (against Mr Walker): breach of duty as liquidator

[717] The 100 Parties’ sixth cause of action is brought under s 301 of the Companies Act in their alleged capacity as unsecured creditors of PVL.

[718] The 100 Parties plead that they were unsecured creditors of PVL pursuant to guarantees provided by PVL in respect of the loan liabilities of the PVL subsidiaries. Those loan liabilities were the liabilities (or debts) under the various loan agreements entered into by the PVL subsidiaries, which the 100 Parties pleaded had been assigned to them. The 100 Parties have not proved that any of those loan agreements were assigned to them. It follows that even if the benefit of some guarantees was assigned to the 100 Parties, there were no underlying liabilities owed to them which were caught by those guarantees.

[719] It follows that the 100 Parties have not proved that they are unsecured creditors of PVL. Their sixth cause of action fails.

Whitley Parties’ first cause of action (against Mr Walker): voidable preference recoveries

[720] The Whitley Parties’ first cause of action is distinct from all the other causes of action in this proceeding in that it has little to do with Mr Walker’s entry into the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement. It focusses on voidable preference payments that Mr Walker recovered as liquidator.

[721] The Whitley Parties plead that Mr Walker had, as liquidator, recovered a total of $705,520 in the form of voidable preference payments that had been made to creditors of PVL or PVL subsidiaries. In closing, Mr Moss confined the claim to a total of $650,468. The Whitley Parties claimed that Mr Walker was under a duty to hold these recoveries on trust for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of the PVL company to which the recoveries belonged, or to apply the recoveries in a way which could benefit those unsecured creditors. They alleged that Mr Walker breached that duty by applying all the voidable preference recoveries to the costs of the PVL proceedings.

[722] There was some debate before me about whether, as the Whitley Parties pleaded, a liquidator holds voidable preference recoveries on trust for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of the company in liquidation.112 I agree with Mr Moss that I do not have to resolve that debate. This is for two reasons. First, the authorities in this area are almost all concerned with the question whether a secured creditor’s security extends to the voidable preference recoveries. In (usually) answering that question in the negative, courts have sometimes said that the recoveries are held on trust for the unsecured creditors.113 Here, the 100 Parties did not claim that their putative securities extend to the voidable preference recoveries.

[723] Secondly, in closing the Whitley Parties advanced their case on this cause of action on a narrow basis. Mr Moss said that whether or not the recoveries were held on trust, a liquidator could use the recoveries to fund a proceeding only if there was at least a chance that the unsecured creditors of the company would benefit from that proceeding. He submitted that Mr Walker’s use of the recoveries to fund the PVL

112 See Heath and Whale, above n 23, at [24.72].

113 See, for example, Re Yagerphone Ltd [1935] Ch 392.

proceedings was a breach of duty because Mr Walker never expected the unsecured creditors of the PVL companies to receive any benefit from the proceedings.

[724] When dealing with issue 2, I rejected the contention that Mr Walker never thought there would be a return to unsecured creditors from the PVL proceedings. I held that he merely thought a return was unlikely. The factual basis upon which the Whitley Parties advanced this cause of action therefore fails.

[725] This cause of action fails also for other reasons. The Whitley Parties’ continued pursuit of it is an abuse of process (issue 10). To the extent that an order was sought under s 301 of the Companies Act, the claim was filed out of time (issue 11).

[726] I note three other matters for completeness. First, Mr Walker’s evidence was that the voidable preference recoveries were used not only to pay for the PVL proceedings but also for other general costs of the liquidations, including other unsuccessful claims to recover voidable preference payments. This aspect of Mr Walker’s evidence was not seriously challenged.

[727] Secondly, in this cause of action the Whitley Parties also sought orders under s 284(1)(a) of the Companies Act that Mr Walker repay the amount of the voidable preference recoveries to the PVL companies to which they belonged. In contrast to other causes of action, here the Whitley Parties were not attempting to use s 284(1)(a) to paper over cracks in their substantive claim against Mr Walker. They were contending that Mr Walker had acted in breach of duty, not merely that it was “reasonable” to make an order under s 284(1)(a) (issue 9). Had I found that this cause of action succeeded, an order under s 284(1)(a) would have been available.

[728] Thirdly, in addition to pleading that the s 301 claim was out of time, Mr Walker pleaded that the entire cause of action was out of time because it was filed more than six years after the voidable preference recoveries were allegedly spent by Mr Walker on the PVL proceedings. The Whitley Parties responded by relying on the late knowledge provisions of the Limitation Act. I have not found it necessary to determine this affirmative defence.

Whitley Parties’ second cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): declarations relating to Services Deed and Distribution Agreement

[729] The Whitley Parties’ second cause of action makes a miscellany of complaints about the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement. It is not well pleaded and is difficult to unpack. I will address it by reference to the relief sought.

A declaration or direction that the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement are not binding on PVL and the PVL subsidiaries and an inquiry into what amount should be repaid by LPF and SPF

[730] The Whitley Parties contend that the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement are not binding on PVL and the PVL subsidiaries either because court approval was required, court approval can be retrospectively refused, or the agreements were illegal. They seek a declaration or direction accordingly, either under s 284(1)(a) of the Companies Act, the court’s inherent jurisdiction or s 73 of the Contract and Commercial Law Act.

[731] I have found that Mr Walker was not under a duty to obtain court approval for the Services Deed (issue 2) or the Distribution Agreement (issue 4). Because court approval was not required, there is no basis on which approval could now be “retrospectively refused”. I have also found that the Deed (as amended by the Agreement) was not unlawful as an abuse of process (issue 5). The Whitley Parties did not advance any other basis for finding the Deed or the Agreement to be unlawful or illegal. They cannot use s 284(1)(a) to make up for any substantive shortcomings in their claim (issue 9). This part of their cause of action therefore fails.

Alternatively, a declaration or direction under s 284(1)(a) of the Companies Act or the Court’s inherent jurisdiction: (a) that the Services Deed was not binding on the PVL subsidiaries; and/or (b) that certain payments were unreasonable expenses and have to be repaid by SPF and LPF

[732] In their final amended statement of claim the Whitley Parties deleted their pleading that the Services Deed was not binding on the PVL subsidiaries, but their prayer for relief still sought a declaration or direction to that effect. I expect that was in error. In any case, their claim for that declaration or direction fails, as I have found that the PVL subsidiaries were bound by the terms of the Deed (issue 3).

[733] The Whitley Parties also sought a declaration or direction that certain payments were unreasonable expenses in PVL’s liquidation because they were not valid Project Costs under the Services Deed. The payments were the costs of purchasing the Allied and DFG debts and securities, the payment of security for costs and various miscellaneous expenses. I have rejected the Whitley Parties’ claims that these were not valid or reasonable Project Costs (issue 8). This claim therefore also fails.

Additionally or alternatively, a declaration or direction under s 284(1)(a) of the Companies Act or the Court’s inherent jurisdiction: (a) that the Distribution Agreement was not binding on PVL or the PVL subsidiaries; and/or (b) that certain payments were unreasonable expenses and have to be repaid by SPF and LPF

[734] The Whitley Parties’ claim for a declaration or direction that the Distribution Agreement is not binding on PVL or the PVL subsidiaries is based on their allegation that in entering into the Agreement, Mr Walker breached his duty to act in the best interests of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries and without any conflict. I have rejected those allegations of breach of duty (issue 4). This part of their claim therefore fails.

[735] Their claim for a declaration or direction that certain payments were unreasonable expenses focusses on a subset of the disputed payments: security for costs and the various miscellaneous expenses. I have rejected their claims that these were not valid and reasonable Project Costs (issue 8). This claim therefore fails.

Alternatively, an order under s 284(1)(b) of the Companies Act reversing Mr Walker’s decision to enter into the Distribution Agreement

[736] The pleaded basis on which the Whitley Parties seek an order under s 284(1)(b) of the Companies Act reversing Mr Walker’s decision to enter into the Distribution Agreement is that Mr Walker breached various duties. I have rejected all the claims of breach of duty (issue 4).

[737] In closing, Mr Moss submitted that the decision to enter into the Distribution Agreement without first obtaining directions was unreasonable and one that no reasonable liquidator would have made. In so doing, Mr Moss relied on his earlier submission that Mr Walker was, in the circumstances, under a duty to seek court approval before entering into the Agreement. I have rejected that submission (issue 4).

Further, it will be clear from my analysis of the Agreement (including the compromise that it reached) that I reject any claim that Mr Walker acted unreasonably in entering into it.

[738] This part of the Whitley Parties’ second cause of action therefore fails.

[739] The entirety of this cause of action has failed. I briefly turn to consider the affirmative defences that were raised.

Affirmative defences: Mr Walker

[740] Mr Walker said this cause of action, to the extent it is against him, is an abuse of process. I agree (issue 10). It fails also for this reason.

[741] Mr Walker raised a limitation defence. The Whitley Parties responded by relying on the late knowledge provisions of the Limitation Act. Given the failure of the cause of action and Mr Walker’s success on the abuse of process defence, I have not found it necessary to determine this affirmative defence.

Affirmative defences: SPF and LPF

[742] SPF and LPF plead that cl 4.1 of the Distribution Agreement is a bar to proceedings against SPF by the Whitley Parties and that any claims against LPF are parasitic on the claims against SPF, meaning the claims against LPF are an abuse of process. Clause 4.1 provides:

SPF, the Liquidators and the Plaintiffs agree that ... this agreement is in full and final settlement of all claims that SPF (on the one hand) and the Liquidators and the Plaintiffs (on the other hand) may have against each other in connection with the matters dealt with in this agreement and each of them irrevocably and unconditionally waives all such claims.

[743] SPF and the PVL companies were parties to the Distribution Agreement. On the face of it, cl 4.1 is a clear bar to the claims brought by the PVL companies against SPF. Mr Moss’s only argument to the contrary was that the Agreement had been entered into in breach of Mr Walker’s duties. I have rejected that argument (issue 4). Clause 4.1 therefore is a bar to the claims of the PVL companies against SPF.

[744] Mr Whitley was not a party to the Distribution Agreement. However, his claims in the second cause of action are all brought on behalf of and for the benefit of the PVL companies. I consider it is an abuse of process for Mr Whitley to advance against SPF claims that the PVL companies have previously settled.

[745] LPF was not a party to the Distribution Agreement. Mr Salmon submitted that the claims against LPF were parasitic on the claims against SPF and were therefore an abuse of process. I accept that the claims against LPF are based on the same acts and events as the (settled) claims against SPF. But this does not mean it is an abuse of process for the claims to be advanced against LPF. Clause 4.1 settled claims against SPF. It could have, but did not, settle claims against LPF.

[746] SPF and LPF also plead a set-off arising from the indemnity contained in cl 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement:

The Liquidators and the Plaintiffs jointly and severally indemnify SPF, its directors, employees, advisors and related companies (“Indemnified Persons”) and shall hold the Indemnified Persons harmless from and in respect of any loss, damage, cost, expense (including on a solicitor and own client basis), proceeding, claim or any liability whatsoever suffered or incurred by the Indemnified Persons (or any of them) in connection with:

(a) a breach by the Liquidators or the Plaintiffs of any term of this agreement; and

(b) a claim by any person against the Indemnified Persons (or any of them) in connection with this agreement, the Allied Debts or the [DFG] Debts.

[747] LPF and SPF are related companies. LPF is therefore an Indemnified Person. It can enforce the indemnity provided by cl 4.3 as cl 4.4 provides that for the purposes of the (then) Contracts (Privity) Act 1982, cl 4.3 is intended to be for the benefit of and enforceable by each Indemnified Person.

[748] If LPF or SPF had any liability under the second cause of action, that liability would have been covered by the indemnity in cl 4.3. That is because the liability would have been “in connection with” a claim by “any person” (the Whitley Parties) “in connection with” the Distribution Agreement, the Allied debts and securities and the DFG debts and securities. Mr Moss’s only submission in response was that the

Agreement, and therefore the indemnity, was unlawful. I have rejected that submission (issue 5).

[749] SPF and LPF would, therefore, each have had a set-off arising from the cl 4.3 indemnity that would have provided a defence to any liability under this cause of action.

[750] SPF and LPF also raise a limitation defence to the second cause of action. Given the failure of the cause of action and the success of the other affirmative defences, I have not found it necessary to determine the limitation defence.

Whitley Parties’ third cause of action (against SPF): breach of contract

[751] In their third cause of action, the Whitley Parties allege three breaches of contract by SPF.

[752] First, they allege that SPF breached the Allied security agreement. I have found that there was no such agreement (issue 1). This part of the claim fails.

[753] Secondly, they allege that SPF breached the Services Deed by wrongly claiming as Project Costs the costs of purchasing the Allied debts securities and the DFG debts and securities, the provision of security for costs and miscellaneous costs. I have found those were valid and reasonable Project Costs (issue 8). This part of the claim fails.

[754] Thirdly, the Whitley Parties claim that SPF breached the Distribution Agreement by wrongly claiming as Project Costs the provision of security for costs and miscellaneous costs. Given that these were valid and reasonable Project Costs (issue 8), this part of the claim fails.

[755] The entirety of this cause of action therefore fails. In addition, for the reasons given when addressing the Whitley Parties’ second cause of action, SPF succeeds in its affirmative defences arising from cls 4.1 and 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement.

Whitley Parties’ fourth cause of action (against Mr Walker): breach of fiduciary and statutory duties as liquidator

[756] In their fourth cause of action, the Whitley Parties allege that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary and statutory duties as liquidator in a wide range of ways. Most of the allegations are concerned with Mr Walker entering into the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement and with the payments made under the Deed and Agreement. I have rejected those allegations (issues 2, 4 and 8).

[757] There are two related allegations that I have not yet addressed. These are that Mr Walker favoured PVL over the PVL subsidiaries and failed to apportion the benefit of recoveries obtained in the PVL proceedings among both PVL and the PVL subsidiaries.

[758] Mr Moss did not address those allegations in closing. However, they were not formally abandoned.

[759] If it had been necessary to do so, I would have found that the PVL subsidiaries were entitled to a share of the settlement proceeds from the PVL proceedings (issue 7). It does not follow, however, that Mr Walker failed to apportion those proceeds between PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. An apportionment would only be necessary if there were net proceeds to apportion. Here, there were no net proceeds: while there was initially a surplus of $2 million from the settlements (after the payments to SPF and the $4.08 million payment to Mr Walker), this surplus was spent on further liquidation costs. The Whitley Parties have failed in their challenges to the amounts paid to SPF, and their challenge to Mr Walker’s remuneration is for another proceeding.

[760] I therefore reject all the alleged breaches underlying this cause of action. Part of the relief sought by the Whitley Parties is an order under s 284(1)(a) that Mr Walker is in breach. They cannot use s 284(1)(a) to make up for any shortcomings in their claim (issue 9).

[761] This cause of action therefore fails. In addition, pursuit of this cause of action is an abuse of process (issue 10) and, to the extent that Mr Whitley seeks an order

under s 301 of the Companies Act, the claim is time-barred (issue 11). Mr Walker also pleads that the entire cause of action was out of time, to which the Whitley Parties reply by relying on the late knowledge provisions of the Limitation Act. I have not found it necessary to determine that wider limitation defence.

Whitley Parties’ fifth cause of action (against SPF and LPF): knowing receipt

[762] The Whitley Parties’ fifth cause of action is against SPF and LPF for knowing receipt. It springs off the breaches of fiduciary duty by Mr Walker alleged in the fourth cause of action. Given that I have rejected those allegations of breach of fiduciary duty (issues 2 and 4), this cause of action fails. It is not necessary for me to address the other elements of the cause of action.

[763] In addition, for the reasons given when addressing the Whitley Parties’ second cause of action, SPF succeeds in its affirmative defences arising from cls 4.1 and 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement, and LPF succeeds in its affirmative defence arising from cl 4.3. It is not necessary for me to address their limitation defences.

Whitley Parties’ eighth cause of action (against SPF and LPF): undue influence

[764] I have rejected the allegation that SPF and LPF unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement (issue 4). The Whitley Parties’ eighth cause of action therefore fails.

[765] In addition, for reasons already given, SPF succeeds in its affirmative defences arising from cls 4.1 and 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement, and LPF succeeds in its affirmative defence arising from cl 4.3. It is not necessary for me to address their limitation defences.

Mr Walker’s counterclaim against the 100 Parties

[766] Mr Walker’s counterclaim against the 100 Parties is based on alleged breaches of the Settlement Deed. Mr Walker alleges that Mr Whitley is associated with Mr Henderson. He then alleges two bases of liability. The first is that Mr Henderson and the 100 Parties (other than Tomanavich Holdings, which was not a party to the Deed) breached the Deed either by aiding, abetting, procuring and funding Mr Whitley

to bring the 274 proceeding or by acting jointly in concert with Mr Whitley in the conduct of that proceeding. The second is that all the 100 Parties knowingly induced and participated in those breaches of the Deed.

[767] I have found that Mr Whitley is associated with Mr Henderson and that the pursuit of the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is a breach of cls 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18 of the Settlement Deed by Mr Henderson and the 100 Parties (other than Tomanavich Holdings) (issue 10). The counterclaim therefore succeeds against the 100 Parties (other than Tomanavich Holdings) on the first basis of liability.

[768] As for Tomanavich Holdings, the issue is whether it is liable for knowingly inducing and participating in the breaches of the Settlement Deed. I received only the barest of submissions on that issue. I consider that Tomanavich Holdings participated in the breaches by acting in concert with the Whitley Parties in the pursuit of the two sets of proceedings. Mr Henderson was the driving force behind all four of the 100 Parties in this litigation. His knowledge of the relevant terms of the Deed is attributed to Tomanavich Holdings. Tomanavich Holdings therefore knowingly participated in the breaches.

[769] Mr Walker’s counterclaim against all the 100 Parties therefore succeeds. Mr Walker sought two forms of relief. The first was damages in the amount of any liability he might have to the 100 Parties under their fourth cause of action or to the Whitley Parties. He has no such liability, so that can be put to one side.

[770] The second was an inquiry into the damages suffered by Mr Walker as a consequence of the breaches. However, no attempt was made at trial to establish that Mr Walker had suffered any loss from the breaches. As Muir J said recently, the jurisdiction under r 16.2 of the High Court Rules to order an inquiry is not to be used “as a springboard for some forensic inquiry into whether damage has occurred and its extent”.114 I therefore decline to order an inquiry.

[771] I appreciate that Mr Walker will have incurred legal costs in defending the 274 proceeding. That, however, is a matter which can be dealt with by costs orders.

114 Cooper v Cooper & Co Real Estate Albany Ltd [2025] NZHC 478 at [301].

Mr Walker’s counterclaim against the Whitley Parties

[772] Mr Walker’s counterclaim against the Whitley Parties is also based on alleged breaches of the Settlement Deed.

[773] I have found that the PVL companies (that is, the Whitley Parties other than Mr Whitley) breached cl 1.24 of the Settlement Deed by continuing to pursue the proceeding against Mr Walker (issue 10). The counterclaim succeeds against those companies.

[774] The remaining issue concerns Mr Whitley’s position. Mr Walker pleads that Mr Henderson and the 100 Parties breached the Settlement Deed by aiding and funding Mr Whitley to bring and pursue the 274 proceeding. Mr Walker then alleges that Mr Whitley, by pursuing the 274 proceeding in concert with Mr Henderson, knowingly induced those breaches of the Deed.

[775] Mr Henderson’s assistance to and funding of Mr Whitley was clearly a breach of the Settlement Deed. The issues are whether Mr Whitley knowingly induced that breach and, if so, whether he was justified in doing so.

Did Mr Whitley knowingly induce a breach of the Settlement Deed?

[776] Mr Moss said that Mr Whitley did not know about the Settlement Deed until May 2023, about 16 months after he had filed the 274 proceeding. Mr Moss submitted that Mr Whitley therefore cannot have induced a breach of the Deed.

[777] I accept that Mr Whitley did not know about the Settlement Deed when he commenced the 274 proceeding. But once he learned of it in May 2023, and particularly once Mr Walker relied on the Deed as an affirmative defence, Mr Whitley knowingly induced Mr Henderson to breach the Deed by continuing to accept Mr Henderson’s assistance in, and funding of, the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker.

Is the defence of justification available to Mr Whitley?

[778] Mr Whitley’s knowing inducement of Mr Henderson’s breach of the Settlement Deed is a tort, unless Mr Whitley can establish the defence of justification.115 The onus is on Mr Whitley to establish the defence.

[779] Mr Moss submitted that the authors of Todd on Torts have recognised, relying on Canadian authorities, that a defence of justification could arise from “the legal duty of a company director to protect the company”.116 Mr Moss said that position was also supported by the authors of Clerk and Lindsell on Torts, who referred to the same Canadian cases.117 He submitted that a liquidator is in a similar position to a company director. Mr Whitley’s duty to the PVL companies and the unsecured creditors of those companies, Mr Moss submitted, was superior to any obligation he may have had to prevent breach of the Settlement Deed. Mr Whitley was therefore justified in bringing and pursuing the 274 proceeding.

[780] It is not necessary for me to decide whether a defence of justification is readily available to directors (and therefore liquidators) in the way the Canadian cases might suggest. This is because Mr Moss’s submission wrongly assumes that the putative tort that needs to be justified is Mr Whitley’s pursuit of the 274 proceeding. It may be that Mr Whitley was justified in pursuing that proceeding. But the allegation that he faces is not merely that he pursued the proceeding but that he did so in concert with Mr Henderson by continuing to accept his assistance and funding. Mr Whitley has not offered any justification for that.

[781] This is not some arid pleading point. Once Mr Whitley learned of the Settlement Deed, he could have continued the 274 proceeding yet avoided liability for inducing breach of contract by refusing further assistance or funding from Mr Henderson. He chose instead to continue in concert with Mr Henderson.

  1. The elements of the tort of inducement of breach of contract were not in dispute. See generally Stephen Todd and others Todd on Torts (9th ed, Thomson Reuters, 2023, Wellington) at [12.2].

116 At [12.2.6], citing 369413 Alberta Ltd v Pocklington 2000 ABCA 307, (2000) 271 AR 280, and

Brae Centre Ltd v 1044807 Alberta Ltd 2008 ABCA 397, (2008) 446 AR 10.

  1. Andrew Tettenborn (ed) Clerk & Lindsell on Torts (24th ed, Sweet & Maxwell, 2023, London) at [23-62].

Outcome

[782] Mr Walker’s counterclaim against all the Whitley Parties succeeds. He sought the same relief against them as he sought in his counterclaim against the 100 Parties. I decline to grant any relief, for the reasons I gave when dealing with that counterclaim.

LPF’s counterclaim against the Whitley Parties

[783] LPF brings a counterclaim against the Whitley Parties under the indemnity in cl 4.3 of the Distribution Agreement. Relevantly, LPF says it is entitled to be indemnified for its costs and expenses (on a solicitor and own client basis) in connection with the claims made in these two proceedings.

[784] When dealing with LPF’s affirmative defences, I held that LPF is entitled to enforce the indemnity in cl 4.3. The indemnity includes protection against costs and expenses (on a solicitor and own client basis) incurred by LPF in connection with any claim against LPF in connection with the Distribution Agreement or the Allied or DFG debts and securities. The claims against LPF in the two proceedings are all in connection with the Agreement or the Allied or DFG debts and securities. LPF is therefore entitled to be indemnified for its costs and expenses, on a solicitor and own client basis, in connection with the claims made against it in the two proceedings.

[785] However, the indemnity in cl 4.3 was given by Mr Walker and Mr Scutter (as liquidators of the PVL companies) and by the PVL companies themselves. Mr Whitley was not a party to the Distribution Agreement. LPF pleaded that Mr Whitley was bound by the Agreement as successor liquidator. This was not explained in LPF’s closing submissions. I see no basis on which Mr Whitley could be bound to the Agreement.

[786] LPF therefore succeeds on its counterclaim against the Whitley Parties other than Mr Whitley. Against those parties, LPF is entitled to damages in the amount of the costs and expenses (on a solicitor and own client basis) it incurred in connection with the claims made in these two proceedings.

[787] LPF is also awarded interest on that amount under s 10 of the Interest on Money Claims Act 2016. Interest is awarded for the periods beginning on the dates on which the various costs and expenses were paid by or on behalf of LPF and ending on the date on which the judgment debt is paid in full.

SPF’s counterclaim against the Whitley Parties

[788] SPF’s counterclaim mirrors LPF’s. My analysis is the same. SPF is entitled to damages from the Whitley Parties (other than Mr Whitley) in the amount of the costs and expenses (on a solicitor and own client basis) it incurred in connection with the claims made in these two proceedings.

[789] SPF is also awarded interest on that amount under s 10 of the Interest on Money Claims Act. Interest is awarded for the periods beginning on the dates on which the various costs and expenses were paid by or on behalf of SPF and ending on the date on which the judgment debt is paid in full.

Costs

[790] The defendants are the successful parties. They are entitled to costs from the plaintiffs.

[791] I anticipate that the parties and counsel will work constructively to agree the amount of costs. If agreement is not reached, submissions should be filed and served as follows:

(a) Defendants by 19 December 2025. No more than 20 pages in total. The defendants are to determine how they share that page allocation.

(b) Plaintiffs by 30 January 2026. No more than 20 pages in total. The plaintiffs are to determine how they share that page allocation.

(c) Defendants strictly in reply by 13 February 2026. No more than eight pages in total. The defendants are to determine how they share that page allocation.

[792] I will then determine any costs issues on the papers.

Result

[793] There is judgment for the defendants on all the plaintiffs’ claims.

[794] There is judgment for Mr Walker on his counterclaims against the 100 Parties and the Whitley Parties but I decline to order any relief on those counterclaims.

[795] There is judgment for LPF on its counterclaim against the Whitley Parties, other than Mr Whitley. Against those parties, LPF is entitled to damages in the amount of the costs and expenses (on a solicitor and own client basis) it incurred in connection with the claims made in these two proceedings. LPF is also awarded interest on that amount under s 10 of the Interest on Money Claims Act. Interest is awarded for the periods beginning on the dates on which the various costs and expenses were paid by or on behalf of LPF and ending on the date on which the judgment debt is paid in full.

[796] There is judgment for SPF on its counterclaim against the Whitley Parties, other than Mr Whitley. Against those parties, SPF is entitled to damages in the amount of the costs and expenses (on a solicitor and own client basis) it incurred in connection with the claims made in these two proceedings. SPF is also awarded interest on that amount under s 10 of the Interest on Money Claims Act. Interest is awarded for the periods beginning on the dates on which the various costs and expenses were paid by or on behalf of SPF and ending on the date on which the judgment debt is paid in full.

Campbell J
